the containment check's own reason to exist - a symlink planted inside the media cache (`newFixture`'s `f.mediaDir`) whose target is a real file outside every allowed root, confirming `OpenFile` follows it with `filepath.EvalSymlinks` before judging it rather than trusting the link's own, safely-nested path. `tests/unit/openurl-coverage.test.cjs`'s existing guard - no QML file calls `Qt.openUrlExternally` directly outside an explicit allow-list - was tightened to allow no file at all, now that every open, local or remote, goes through the helper instead.

## The WhatsApp download size cap never actually ran

A security review found that `backend/internal/connector/whatsapp/download_file.go`'s `boundedFile` embedded `*os.File` rather than holding it as a named field. Embedding promoted the file's own `ReadFrom` method onto `boundedFile`, and whatsmeow's real download loop copies with `io.Copy(file, io.TeeReader(resp.Body, hasher))`: a `TeeReader` has no `WriteTo`, so `io.Copy` looked at the destination for `ReadFrom` instead of calling `Write` in a loop, found the promoted one, and used it - writing every byte straight to the file and skipping `boundedFile.Write` entirely, cap and idle-timer reset both. whatsmeow puts no size limit of its own on the response, so a sender who left a message's `FileLength` at 0 (skipping the declared-size check in `fetch.go`/`fetch_stream.go`) could push an unbounded download through a fetch the UI makes automatically, the moment a chat opens - no click needed, and large enough to pressure RAM or fill the home filesystem. The package's own tests never caught this: the hand-written fake called `file.Write` directly, which real whatsmeow never does.

**The fix.** `boundedFile` now holds the real `*os.File` as a named `file` field and implements exactly the methods `whatsmeow.File` needs - `Read`, `Seek`, `ReadAt`, `WriteAt`, `Truncate`, `Stat` - each forwarding straight to `file`, alongside the existing capped `Write`. With no embedding, `boundedFile` has no `ReadFrom` of its own, so `io.Copy` falls back to calling `Write` repeatedly, which is what actually enforces `max`. `WriteAt` forwards without re-checking the cap: whatsmeow only ever uses it to decrypt bytes already written and counted in place, never to grow the file past what `Write` already bounded. The declared-size checks before the download starts (`fetchImage`, `fetchFile`) are unchanged and still refuse outright when a sender's own `FileLength` already exceeds the limit; this fix is what makes the streaming backstop behind them - the one that catches an undersold or absent declared size - actually run.

**The fake now matches real whatsmeow.** `fetch_helpers_test.go`'s `downloadMediaToFile` copies its scripted data with `io.Copy(file, io.TeeReader(bytes.NewReader(data), io.Discard))` instead of calling `file.Write` directly, reproducing the exact call shape that bypassed the cap. Without this, a test fake calling `Write` itself would never exercise the promoted-`ReadFrom` path at all, whichever way `boundedFile` was built - this is why the original bug shipped with passing tests. Confirmed against the old `boundedFile`: with this fake change alone (before the field rename), `TestFetchMedia_CapsAFileStreamLargerThanDeclared` and `TestFetchMedia_CapsAnImageStreamWithNoDeclaredSize` failed, oversized data sailing straight through; both pass again once `boundedFile` stopped embedding.

**Telegram's own streamed download was already safe.** gotd's `downloader.Builder.Stream` takes a statically-typed `io.Writer` and its internal `writeLoop` calls `w.Write(part.data)` directly - never `io.Copy`, so there is no `ReadFrom`/`WriteTo` promotion to bypass in the first place. `connector.BoundedWriter`, which Telegram's `download` wraps the destination file in, is a plain struct that embeds nothing, so even a future call path that did use `io.Copy` would find no promoted method to exploit. No change was needed there; `fetch_limits_test.go`'s `TestFetchMedia_CapsADocumentStreamLargerThanDeclared` already drives the real gotd downloader end to end, not a hand-rolled stand-in, so it would have caught the same class of bug had one existed.

## Extra `0xFF` fill bytes before a JPEG marker bypassed the scan and table caps

A follow-up security review found that `imagesafe/jpeg.go`'s `copyJPEGRemainder` - added to bound a progressive JPEG's own repeated scans, Huffman and quantization tables (see the entry above it) - classified whatever byte sat right after the *first* `0xFF` it found as the marker code. The JPEG spec allows any number of `0xFF` fill bytes to precede a real marker, and libjpeg-turbo's own `next_marker` skips all of them before reading the marker code; this package's own walk did not, so `FF FF DA` was read as marker `0xFF` (not a marker this package recognises) followed by a fresh search starting at `DA`, and the start-of-scan the fill byte was hiding was never counted at all. The same hole let a repeated DHT or DQT segment back past `jpegMaxRepeatedTableSegments`. This was the exact attack the scan cap was meant to close, just with one fill byte added back in front of each repeat.

**The fix.** `copyJPEGRemainder` now skips a run of `0xFF` fill bytes the same way `next_marker` does, after writing the first one through but before classifying the byte that follows as a marker: whatever comes after the run is what gets compared against `0xD9`/`0xDA`/`0xC4`/`0xDB` and counted, regardless of how many fill bytes sat in front of it. The run itself is collapsed in the output down to that single leading `0xFF` - fill bytes carry no meaning of their own, so a decoder reads the collapsed form identically, and a long run can no longer be used to push a marker out of where a naive scan would expect to find it. A new `jpegMaxFillBytes` (64 KiB, counted across the whole walk, not per run) bounds the total skipped even when no single run or marker count on its own would trip `jpegMaxScans` or `jpegMaxRepeatedTableSegments` - the same "bound a count no real encoder would ever produce" reasoning the other two caps already use, since a real encoder has no reason to pad with anything beyond a handful of bytes.

**Tests.** `insertFakeMarkersWithFill` (`helpers_test.go`) builds the exact bypass shape - `extraFill` additional `0xFF` bytes before a chosen marker, repeated `count` times - reusing `insertFakeMarkers`' own trick of needing only the two marker bytes, not a fully-formed segment, to drive the counts. `TestSanitizeJPEG_CountsMarkersPastFillBytes` reproduces the report's bypass for SOS, DHT and DQT with one extra fill byte each, repeated two million times, and confirms rejection well under a second - run against the pre-fix `copyJPEGRemainder`, this failed exactly as the review described, bytes sailing through uncounted. `TestSanitizeJPEG_CollapsesOrdinaryFillBytes` confirms a small, ordinary fill run is still accepted, with the run actually collapsed in the output rather than just tolerated. `TestSanitizeJPEG_CapsTotalFillBytes` covers a single fill run past `jpegMaxFillBytes` with too few markers to trip the other two caps on its own. `FuzzSanitizeJPEG` gained seeds at this exact shape, past `jpegMaxScans` and `jpegMaxRepeatedTableSegments` with fill bytes added, and past `jpegMaxFillBytes` alone.

## A saved file's own name could still carry markup characters

A regression review found that `app/mediasave.go`'s `sanitizedStem` stripped control and bidi/format characters from a sender's claimed file name, but still kept `<`, `>`, `&`, `=`, `#`, `;` and other punctuation outside any allow-list. Qt's own non-native `FileDialog` (`options: FileDialog.DontUseNativeDialog`, used by `Composer.qml`'s attach picker and `ListController.qml`'s notification-sound picker whenever the portal's own chooser is unavailable) lists each file through `FileDialogDelegateLabel.qml`'s plain `Text { text: root.delegate.fileName }`, with no `textFormat` set - every Qt Quick Controls style defaults that to `AutoText`, which renders a name containing `<img src=...>` as rich text and fetches whatever it points at just by listing the folder. A document named `<img src=https:&#47;&#47;evil.example&#47;t.png>.pdf` - Qt's own HTML parser decodes the `&#47;` entities even though a real file name can never contain a literal `/` - would leak the user's IP address and a timing signal to the sender the moment they browsed into Downloads for an unrelated reason, with no click on the message itself.

**The fix.** `sanitizedStem` now runs a conservative allow-list, `restrictStemChars`, after collapsing whitespace: letters, digits, space and a fixed set of punctuation (`- _ . ( ) , +`) pass through unchanged, and everything else - markup characters included - becomes `_`. This runs in the one place every saved name already passes through, so it covers every sender and every connector without touching `ui/` at all.

**The fallback dialogs' own starting folder was not changed.** The regression review's general shape of a fix also suggested the fallback dialogs stop relying on Qt's own rendering of a foreign file name altogether - for instance, by never letting them start browsing inside Downloads. Neither `Composer.qml`'s attach `FileDialog` nor `ListController.qml`'s sound `FileDialog` sets a `currentFolder` today, so neither already points at Downloads specifically; a user who wants to attach or pick a sound they just received would have to navigate there themselves either way. Once a saved name can no longer carry a character `AutoText` reads as markup, there is nothing left in Downloads for either dialog's label to misread, so steering their starting folder away from it would add complexity (a `StandardPaths` lookup, a decision about where to point instead) without closing a gap the character fix had not already closed. Left as is unless a future review finds another reason to change it.

**Tests.** `TestSaveMedia_NeverWritesMarkupCharactersInTheFileName` (`mediasave_test.go`) covers the report's own scenario, a sender-claimed name built around `<img src=x>` alongside `&`, `#` and `;`, and asserts none of those six characters ever reach the saved file's own name - confirmed to fail against the pre-fix `sanitizedStem` before `restrictStemChars` was added.

## A second quadratic trailing-character regex, in `Links.js`'s own copy, plus two more of the same shape found by sweeping the rest of `ui/lib`

A security review found `Links.js`'s `normalizeTarget` trimmed a link's own trailing slash with `/\/+$/` - a quantified character class anchored only at the end, not the start. With no start anchor, a regex engine retries the match from every position in the string, and at each one inside a long run of "/" it backtracks the `+` from its greedy maximum back down to one before giving up - costing the square of the run's length, not its length. `sameTarget` calls this twice on every link open (the click path and the "o" key, each normalizing both the link's own visible text and its href), so `https://a` followed by about 65,000 "/" and then "b" cost roughly 2×10⁹ backtracking steps per call - the exact shape `Format.splitTrailingPunctuation` and `Highlight`'s own copy of it (since folded into a call to `Format.plainLinks` - see "The `o` key opened more of a link than its own bubble ever showed" below) were already rewritten as backward scans to close, in `ui/lib`'s other two link-handling files, when this same review previously found it there; this copy, added later in `Links.js`, was missed.

**The fix.** `normalizeTarget` now calls `Links.trimTrailingSlashes`, a backward scan from the string's own end - linear by construction, since there is no quantifier ambiguity left for anything to backtrack.

**The sweep.** Fixing a regex found this way by inspection alone risks missing a sibling copy, so every regex literal and `RegExp` construction in `ui/lib`, `ui/components` and `ui/controllers` was listed and checked for the same shape: an unanchored or globally-reapplied quantifier whose character class can run to the end of the input with nothing to stop it early. Most are either anchored at both ends (so only one start position is ever tried), or already excluded their own terminator from an adjoining character class AND have a terminator that is guaranteed nearby - `Format.URL_PATTERN`'s HTML-entity suffix, for instance, where `[^\s<"&]*` can only stop at a `&` that is itself followed by one of a handful of fixed-length entities, each closed with its own `;` within a few characters. Two more did not have that guarantee once fed adversarial input directly, confirmed by writing the same timed, 65,000-character adversarial case for every regex-bearing exported function in the sweep (`tests/unit/redos-bounds.test.cjs`) rather than trusting the reasoning alone:

- `Format.highlight`'s own `.split(/(&[^;]*;)/)`, meant to keep an HTML entity opaque while highlighting a search match around it, excludes `;` from its class the same correct way, but a run of `&` with no `;` anywhere after it - not reachable through `highlight`'s one real caller today, which always pre-escapes its text through `escapeHtml`, but reachable by calling the function directly - backtracks that class to zero at every `&` in the run, the identical shape. Replaced with `Format.splitEntities`, a forward scan that finds each entity's own next `;` directly rather than letting the regex engine search for it by trial and error.
- `Format.initials`'s own `.replace(/\([^)]*\)/g, ' ')`, stripping a bracketed aside such as "(Manager)" from a display name before picking initials, has the same shape: a run of `(` with no `)` anywhere after it. A display name is capped server-side at `domain.MaxDisplayNameLength` (256) today, so the practical cost through `Avatar.qml`'s one real caller is trivial, but the function itself had no bound of its own. Replaced with `Format.stripAsides`, the same kind of forward scan.

Both replacements preserve the exact split or substitution the regex produced, including the "no terminator found at all" case: the original regex simply fails to match from that point on, leaving the remainder of the text untouched, which each new loop does explicitly by copying the rest through unchanged rather than continuing to scan for a terminator that cannot exist.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/links.test.cjs` gained the same adversarial case `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` and `tests/unit/highlight.test.cjs` already carry for their own trailing-punctuation fix, now for `sameTarget`'s trailing-slash one: a 65,000-character run, asserting both the real result and a two-second bound. `tests/unit/redos-bounds.test.cjs` is the sweep's own record, independent of any single lib file: one bounded, adversarial case per regex-bearing exported function the sweep covered, each fed 65,000 of its own regex's boundary character, so a future change that reintroduces this shape - in one of these functions or a new one added the same way - fails the build on its own rather than waiting for another review to notice by inspection.

## A real GIF video's AnimatedImage still sized its decode from the sender's claimed width/height, not the file's own verified canvas

A security review found that `InlineVideo.qml`'s and `FullVideoPlayer.qml`'s own real-GIF `AnimatedImage` - the one case where a video's full, downloaded file (never a thumbnail) decodes through an image engine at all, once `decodeAs` confirms it is a genuine GIF - called `ImageBounds.boundedSourceSize` with `root.video.width`/`root.video.height`: the sender's own claimed video dimensions, read from the message's metadata, with nothing bounding them server-side and nothing checking them against the real file. Every other sink `boundedSourceSize` ever shrinks a decode for was already fixed to use a verified size instead ("`ImageBounds.boundedSourceSize`'s own fix still read a sender's claimed width and height, not the real file's", above); a line this file's own earlier comment added - "A video's full file is never shown through a plain Image at all - only AnimatedImage, measured not to amplify a mismatch this way" - never actually measured that claim, and this is the counterexample: `boundedSourceSize`'s own math is correct relative to the *box*, but a box of, say, 1400x1 (what a 2000000x1 claim scales down to inside a 1400x860 bubble) still makes Qt's own covering scale read the *real* file's wildly different ratio (a 128x2048 GIF, say) and amplify past it - exactly the decode-bomb shape every other fix in this section closes, just reached through a video's claimed size rather than a photo's.

**Why nothing caught this earlier.** `servedPath` (`app.Commands`) only runs `connector.EnsureImageSafe` for a photo or sticker, so `media.fetch` never returned a verified size for a video at all; `connector.SanitizeIfGIF` sanitized a real GIF video's bytes through `imagesafe.AnimatedGIF` but discarded the width and height that sanitizer already parsed. There was nothing for `ImageBounds.verifiedDimensions` to read for a video even if the UI had called it.

**The fix, three layers.**
- `connector.SanitizeIfGIF` now returns the GIF's own real width and height alongside `isGIF`, from the same `imagesafe.Sanitize` call it already makes.
- `app.Commands.DecodeAsForMedia` returns them too, and `server.mediaFetch` (`methods.go`) uses them in place of `FetchMedia`'s own (always zero, for a video) width/height whenever `decodeAs` comes back `"gif"` - the same additive `width`/`height` fields `media.fetch`'s result already carries for a photo or sticker, now also set for this one video case (`docs/api.md`).
- `MediaController._applyVerifiedSize` needed no change at all: it already records any non-zero `result.width`/`result.height` as `verifiedWidth`/`verifiedHeight` on the message's own media, whatever kind that media is. `InlineVideo.qml`'s and `FullVideoPlayer.qml`'s real-GIF `AnimatedImage` now read `ImageBounds.verifiedDimensions(video, true)` the same way `PhotoView`/`PhotoViewer`/`StickerView` already do, falling back to the box - never the claim - before that answer exists.

**Defence in depth: `domain.CapMedia` now bounds `Media.Width`/`Height` too**, at a new `MaxMediaDimension` (65536px), the same belt-and-suspenders reasoning `ImageBounds.boundedSourceSize` itself already gets even once every real sink reads a verified size: cheap insurance for any sourceSize calculation that ever again reads this claim directly, in this file or a new one, without ever becoming the actual fix - the real fix is the UI never reading the claim for sizing a decode at all.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/connector/media_test.go` and `backend/internal/app/media_decode_test.go` cover `SanitizeIfGIF`'s and `DecodeAsForMedia`'s new width/height return values, the app-level one with a message carrying a 2000000x1 claim to confirm the real, decoded 1x1 canvas is what comes back, never the claim. `backend/internal/server/server_test.go`'s `TestMediaFetch_ReportsVerifiedDimensionsForARealGIFVideo` covers the same thing through `media.fetch` end to end. `backend/internal/domain/limits_test.go` covers `CapMedia`'s new width/height clamp. `tests/unit/image-bounds.test.cjs` covers `verifiedDimensions`/`boundedSourceSize` together producing the box fallback before a verified size exists and the real, small size once it does, for the exact 2000000x1-claim shape. `tests/qml/InlineVideo/shell.qml` and `tests/qml/PhotoViewer/shell.qml` each gained a case recomputing `boundedSourceSize` from a verified 4x3 over whatever box the component's own (lying-aspect-ratio-driven) layout settled on, and requiring the component's own `AnimatedImage.sourceSize` to match it exactly - the same pattern `PhotoView`'s own lying-dimensions case already used for a photo.

## `Media.ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight` were only ever computed by a read, so a live, backfilled or edited message reached the UI without them

A security review found that `store.sanitizedThumb` - the function that turns a thumbnail's own bytes into `ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight` - ran only inside `scanMessage`'s own `decodeMessageColumns`, which fires when a row is read back out of the database with `Message`, `MessageByRemote` or `Messages`. Nothing called it on the write side: `addMessage` returned the connector's own message (`m`, built straight from its argument) with no read in between, and `EditMessage` built its return value from `edit.Media` the same way. `app.Ingest.Incoming`, `.History` and `.Edited` all publish that returned value directly as `message.added` or `message.updated`, with no extra read of the row. So a message delivered live, backfilled from history, or edited to attach new media reached the UI with `ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight` still zero, and `ImageBounds.verifiedDimensions` then fell back to the display box alone for `sourceSize` - the same decode-bomb shape the box-alone fallback was already shown unsafe against elsewhere in this file, now reachable again through any of these three paths, including re-arming an older photo already loaded with its correct dimensions by editing it.

**The fix.** `store.verifyThumb(media *domain.Media) *domain.Media` wraps `sanitizedThumb` for a `*domain.Media` whole, returning a copy with `Thumb`, `ThumbWidth` and `ThumbHeight` all freshly computed, or `media` unchanged when it is nil or carries no thumbnail. `addMessage` calls it on `m.Media` right after assigning defaults, before the row is ever inserted; `fillMedia` (a message stored before the service reported media for it, filled in later) calls it on its own `media` argument before storing; `EditMessage` calls it on `edit.Media` before storing and before building its return value. The message every one of these three functions returns - and so what `Ingest` publishes - now carries exactly the same `ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight` a subsequent read of the same row would compute, because both paths now call the one function. This is not a new dependency: `store` already imports `imagesafe` for `sanitizedThumb` itself.

**Why in `store`, not `app` or the connectors.** The connectors already run a thumbnail through `imagesafe.Sanitize` once (WhatsApp's `thumb`, Telegram's `strippedThumb`), but both deliberately discard the width and height that call returns, reasoning (now outdated) that `store.sanitizedThumb` would recompute them on every read regardless. Threading the connectors' own already-verified width and height through `mediaRef`/`domain.Media` all the way to `store` would mean two separate, divergent paths for the exact same number; calling `verifyThumb` in `store` instead keeps the one place `ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight` are ever computed exactly one place, for a write the same way it already was for a read, at the cost of one extra, cheap re-parse of a thumbnail already capped to `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s own small size.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/store/thumbsafe_test.go` gained `TestAddMessage_ReturnsVerifiedThumbDimensions` and `TestEditMessage_ReturnsVerifiedThumbDimensions`, each asserting the dimensions on the function's own return value, with no intervening read. `backend/internal/app/ingest_test.go` gained `TestIncoming_PublishesVerifiedThumbDimensions`, `TestHistory_PublishesVerifiedThumbDimensions` and `TestEdited_PublishesVerifiedThumbDimensions`, each reading the data `fakePublisher` recorded for the matching event and confirming it already carries the thumbnail's real 8x8 size over a sender's claimed 60000x1, all four failing against the pre-fix code for the expected reason (zero width and height) before `verifyThumb` existed.

## The fallback file-picker dialogs are gone outright, not just the saved name's own markup characters

A regression review of "A saved file's own name could still carry markup characters" (above) found that fix - `restrictStemChars`, an allow-list applied the moment a file is saved - covered only names this helper writes from now on. It left three things still reachable: a file already saved under an earlier release, before that allow-list existed; any other file a markup-named attachment could end up as on disk by a route that never goes through `mediasave.go` at all, such as an archive a sender sent and the user extracted themselves; and the fallback dialogs' own delegate, which the character fix never touched. The review judged the character fix real but incomplete, since "there is nothing left in Downloads for either dialog's label to misread" (the earlier entry's own words) assumed every markup-named file in Downloads came from this helper's own `media.save`, which is not true.

**The fix this time: remove both fallback `FileDialog`s outright, rather than keep narrowing what can reach their label.** `Composer.qml`'s `fileDialogLoader` (the attach button's fallback) and `ListController.qml`'s `soundFileDialogLoader` (the notification-sound picker's) are deleted along with the state that gated them (`_fileDialogActive`, `_soundFileDialogActive`) and the functions that set it (`Composer.openFallbackPicker`, the equivalent branch in `ListController._setNotificationSound`). `ComposerController.pickAttachFile` and `ListController._setNotificationSound` now do exactly what `WindowController.openExternalLink` already does for a failed `links.open`: an `"unavailable"` reason from `files.pick` sets the open conversation's (or the list's own) `lastError` to a fixed notice, "Couldn't open the file picker: no desktop portal", rather than asking anything to open a dialog of this UI's own. `ComposerController.filePickerUnavailable` (the signal that used to ask `Composer` to fall back) is deleted with it, since nothing needs to cross that boundary any more. There is now exactly one file picker this UI ever shows - the portal's - matching how a missing portal is already handled everywhere else `docs/api.md` documents an `"unavailable"` reason.

**Why remove rather than keep patching the label.** The markup-character fix closes the gap for one producer (this helper's own saves) of one kind of file (one this helper itself named). Every other way a file can end up in a folder the dialog might list - copied in by hand, extracted from an archive, synced from another device, saved by an older release - stays open forever unless something stops relying on Qt's own `AutoText` rendering of an arbitrary file name at all. The dialog's own content is otherwise unremarkable: both fallbacks exist only because the portal can fail to show its own chooser, a rare, already-handled condition every other picker in this UI (`links.open`, `files.open`) already turns into a plain refusal rather than a second, weaker path. Keeping that one case exceptional - falling back to code with its own rendering surface, instead of just saying so - was the thing to fix, not the label inside it.

**`Composer.fileAttached` and `ListController.notificationSoundPicked` stay.** Both are still real entry points a successful portal pick calls into (`ComposerController.attachFile`, `ListController.notificationSoundPicked` itself), and `tests/qml/Flows` and `tests/qml/ComposerController`/`tests/qml/ListController` call them directly to simulate a pick with no real dialog to drive - the same seam ui-testing.md already uses for this. Removing the fallback dialogs did not touch this signal or function, only what used to call `Composer.fileAttached` from inside the composer itself.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs` moves `FileDialog` and `FolderDialog` out of `KNOWN_CONTAINER_TYPES` - which only ever asked that a container be gated behind a `Loader`, an allowance this review's whole point is to withdraw for these two - into a new, separate test, `'FileDialog and FolderDialog are never declared anywhere in the UI, gated or not'`, that fails the build on any appearance of either, gated or not, so the fallback cannot creep back in one component at a time. `tests/qml/Composer/shell.qml`'s `checkFileDialogLifetime` (which drove the now-deleted Loader's own open/reject cycle) and `tests/qml/ListController/shell.qml`'s `checkSoundFileDialogLifetime` are deleted; `tests/qml/ListController/shell.qml`'s `soundList` no longer needs the `FloatingWindow` it only needed to host that dialog. `tests/qml/ListController/shell.qml` gained `checkFilePickUnavailable` and `tests/qml/ComposerController/shell.qml`'s `checkPickAttachFile` gained a case, both asserting the fixed notice lands in `lastError` instead.

## A mention's own Name had no length cap, unlike every other display name

A differential security review found that `domain.CapMentions` bounded how many mentions a message's `Mentions` list could carry (`MaxMentions`) and dropped any mention whose range no longer fit the truncated text, but never looked at the length of a kept mention's own `Name` - unlike `SenderName`, which `SanitizeIncoming` already caps directly with `CapDisplayName`. A mention's `Name` comes from the service (for Telegram, `userName(user)` in `connector/telegram/mentions.go`), with no length limit of its own at the connector, and a single message can mention the same user up to `MaxMentions` (64) times. A hostile or compromised service could send one user with a multi-megabyte name, mentioned 64 times in one message, copying that name 64 times into `server/methods.go`'s result and `ui/lib/Timeline.js`'s in-memory message list (kept for up to 2,000 rows) for one message on the wire. An ordinary Telegram account can't reach this - Telegram's own servers cap a name at 64+64 characters - so this only matters because the threat model treats the services themselves as potential attackers.

**The fix.** `CapMentions` now runs `CapDisplayName` over every kept mention's `Name`, the same `MaxDisplayNameLength` cap every other sender-controlled display name gets. This lives in `CapMentions` itself, the one place `SanitizeIncoming` already calls for every mention, so a row an older release stored before this cap existed is repaired on every read, the same way `CapDisplayName` already repairs a legacy `SenderName` or `Title`.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/domain/limits_test.go`'s `TestCapMentions_CapsEachMentionsName` covers a mention Name past `MaxDisplayNameLength`, confirmed to fail against the pre-fix `CapMentions`.

## Cover art in audio and video reached Qt's own image decoders unchecked

A differential security review found that `audiosafe` and `videosafe` validated an audio or video file's streams, codecs and dimensions, but never looked at whether the file also carried an embedded picture: an MP3's ID3v2 tag, an Opus stream's comment header, or an MP4/M4A's iTunes-style metadata atom. Qt's FFmpeg plugin (`libffmpegmediaplugin.so`) pulls out any attached picture a playing file carries and decodes it with `QImage::fromData` - the same call `imagesafe` exists to guard everywhere else a sender's image bytes reach this shell, but one this file's own metadata never passed through at all. The machine's own Qt image plugins include a full PDF renderer and decoders for formats imagesafe never allow-lists, and Qt's own default allocation limit for that decode (about 256 MB) is far above imagesafe's own caps. A contact sending an ordinary-looking voice note or song with a crafted cover image could reach this the moment the message is played, with no click on the image itself.

**Which formats actually needed a fix.** Both packages' own `Validate` accepts exactly three container shapes between them: MP3 (audiosafe), Ogg/Opus (audiosafe), and MP4/M4A/MOV (both, audio-only in audiosafe and video in videosafe). Every other format this review's brief named was already excluded on inspection, confirmed by tracing each package's own container detection rather than assumed:

- **Matroska/WebM** (videosafe): `matroskaPlayable` returns true only when it has seen at least one *allowed* video track, and no WebM video codec is allowed at all (see the `allowedISOVideoCodec`/matroska entry above) - an audio-only WebM is refused for exactly the same reason a video one is, since `sawValidVideo` can never become true either way. No audio WebM, with or without an attachment, has ever been reported playable; audiosafe does not recognize Matroska's own EBML header at all and falls through to its MP3 sync check, which also fails on the very first byte.
- **FLAC**, bare or inside Ogg: audiosafe's `Validate` only recognizes `OggS`, an ISO BMFF box type, or an MP3 frame sync; a bare FLAC file's own `fLaC` magic matches none of those, and an Ogg FLAC stream's first packet opens with `\x7FFLAC`, never `OpusHead`, so `validateOgg` already refuses it before any comment header would even matter.

That left the three shapes the fix below actually closes.

**MP3: ID3v2 frames are now walked, not skipped.** `mp3StartOffset` used to trust an ID3v2 tag's own declared size to jump straight past it, never reading a single frame inside. `id3v2.go`'s new `id3v2Scan` walks every frame in the tag instead, refusing the whole file outright the moment it finds an `APIC` or `GEOB` frame (ID3v2.3/2.4) or their three-letter ID3v2.2 equivalents `PIC`/`GEO` - every shape of attached picture or embedded object FFmpeg's own id3v2 demuxer reads out of a tag - or anything it cannot fully account for: an extended header this package does not parse, a frame id with a character no real one ever uses, or a declared frame size that runs past the tag's own end. An all-zero frame id (a tag's own padding after its last real frame) ends the walk successfully rather than being misread as a malformed frame. `GEOB`/`GEO` are refused the same way `APIC`/`PIC` are even though this review could not confirm from source whether FFmpeg's id3v2 demuxer exposes a `GEOB`'s payload as anything on this machine: a legitimate voice note or song never needs one, so refusing it costs nothing, and assuming otherwise risked leaving the exact shape of gap this fix exists to close.

**Ogg/Opus: the comment header packet is now read, not skipped.** `validateOgg` confirmed the first page's own `OpusHead` magic and tracked granule positions for duration, but the second packet - the comment header ("OpusTags"), which every real Opus stream carries right after the ID header and which can carry a `METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` (a base64-encoded FLAC `PICTURE` block) or the older `COVERART` comment field - was never looked at, its bytes skipped by the same page-walking arithmetic that skips actual audio data. `ogg.go`'s new `opusCommentHeaderSafe` reassembles that packet (across as many page continuations as `maxOpusCommentPacketLen`, 1 MiB, allows - generous for any real vendor string and a handful of short tags, refusing outright rather than reading further for anything larger, which a real comment header never needs to be anyway), confirms it opens with the `OpusTags` magic, and reads only each comment's own declared length and the bytes needed to compare its field name - never a comment's actual value - against `METADATA_BLOCK_PICTURE` and `COVERART`, case-insensitively per RFC 7845. A packet that cannot be reassembled or parsed in full, from a different serial, a malformed length, or simply too large, refuses the file the same way a forbidden tag does.

**MP4/M4A/MOV: the iTunes-style cover art atom is now checked.** Neither package's box walker ever looked outside `moov`'s own `trak` children, where cover art does not live at all: iTunes-style metadata stores it at `moov > udta > meta > ilst > covr`, a sibling of every track, not a track of its own. Both `isobmff.go`s (audiosafe's audio-only one and videosafe's) gained an identical `hasCoverArtAtom`, walking exactly that path (skipping `meta`'s own 4-byte FullBox version/flags before its children) and refusing the file outright the moment a `covr` box is found there; any step of the path missing - no `udta`, no `meta`, no `ilst`, no `covr` - means nothing for this check to object to, the ordinary case for a file with no cover art. A video track carrying an image codec (rather than a metadata atom) was already refused before this fix: audiosafe rejects any `vide` track outright, and videosafe's own `allowedISOVideoCodec` already excludes every image codec, so that half of the brief needed no change.

**Tests.** Each fix has its own fixture builder and table test: `backend/internal/audiosafe/id3v2_test.go` (`TestValidate_MP3RefusesID3v2AttachedPicture`, covering every frame id and tag version; `TestValidate_MP3AllowsOrdinaryID3v2Frames`; `TestValidate_MP3RefusesID3v2EdgeCases` for the extended-header, bad-id and size-overrun refusals) plus `FuzzValidateMP3ID3v2`; `backend/internal/audiosafe/ogg_test.go`'s `TestValidate_OggRefusesEmbeddedPicture` (both tag names, both cases), `TestValidate_OggAllowsOrdinaryComments` (including a field that merely contains "cover" as a substring, confirming the comparison is against the whole field name, not a loose match), and `TestValidate_OggRefusesUnparseableCommentHeader`, with new `FuzzValidateOgg` seeds; `backend/internal/audiosafe/isobmff_test.go` and `backend/internal/videosafe/isobmff_test.go` each gained an "otherwise ordinary ... with an iTunes-style cover art atom" case and a matching fuzz seed. Every existing fixture representing a real-world file without art - `oggOpusFixture`'s default now always builds the comment-header page a real Opus stream actually carries, rather than the single-packet shape the tests previously simplified down to - still validates as playable; none of the new checks' own true-case tests needed a changed expectation. Confirmed against real files: `ffmpeg`-encoded MP3, M4A and Opus files, with and without embedded cover art, built and checked against `audiosafe.Validate` directly and deleted immediately after (never committed) - every "plain" file validated playable and every "withart" file was refused, for all three formats.

## A WhatsApp edit or revoke was applied without checking who sent it

A security review found that `whatsapp/live.go`'s `handleEdit` and `handleRevoke` applied WhatsApp's own `MESSAGE_EDIT` and `REVOKE` protocol messages unconditionally: neither the connector, `app.Ingest.Edited`/`Deleted` nor `store.EditMessage` ever compared the edit or revoke's own sender against the sender already recorded for the message it named. WhatsApp's end-to-end encryption authenticates who sent a stanza, but not what it claims to act on - the edit or revoke travels inside the sender's own encrypted payload, so the server cannot check this either, and whatsmeow (`types/events/events.go`) only unwraps it. Anyone in a chat - a direct contact, or any member of a group - could therefore rewrite or delete any other message in it, including one the user sent, just by sending a protocol message naming its id. Official WhatsApp clients make this check on the receiving side; this connector did not.

**The fix.** `whatsapp/authorize.go`'s `authorizedActor` checks an edit or revoke's own sender (`event.Info`) against whoever `message_keys` (`keys.go`) recorded as the target message's sender when it first arrived: the same record `DeleteMessages`'s own `errCannotRevoke` already relies on for this account's *outgoing* revokes. A phone-number and hidden-id (LID) form of the same person are treated as equal through `personID`'s own normalisation, the same one `reactorKey` already uses to avoid double-counting a reaction under either address form. `fromMe` is compared on both sides directly, rather than through `personID`, so an edit or revoke this account makes from one of its own other linked devices still works, and a message_keys miss - an id this connector never saved a key for - is never authorized, since there is nothing recorded to check the claim against. `handleEdit` and `handleRevoke` (`live.go`) now call this before reporting anything, and drop (logging only the safe class, `"forged edit"`/`"forged revoke"`, through the existing `logDropped`) when it refuses.

**Admin deletes in a group are intentionally not supported.** WhatsApp lets a group admin delete another member's message for everyone, but nothing in the wire format distinguishes that from an ordinary member's revoke: `waCommon.MessageKey`'s `Participant` field just names whichever message the revoke claims to act on, and any sender can set it to anyone's id, so it carries no authority of its own. The only way to check real admin status is a live `groupParticipants` fetch against WhatsApp's own server, which `authorizedActor` deliberately does not make: it runs on every edit and revoke this account receives, live, and a network round trip there would let a flood of fake revokes drive a round trip per message, and would still only be as fresh as whatsmeow's own group-metadata cache. This matches the policy this connector's own *outgoing* `DeleteMessages` already documented before this fix (`delete.go`'s `errCannotRevoke`: "an admin revoking someone else's in a group is out of scope here"); `authorizedActor` keeps incoming revokes consistent with that rather than introducing a second, looser rule only for the receiving side. A real admin delete from WhatsApp's own apps is dropped here exactly as a forged one is: accepted as a known limitation rather than a bug, revisited only if a future review finds a reliable, forgery-proof signal for it.

**The Telegram connector needed no equivalent fix.** Telegram's server enforces who may edit or delete a message before an `UpdateEditMessage` or `UpdateDeleteMessages`/`UpdateDeleteChannelMessages` update is ever sent to a client, including a channel admin editing another admin's post (attributed to the channel itself, not an individual, so `telegram/normalize.go`'s `message` reports the same `SenderID` for it no matter which admin edited it - the correct outcome, not a gap). `OnDeleteMessages`' own scoping (`telegram/live.go`'s `deleteMessages`, searched across `nonChannelRemotes()`) relies on Telegram's private-chat and basic-group message ids sharing one space per account, confirmed safe and already covered by `TestHandleUpdates_DeleteMessagesScopesToNonChannelConversations`; a channel delete is scoped to that one channel's own remote id. Reviewed as part of this fix; no code or test change was needed there.

**Defence in depth: `app.Ingest.Edited` now also compares the stored sender.** `authorizedEdit` (`ingest.go`) re-reads the message an edit names and refuses when its stored `SenderID` differs from the edit's own, so a connector that forgets its own check (or a future connector that never gets one) still cannot have an edit applied under the wrong identity. `Ingest.Deleted` gets no equivalent check: `connector.Sink.Deleted`, one of this project's two stable published boundaries (see interfaces.md), carries no actor at all, only the conversations and ids to remove, and adding one now would mean breaking that signature for every existing connector to guard a path (WhatsApp's revoke) the connector-level fix above already closes. Left as the connector's own responsibility unless a second connector is found to need the same backstop `Edited` now has.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/connector/whatsapp/authorize_test.go` covers `authorizedActor` directly: the original sender accepted, the sender's phone and LID forms treated as equal, a different sender refused, an unknown message id refused, this account's own other-device edit/revoke accepted, a `fromMe` claim over someone else's message refused, and a nil media store refused. `live_test.go`'s `TestHandleMessage_RevokeAndEdit` now seeds the target message's key before revoking and editing it, and three new tests - `TestHandleMessage_RefusesARevokeFromSomeoneElse`, `TestHandleMessage_RefusesAnEditFromSomeoneElse`, `TestHandleMessage_RefusesARevokeOfAnUnknownMessage` - reproduce the vulnerability's own forged scenarios end to end through `handleMessage`, confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code. `backend/internal/app/edit_test.go`'s `TestIngestEdited_DropsAnEditFromADifferentSender` covers the app-layer backstop the same way.

## The WhatsApp sender-authorship fix above was itself incomplete: a reused message id could still overwrite `message_keys`

A follow-up security review found that the fix above - `authorizedActor` trusting `message_keys`' own record of a message's sender - never accounted for that record itself being overwritable. `keys.go`'s `putMessageKey` unconditionally upserted whatever `(conversation, message id)` row a new message named: `ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE SET sender_id = excluded.sender_id, from_me = excluded.from_me`. WhatsApp lets whoever sends a stanza choose its own id (whatsmeow only drops an exact duplicate ciphertext, never a different one reusing an id; `message.go`'s own dedup). So Mallory, in a group with Bob, could send any message of her own naming Bob's already-used id: the duplicate *message* insert fails on `store`'s own unique index, but `saveMessageKey` runs before that check and happily overwrites `message_keys`' row to say Mallory sent it. A revoke or edit she sends next for that same id then passes `authorizedActor` outright, deleting or rewriting Bob's message - or the user's own sent message, the same way, since a sent message's key carries no sender id of its own and only `fromMe` to distinguish it. This is exactly the forgery the fix above set out to close, reopened one layer down.

**The fix: first-writer-wins, enforced in the one SQL statement that writes the row.** `putMessageKey`'s own `ON CONFLICT` gained a `WHERE` clause: `message_keys.sender_id = excluded.sender_id AND message_keys.from_me = excluded.from_me`. A conflicting row is only ever updated (refreshing its timestamp, the one field that legitimately changes on a later save of the *same* message, such as resolving an undecryptable placeholder) when the identity being saved already matches what is on record; a different identity's save is silently rejected, logged through the existing `logDropped` with a new, safe reason (`reasonReusedMessageID`), and `authorizedActor` never sees anything but the original sender for that id. This is enforced in the database write itself, not only at a call site, so any future caller of `putMessageKey` inherits the guarantee rather than having to remember it.

**The same id-reuse gap existed in `media_refs` and the poll tables, with no sender column of their own to protect it the same way.** `storage.go`'s `media_refs` and `pollstore.go`'s `poll_meta`/`poll_options` are both keyed only by `(conversation_id, message_id)`, with nothing recording who saved a row - `saveMediaRef` and `savePoll` upsert unconditionally, the same shape of bug. Mallory reusing Bob's id with her own photo or poll would swap the file `FetchMedia` downloads, or the question and options a vote tallies against, for what still displays as Bob's message - and, for a poll, would additionally pollute `poll_options` with both senders' options at once, since each option's own id is a hash of its text and so never collides between two different polls. Rather than add a sender column to either table, `keys.go` gained `reusesAnotherSendersID` (checks a save's own identity against whatever `message_keys` already has for that id, the same source of truth `authorizedActor` trusts) and `saveIncomingKeyedData`, which `live.go`'s `handleContent` and `history.go`'s `syncMessage` now call instead of saving the media reference, key and poll separately: it skips all three together the moment the id is reused, rather than letting `media_refs` or the poll tables end up written by one sender while `message_keys` keeps the other.

**This also closes the same gap in `resolveUndecryptable`'s placeholder replacement, a variant the first review missed.** `handleContent` used to call `c.resolveUndecryptable` unconditionally once it reached that point, so Mallory naming a message still waiting on an undecryptable placeholder - whether Bob's or the user's own - would have her own content silently replace that placeholder (reported as an edit, not a new message) with nothing in `Ingest` to deduplicate it: unlike an ordinary duplicate insert, this path updates an existing row on purpose. `handleContent` now only calls `resolveUndecryptable` when `saveIncomingKeyedData` reports the save actually went ahead, so an id reused by a different sender is left for `resolveUndecryptable`'s own bookkeeping untouched, and the real sender's eventual, legitimate redelivery still resolves the placeholder normally.

**Audit of every other table a sender-chosen id reaches, and why each needed, or did not need, the same fix:**

- **`message_keys`, `media_refs`, `poll_meta`, `poll_options` (fixed, above).**
- **`poll_votes` (`pollstore.go`): safe already.** Keyed by `(conversation_id, message_id, voter_id, option_id)`, but `voter_id` is never sender-chosen in the sense that matters here: it is `reactorKey`'s own normalized, whatsmeow-authenticated sender identity (`"self"` or `personID`), the same value `authorizedActor` already trusts. A voter can only ever overwrite their *own* prior vote; one voter can never overwrite another's row, id reuse or not. The poll `message_id` a vote names is itself sender-chosen, but a vote against a `message_id` that was never saved as a real poll (`pollShell` not found) is already a no-op (`reportTally`), so naming someone else's non-poll id does nothing either.
- **Live reactions (`react.go`'s in-memory `c.reactions`): safe already.** Keyed by `conversationRemoteID + "/" + messageRemoteID`, each message's own tally is itself a map keyed by `reactorKey` - the same authenticated identity `poll_votes` uses, never a value the reacting sender chooses. Reusing someone else's message id as the *target* of a reaction only adds that reactor's own emoji to whatever tally already exists for that id (or starts a new one); it can never overwrite another reactor's own entry.
- **Edits, as a protocol action (not a table): safe already.** An incoming edit is authorized by `authorizedActor` against the now-protected `message_keys` row, the fix two sections above. An outgoing edit (`edit.go`'s `editableKey`) only ever acts on a key this account itself saved with `fromMe: true`, and `putMessageKey`'s own fix means a third party reusing that id can no longer make `fromMe` false for it, so `editableKey`'s `!key.fromMe` guard still holds.

**Tests.** `keys_test.go` replaces the old "later save wins" test (the behaviour this fix reverses) with `TestMessageKey_PutKeepsTheFirstSenderWhenAnotherOneReusesTheID`, `TestMessageKey_PutKeepsOurOwnSentMessageWhenAnIncomingSenderReusesTheID`, and `TestMessageKey_PutUpdatesTheTimestampWhenTheSameSenderSavesAgain` (confirming a legitimate re-save, same identity, still refreshes). `live_revoke_edit_test.go` gained `TestHandleMessage_RevokeSurvivesAnIDReusedByADifferentSender` and `TestHandleMessage_RevokeSurvivesAnIDReusedForOurOwnSentMessage`, driving Mallory's message and her forged revoke through `handleMessage` end to end, both confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code. `live_mediaref_test.go`'s `TestHandleMessage_KeepsTheFirstSendersMediaRefWhenAnotherOneReusesTheID` and `poll_test.go`'s `TestHandleMessage_KeepsTheFirstSendersPollWhenAnotherOneReusesTheID` cover the same id-reuse protection for `media_refs` and the poll tables.

## The id-reuse fix above still let the reused message itself reach the sink, and the store merge it into another sender's row

A follow-up security review found that `saveIncomingKeyedData`'s own refusal (the fix two sections above) was never acted on by its two callers. `live.go`'s `handleContent` called it inside `if saveIncomingKeyedData(...) && c.resolveUndecryptable(...)`: `&&` short-circuits on a `false` left side, so a refusal simply skipped the placeholder-replacement branch and fell through to the same `sink.History`/`sink.Incoming` call every other message reaches. `history.go`'s `syncMessage` called it and discarded the bool outright, then called `sink.History` unconditionally. In both connectors' case, Mallory reusing Alice's id - or the user's own - with her own text, media, reply or reactions was still reported to `app.Ingest` as if it were a legitimate update, exactly the content `saveIncomingKeyedData` was already refusing to let into `message_keys`, `media_refs` and the poll tables.

From there, `store.addMessage` made it worse: `existingByRemote` finds a stored message purely by `(conversation_id, remote_id)`, and the only check before merging a new message's media into it was `existing.Media != nil || m.Media == nil` - whether the slot was already filled and whether anything new was offered, never who either message was from. So even a connector that *did* drop the sink call for a reused id would still have been one step from exposure: any other call path that reaches `AddMessage`/`AddHistoryMessage` with a reused remote id and a sender mismatch merges regardless, since nothing before this fix compared sender or direction at all.

**The fix, at both layers.** `handleContent` now returns immediately when `saveIncomingKeyedData` refuses, before either the undecryptable-replacement branch or the ordinary `History`/`Incoming` report; `syncMessage` now returns `false` the same way, instead of calling `sink.History`. Neither function reports anything at all for a refused id - not as an edit, not as a new message - so nothing about Mallory's reused-id message (text, media, reply or reactions) ever reaches `app.Ingest`. Separately, and regardless of what any connector does, `store.messages.go` gained `sameSender(existing, m domain.Message) bool`, comparing `SenderID` and `Outgoing`: `addMessage`'s merge condition now also requires `sameSender(existing, m)`, so a message naming an already-stored remote id is only ever treated as filling in that same message's own media when it agrees on who sent it and in which direction. A mismatch is silently treated as a duplicate of the existing row (returned unchanged, `inserted` false) rather than inserted separately: the schema has no uniqueness constraint on `(conversation_id, remote_id)` to fall back on for a second row, and `MessageByRemote`'s single-row lookup is exactly what every edit, reaction and reply-quote resolution key off, so a second row under the same id would only reopen the ambiguity this fix closes.

**Why `EditMessage`, reactions and status updates needed no equivalent change.** Audited as part of this fix:

- **`store.EditMessage`:** its only caller fed by an untrusted sender is `app.Ingest.Edited`, which already calls `authorizedEdit` - comparing the stored message's `SenderID` against the edit's own - before ever reaching the store (see the sender-authorship fix above). `app.Commands.EditMessage` and its `revertEdit` call it too, but only for a message already resolved by its own local id, never by a sender-chosen remote id reused to impersonate another row.
- **Reactions (`store.SetReactions`):** a reaction tally carries no sender of the *message* it decorates - any chat member may legitimately react to any other member's message - so there is no "wrong sender" for a reused id to forge here; `domain.Reaction` has no sender field to compare.
- **Status updates (`UpdateMessageStatus`, `SetMessageRemoteID`):** both are keyed by the message's own local id, assigned by this account when it created an outgoing message, never by a service-chosen remote id an attacker could reuse to reach someone else's row.

**Tests.** `live_test.go` gained `TestHandleContent_DropsALiveMessageThatReusesAnotherSendersID` and `TestHandleContent_DropsALiveMessageThatReusesOurOwnMessagesID`; `history_test.go` gained `TestHandleHistorySync_DropsASyncedMessageThatReusesAnotherSendersID`; all three drive the reused-id scenario end to end through the connector and confirm nothing reaches the sink, failing against the pre-fix code. `store/messages_test.go` gained `TestAddMessage_RefusesToFillMediaFromADifferentSender` and `TestAddMessage_RefusesToFillMediaWhenOutgoingDiffers`, alongside the existing `TestAddMessage_KeepsMediaAndFillsItInLater` as the same-sender regression case.

## The cover-art fix above was itself incomplete: `hasCoverArtAtom` only ever looked at the first `udta` box's own `firstChild`

A follow-up security review found that the fix above - `hasCoverArtAtom` in both `audiosafe/isobmff.go` and `videosafe/isobmff.go` - only ever walked the single, most common path: `moov`'s *first* `udta` child, that `udta`'s *first* `meta` child, and so on down to `covr`. `firstChild` stops at the first box of the requested type (or the first box it cannot parse at all) and never looks further, so this missed every other place FFmpeg's own mov demuxer (`mov_read_udta`, `mov_read_meta`) also reads a `covr` box from: a second `udta` sibling, after an empty or decoy first one; a bare `meta` directly under `moov`, with no `udta` wrapper at all; a `meta` at the file's own top level, outside `moov` entirely; the same shapes under a `trak`'s own `udta`; and, separately, `firstChild` returning "not found" (not "refuse") the moment it hit a box it could not parse, so a single malformed decoy box placed ahead of a real `udta > meta > ilst > covr` atom hid it completely - the check reported no cover art while FFmpeg could still read the real one behind the decoy.

**The fix: a bounded, recursive walk of the whole file, not one fixed path.** Both `isobmff.go`s gained an identical `hasCoverArtAnywhere`/`walkForCoverArt`/`coverArtContainer`, replacing `hasCoverArtAtom`. The walk starts at the file's own top level and recurses into every child box of type `moov`, `trak`, `udta`, `meta` (skipping its own 4-byte FullBox version/flags first) or `ilst` - the five box types a cover art atom's path can ever pass through - checking every sibling at every level, not only the first match, and refusing outright (`ok = false`) the moment any box anywhere in that walk cannot be parsed, rather than silently treating an unparseable box as "no cover art here." A box of any other type is skipped over by its own declared size and never descended into: most of this format's leaf boxes (`mdhd`, `hdlr`, `stsd`, `tkhd`, and the rest) open with a FullBox version/flags field, or other fixed data such as `tkhd`'s own flags byte, that would otherwise misparse as a nested box header - recursing into literally every box, not only the five container types, was tried and rejected for exactly this reason: a real `tkhd`'s own non-zero flags byte reads as a box size smaller than any valid header, which would have refused every ordinary MP4 carrying one. The recursion is bounded both ways the brief asked for: the existing shared `c.boxes` budget (`maxBoxBudget`, already decremented by every `boxHeader` call across one `Validate` call) still caps total work, and a new `maxCoverArtDepth` (16) caps how many levels deep the recursion itself will ever go, so a file nesting trivial single boxes many levels deep cannot grow this walk's own stack without bound either. Running out of either budget mid-walk means the scan could not finish, and an unfinished scan is refused exactly like a malformed box: `found` is never trusted once `ok` is false.

**Video and audio tracks whose own handler or codec marks them as an image were already refused, audited and now also tested directly.** The brief's other half - "a track whose handler/codec is an image or carries an attached-picture disposition" - was already closed by each package's existing allowlist, confirmed by reading the code and adding a test that locks it in rather than merely asserting it from memory: `audiosafe`'s `isoAudioTrackAllowed` refuses any track whose handler is not exactly `"soun"` outright (no video-handled track, "attached-picture" or not, is ever allowed in an audio file at all), and `videosafe`'s `allowedISOVideoCodec` admits only `avc1`, so a track using an image codec such as `mjpeg`, `png` or `bmp` - the codecs FFmpeg's own mov demuxer marks `AV_DISPOSITION_ATTACHED_PIC` for - was already refused the same way an unsupported video codec always is. There is no field in the container itself that states a track's "disposition": FFmpeg infers it from handler, codec and sample count together, so there is nothing more specific this package could check than the codec and handler allowlists it already enforces.

**Tests.** Both packages' `helpers_test.go` gained `mp4CoverArtMeta` (factored out of the existing `mp4CoverArtUdta`) plus `mp4CoverArtUnderSecondUdta`, `mp4CoverArtBehindMalformedBox`, and a new `coverArtPlacement` fixture field ("moovMeta", "secondUdta", "malformedDecoy", "topLevel") alongside the existing `coverArt` bool for the original placement. Both `isobmff_test.go`s gained one case per new placement in the existing `TestValidate_MP4Audio`/`TestValidate_MP4` tables, each confirmed to fail (reporting `playable = true`) against the pre-fix code, plus fuzz seeds for all four, and a case naming an image codec (`mjpg`) in the one track slot each package allows, confirmed already refused by the existing allowlist with no code change needed. `FuzzValidateMP4Audio` and `FuzzValidateMP4` each ran 30 seconds locally with no new crashers. Checked against real files built with `ffmpeg` into `build/` and deleted immediately after (never committed): a plain AAC M4A and a plain H.264 MP4 each validated playable; the same two files with a real `mjpeg`-coded `attached_pic` stream added (`ffmpeg`'s own mov muxer writes this as a genuine `moov > udta > meta > ilst > covr` atom, confirmed with `ffprobe`'s `DISPOSITION:attached_pic` and a byte-level count of each box's own tag) were both refused.

## Qt's own clipboard reader must never see a foreign offer, even as Ctrl+V's own fallback

A follow-up security review found that `media.paste`'s own bound, size- and time-capped `wl-paste` read (see "A pasted or outgoing image is capped and sanitized the same way a downloaded one is" above) protected only the image path. `ComposerController.pasteImage`'s fallback, once `media.paste` found no image or was refused for one too large or too slow, called the real text field's own `input.paste()` - Qt's in-process `QClipboard` read, with no size or time limit of its own, reading the exact same clipboard offer the helper had just refused. A clipboard owner offering both `image/png` and `text/plain`, each an endless or trickling stream, made the helper's own cap useless: Ctrl+V still handed the same hostile offer straight to Qt, which has no cap at all, growing the shell's memory without bound or blocking its UI thread indefinitely.

**The fix: a second helper-side read, never the field's own `paste()`.** `clipboard.Wayland.ReadText` (`backend/internal/clipboard/clipboard.go`) is `Read`'s own bounded `wl-paste` path, reused with a far smaller cap - `MaxPasteTextLen` (32 KiB), comfortably more than `domain.MaxTextLength`'s 4096 code points' worst-case UTF-8 size, since nothing this UI ever sends as text needs anywhere near that much room. `clipboard.readText` (`backend/internal/server/clipboard.go`) exposes it, additive to the protocol (`docs/api.md`): `{}` in, `{text}` out, an invalid-input error when the clipboard holds no text. `ComposerController.paste` (renamed from `pasteImage`, since it now handles both) tries `media.paste` first, same as before; on any refusal it now calls `clipboard.readText` instead of the field's own `paste()`, and asks the caller to insert the result at the cursor (`textInsertRequested`, the same shape `newlineRequested` already uses for Shift+Enter) rather than ever touching Qt's own clipboard reader. A refusal of the text read too - the clipboard holds nothing, or the helper's own bounded read hit its cap or timed out - shows a short notice through the open conversation's own `lastError`, the same banner every other refused request already uses.

**Shift+Insert, middle-click and a context menu: the other three ways to trigger a native paste.** `compose.attach` (Ctrl+V) now also binds `Shift+Insert`, the other standard paste shortcut, to the same handler (`ui/lib/Keymap.js`). Middle-click paste (the Wayland primary selection, a second, separate clipboard Qt would also read in-process with no cap) is not routed through a second helper-side read - that would mean a second clipboard-reading path, doubling this surface for one rarely used gesture - instead the composer's existing right-click `MouseArea` (added for the spelling picker) now also claims `Qt.MiddleButton` and swallows it outright, so the TextArea's own built-in handler never sees the press at all. A context-menu "Paste" was not found anywhere in this UI to begin with: neither `Composer`'s `TextArea` nor `ListColumn`'s `Ui.TextField` wires up a `ContextMenu`, and the only right-click handling present is that same spelling-picker `MouseArea`, which already claims the press before any default menu could appear.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/clipboard/clipboard_test.go` covers `ReadText`'s own bound, failure and cap the same way `Read`'s already are. `backend/internal/app/paste_test.go` covers `PasteText`. `backend/internal/server/methods_test.go` covers `clipboard.readText` end to end. `tests/qml/ComposerController` drives `paste()`'s own fallback - including that a reply for a conversation no longer open never even starts the text read, the same staleness guard the image path already had - and the refusal notice.

## The search field and the composer's input exist only while actually being edited, not merely while shown

A follow-up security review found the fix above ("The search field and the composer's text input are destroyed, not merely hidden...") still left both fields themselves live sinks: `ListColumn`'s search field was gated on `root.visible` (true for as long as the list column is the one shown, focused or not, even on another workspace) and `Composer`'s input on `active`, itself only ever "is the conversation pane open" - the same "merely visible" gap `tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs` otherwise exists to close for every other field in this UI, just not yet for these two. Simply having the window open in a wide layout, with no conversation open and nobody typing, kept the search field alive and reading Wayland clipboard changes the whole time.

**Each field now has its own, narrower edit-intent flag**, read by its own Loader instead of the broader "is my column shown" condition: `ListColumn._editingSearch` and `Composer._writingRequested`. Both start `false` and turn `true` only from a real request to start typing - a click on the plain `Text` each field's own column now shows in its place (`searchPlaceholder`/`composerPlaceholder`, styled the same dimmed way the real field used to look while not writing), `focusSearch()`/`focusInput()` called directly, or (for the composer) `compose.focus` (i/a) and the Up-into-an-empty-draft edit path - and turn `false` again the instant the real field's own `onActiveFocusChanged` reports it lost focus for any reason (Escape, Tab, a click elsewhere), destroying it through the same Loader rather than leaving it merely unfocused. `_focusPending`/`_searchFocusPending` (the existing idiom) grants the focus a `focusInput()`/`focusSearch()` call asked for once the Loader actually finishes loading, the same as before.

**What still brings each field in automatically, and why that is not the same gap.** Opening a conversation already auto-focuses the composer (`MessengerLayout`'s `Connections` on `conversationController.activeId`, unchanged); restoring one on the panel being torn down and rebuilt (Omarchy reloading the plugin) goes through `ConversationController._restore()`'s own `Component.onCompleted`, which can run - and change `activeId` - before that `Connections` block even exists yet in the freshly built tree, missing the signal outright; `MessengerLayout` now also checks once for an already-open conversation from its own `Component.onCompleted`, deferred with `Qt.callLater` so it runs only once the whole tree is guaranteed built. Scheduling a message via the "Send later…" palette, and `message.edit`'s Up-key path, both already require the composer focused before they run, so clearing the draft or loading another into it does not need to ask again; `message.edit`'s other path, Shift+E on a highlighted message, deliberately does not focus the composer at all - the very next key the real-keyboard flow this guards (`tests/qml/MessageHighlight`'s own `checkShiftEEditsHighlightedMessage`) presses is plain `e`, which must still reach the reactions picker, not be typed into a field Shift+E never asked for. `WindowController._scheduleDraft`'s own completion now does ask again (`composerController.composeFocusRequested()`) once a message is actually queued, since that path starts from writing (Ctrl+L only works with text already typed) and should hand it back.

**`tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs` gained a check `fieldSelfGated` cannot make on its own:** that check only asks whether a field sits inside *some* gated `Loader`, and a `Loader` gated on `root.visible` reads exactly like one gated on `_editingSearch` to it - both are "not a bare `true`". A new test reads each field's own `active:` binding directly and requires it to name the real edit-intent flag (`_writingRequested`/`_editingSearch`), confirmed to fail against the pre-fix `root.visible`/`root.active` bindings. `tests/qml/OverlayLifecycle`'s own baseline (`fieldsAlwaysPresent`) dropped the search field - it is no longer the one field this fix's `ALLOWLIST`-era reasoning excused as "always on screen" - and `tests/qml/FieldLifetime`, `tests/qml/ChatNavigation`, `tests/qml/Flows` and `tests/qml/VoicePlayback` were updated for the same reason every check elsewhere in this project that reads "a field exists but is not focused" now reads "the field does not exist at all" instead: a composer-controller-level fact (`composerController.draft`, reached through a new `objectName` on it in `Panel.qml`) replaces reading the real text field's own `.text` wherever a check's point was the loaded text, not whether the field happened to be focused right then.

## A reply quote could be relabelled by a rename that happened in a different conversation

A security review found that `store.RefreshSenderName`'s own reply-quote correction (`sendernames.go`'s `refreshReplyQuotes`) matched the wrong thing. It collected every remote message id the renamed sender has ever sent, anywhere in the account (`sentRemoteIDs`), then rewrote `SenderName` on any reply, in any conversation, whose quoted `RemoteID` was in that set (`staleQuotes`). A message's own remote id is only unique within one conversation (`messages_remote`'s own unique index is `(conversation_id, remote_id)`), never across the whole account, so this matched on the wrong axis: on WhatsApp, a second sender can simply send their own message reusing an id already used in a conversation they are not even in - the existing reused-id guard (`message_keys`, see the fix above) is keyed per conversation and never fires for a different one - and on Telegram, private-chat and supergroup/channel message ids collide naturally, with nothing crafted needed at all. Either way, renaming that second sender rewrote a quote that had never named them: Alice posts message X in group G; Bob replies there, quoting it; Mallory, unrelated to X, reuses the same remote id on a message of her own elsewhere in the account; renaming Mallory relabelled Bob's quote of Alice with Mallory's new name.

**The fix: a reply keeps its own quoted sender id, resolved and matched within one conversation, never a shared remote id.** `domain.Reply` gained `SenderID` (additive, `docs/api.md`), the quoted message's own sender id. `store.resolveReply` (`messages.go`) fills it exactly the way it already fills `SenderName` and `Text`: by looking up the quoted message through `MessageByRemote`, scoped to the reply's own `ConversationID` - never the whole account - so the id this field carries can only ever have come from a message actually in that same conversation. `app.Commands.resolveReplyTo` (`messages.go`), which builds an outgoing reply's quote directly rather than through `resolveReply`, sets it from the same already-conversation-checked `quoted.SenderID`. `refreshReplyQuotes` now scopes its own query to the conversations the renamed sender actually has a message in (dropping `sentRemoteIDs` entirely), and `staleQuotes` matches each candidate quote's stored `SenderID` against the sender being renamed, never a quoted message's `RemoteID`: a quote with no stored `SenderID` - one whose quote could not be resolved when it was first stored - is never rewritten. Both the query's own conversation scope and the per-row `SenderID` check have to agree before a quote is touched, so even a sender who genuinely has messages in both the group and the colliding conversation still cannot relabel a quote that was never theirs.

**Existing rows needed a one-time backfill, not a shrug.** A reply stored before `SenderID` existed carries none, so `backfillReplySenderIDs` (`replysenderid.go`), the migration's last step, resolves each one the same way `resolveReply` would have, strictly within its own conversation; one whose quote cannot be resolved there - including one that only resolves against a message in a *different* conversation, the exact shape this fix exists to stop trusting - is left with no `SenderID` at all, exactly as an unresolved reply already is, rather than guessed from the wrong conversation's message.

**Tests.** `store/sendernames_test.go` gained `TestRefreshSenderName_NeverRelabelsAQuoteFromAnotherConversationsReusedRemoteID` (the WhatsApp scenario above, with the renamed sender also posting in the group, so the query's own conversation scope alone would not have been enough without the per-row `SenderID` check) and `TestRefreshSenderName_IgnoresANaturallyCollidingRemoteIDInAnotherConversation` (Telegram's natural id collision, no crafted input); the existing `TestRefreshSenderName_CorrectsAReplyQuoteToo` continues to cover the normal case, a sender's own rename reaching their own quotes in their own chat. `store/messages_test.go`'s `TestAddMessage_FillsReplySenderAndTextFromTheQuotedMessage` now also checks `SenderID`. `app/messages_test.go` gained `TestSend_WithReplyToAContactsMessageCarriesThatSendersID`. `store/migrate_test.go` gained `TestMigrate_BackfillsReplySenderID`, covering a resolvable quote, one already carrying a `SenderID`, one that would only resolve against a different conversation's message (confirmed to stay empty) and idempotency.

## `videosafe`'s SPS check bound only the cropped size; FFmpeg allocates frames at the coded size, with however many reference buffers the SPS asks for

A security review found that `picSizeFromSPS` computed an H.264 SPS's coded picture size from its macroblock counts, subtracted the crop offsets, and returned only that cropped size - the one value `isoVideoTrackAllowed` ever compared against `maxDimension` and the container's own width/height. The coded size itself was bounded only by `maxExpGolombPrefix`, which still allows roughly 2 million pixels per side; `level_idc` was read and discarded; `max_num_ref_frames` and bit depth were skipped entirely. A sender could declare an SPS whose macroblock counts describe an enormous coded picture, crop it down to a small, "safe-looking" size that matches the container's own `tkhd`/`avc1` fields exactly, and have the file reported `playable`. FFmpeg allocates its decode and reference-picture buffers at the coded size, not the cropped one, so one click on such a video in the shell's own inline player would make FFmpeg allocate hundreds of megabytes to several gigabytes per frame, times however many reference frames the same SPS also declares.

**The fix bounds what FFmpeg actually allocates, not merely what it finally shows.** `picSizeFromSPS` (`h264sps.go`) now returns the coded width and height alongside the cropped ones, and `parseSPSFields` requires all of the following before accepting the SPS at all:

- **Coded size.** `dimensionsWithinCap` (already used for the cropped/container comparison) is applied to the coded size too: both axes, before cropping, must be within `maxDimension` (1920) independently of whatever the cropped size says.
- **Crop slack.** The gap between a coded and a cropped axis must be under one macroblock's own width (`maxCropSlack`, 16px) horizontally, or one macroblock row's height vertically (16px, doubled to 32 for field-coded pictures, which count two field lines per map unit). Real encoders only crop the small remainder a coded dimension rounds up to a whole macroblock; a crop at or past that bound means the coded and cropped sizes were never meant to agree the way the container comparison assumes - confirmed against real `ffmpeg`-encoded 1920x1080 (cropped 8px) and 640x360 (cropped 8px) video, both well under the bound, and checked that a crafted SPS cropping 16000x16000 down to a plausible 640x2000 is refused by this check alone.
- **Reference frames, two ways.** `max_num_ref_frames` must be at most `maxH264RefFrames` (16, the absolute ceiling every H.264 level allows per Annex A), and `h264BoundsOK`'s own `maxCodedFrameBudget` check bounds coded pixels times (ref count + 1) - the number of same-sized buffers FFmpeg's decoder actually allocates - against the product `maxDimension² × (maxH264RefFrames+1)`: an explicit, single assertion of a bound the two per-field checks already imply, computed from those same constants rather than a separate magic number, so it can never silently fall out of step with either.
- **The declared level, as a cross-check, not a trust.** `level_idc` is now read and checked against `h264Levels`, a table of Annex A's own Table A-1 (`maxFS`, the largest coded frame size in macroblocks a level allows, and `maxDpbMbs`, the largest decoded picture buffer): a `level_idc` this package does not recognize refuses the file outright, the coded frame size in macroblocks must fit the level's own `maxFS`, and `max_num_ref_frames` must fit `floor(maxDpbMbs / frameSizeInMbs)` (also capped at `maxH264RefFrames`). This is what actually ties a coded size to a plausible reference-frame count: a sender declaring a low level with a tiny `maxDpbMbs` cannot also claim a large coded size with many reference frames, even when each bound alone would pass.
- **Bit depth and chroma format.** `chromaArrayTypeOf` now also reports whether both `bit_depth_luma_minus8` and `bit_depth_chroma_minus8` read as 0 (profiles without these fields default to plain 8-bit), and `parseSPSFields` refuses anything else - 10-bit and higher decode to a wider buffer this package has never bounded. The same central check restricts `chromaArrayType` to exactly 1 (4:2:0): 4:2:2, 4:4:4 and monochrome/separate-colour-plane streams are refused outright rather than bounded for a wider per-pixel cost, since nothing this UI sends or receives needs them (a phone camera's H.264 encode is 8-bit 4:2:0 in practice).

**Why a cross-check against the declared level, rather than trusting maxDimension and the ref-frame cap alone.** The flat caps bound the worst case either allows on its own, but nothing short of the level check ties coded size to a plausible reference-frame count for that size: a 1920x1920 stream (within `maxDimension` on both axes) declaring 8 reference frames looks fine against the flat 16-frame cap, but level 5.0's own `maxDpbMbs` (110400) only supports 7 reference buffers at that frame size (14400 macroblocks) - a real encoder would never pair this size with level 5.0 and 8 references, and a sender doing so anyway gets refused.
