**Tests.** `h264sps_internal_test.go`'s `TestParseSPSDims` gained one case per new bound: a coded size past `maxDimension` with no crop to hide it, a crop of exactly one macroblock column's own width refused on its own (an otherwise ordinary 640x480 coded size), the full attack shape (16000x16000 coded, cropped to a plausible 640x2000), `max_num_ref_frames` at and one past the absolute ceiling, a reference count within that ceiling but past what the declared level allows for the coded size, an unrecognized `level_idc`, non-8-bit luma and chroma depths, and the existing 4:2:2/4:4:4/separate-colour-plane cases (previously expected to succeed) updated to expect refusal. Each new case was confirmed to depend on its own bound, not a different one already catching the same input, by temporarily loosening that one constant and watching only that case fail. `FuzzParseSPSDims` gained a seed per bound. The package's own fixture builders (`buildAVCSPS` in `helpers_test.go`, and every SPS builder in `h264sps_internal_test.go`) declare `level_idc` 50 (5.0) rather than an arbitrary fixed value, generous enough for every size this package's own tests build; real `ffmpeg`-encoded H.264 (720x1280 high profile, 1920x1080, 640x360 baseline, 848x480, built into `build/` and checked against `videosafe.Validate` directly, then deleted) all still validate as playable - none needed the level bumped past what `ffmpeg` itself declared.

**Revalidation of already-cached video.** `VideoPlayableForMedia` (`app/media.go`) caches its own verdict in `videoVerdicts`, a plain in-memory map created fresh in `app.go`'s own `Commands` construction - it holds nothing across a helper restart. A video cached by an older helper is therefore revalidated against this fix the first time `media.fetch` reads it after an upgrade, the same guarantee every other `videosafe`/`audiosafe` tightening has relied on.

## `audiosafe`'s ID3v2 frame walker and FFmpeg's own demuxer parsed a v2.4 frame's own size differently, letting an attached picture hide in bytes the walker skipped

A differential security review found that `id3v2FrameSize` always decoded a v2.4 frame's own declared size as synchsafe (masking nothing), while FFmpeg's `id3v2_parse` falls back to a different decoding once a frame's plain big-endian size reads above `0x7f`: it re-reads the same bytes as a synchsafe size with the high bit of each byte simply dropped, and if a valid frame id happens to sit at the offset that gives, treats the original frame as empty and resumes there. A sender could craft a tag where audiosafe's own walk reads a `TXXX` frame's size as a few bytes and skips past it, reporting the tag safe, while FFmpeg reads the same bytes as a zero-length frame and finds a real `APIC` frame sitting inside what audiosafe had already walked past and called padding. That picture then reaches FFmpeg's id3v2 demuxer, and Qt's FFmpeg plugin decodes it with `QImage::fromData` - unchecked by `imagesafe` - exactly the sink the original ID3v2-walking fix (see the "Cover art in audio and video" entry above) existed to close off, reopened by the walker and FFmpeg disagreeing about where a frame actually ends.

**The fix taken is deliberately blunt, not a closer match to FFmpeg's own parsing.** Tracking every way the two parsers could still disagree - this gap, and whatever the next one turns out to be - is an open-ended chase with no way to prove it is finished. Instead, an MP3 is only ever played in the shell's own in-window player when it carries **no ID3v2 tag at all**, anywhere in the file (leading, appended after the audio, or a second one alongside a first), and no APE tag and no Lyrics3 tag either (`mp3HasForbiddenTag`, `mp3.go`): with no tag structure left to walk, there is nothing left for the two parsers to parse differently. An ID3v1 trailer - a fixed 128 bytes of plain text fields, with no room for a picture at all - is still allowed, and only where the format ever puts one: at the very end. The whole former frame walker (`id3v2.go`: `id3v2Scan`, `id3v2FramesSafe`, `id3v2FrameHeader`, `id3v2FrameSize`, and the forbidden-frame-id table) is gone; nothing else in this package used it.

**Why a plain byte-signature scan, not a structural check.** `mp3HasForbiddenTag` looks for three fixed byte sequences anywhere in the file - `ID3` (an ID3v2 header, at any version), `APETAGEX` (an APE tag's own header or footer) and `LYRICSBEGIN` (the opening marker both Lyrics3 v1 and v2 share) - read in bounded, overlapping 64 KiB chunks (`mp3ScanChunkSize`) so a marker straddling a chunk boundary is never missed and the whole file is never loaded into memory at once. This can in principle refuse a file whose audio data happens to contain one of these sequences by chance (astronomically unlikely for an 11-byte marker, more plausible but still rare for the 3-byte `ID3`), but a false refusal only means the file does not play inline - it still opens fine in the user's own player, so erring conservative here costs nothing a real false positive would notice.

**Most real MP3s will now be refused in-window, and that is accepted.** Essentially every encoder writes an ID3v2 tag by default (confirmed against real `libmp3lame`-encoded output via `ffmpeg`: the default run carries an `ID3` header from byte zero; only `-id3v2_version 0 -write_id3v1 0` produces a tag-free file, which still validates as playable). Shared MP3s and songs will therefore usually fall back to "open externally" rather than play inline, the same safe fallback an unsupported codec already takes. Voice notes - the common case this package actually needs to keep working in-window - are unaffected: WhatsApp and Telegram voice notes are Opus (inside Ogg or M4A), never MP3, and M4A/Ogg go through their own, unrelated checks (`isobmff.go`, `ogg.go`).

**Tests.** `mp3_test.go`'s `TestValidate_MP3RefusesAnyForbiddenTag` covers every place and shape of tag `mp3HasForbiddenTag` refuses - a leading ID3v2 tag with only ordinary text frames, one with an attached picture (both now refused the same way, where the old walker told them apart), an ID3v2 tag appended after the audio, a second ID3v2 tag alongside a first, an APE tag, and a Lyrics3 tag - confirmed, by temporarily disabling the new check, that the appended/second/APE/Lyrics3 cases actually depend on it (the leading-tag cases also fail without it, for the pre-existing reason that an ID3v2 header is never a valid MP3 frame sync, so they serve as a regression check on the overall required behaviour rather than isolating the new scan specifically). `TestValidate_MP3ForbiddenTagAcrossAChunkBoundary` covers the chunk-overlap bound directly. `TestValidate_MP3`'s "a trailing ID3v1 tag... is still allowed" case confirms the one tag kind this package does not refuse. `FuzzValidateMP3` gained seeds for each marker and each real tag shape. Confirmed against real `ffmpeg`-encoded MP3 (`libmp3lame`, CBR 128kbps): a file built with no ID3v2 or ID3v1 tag at all validates as playable; `ffmpeg`'s own default output (which writes an ID3v2 tag) is refused, matching the accepted trade-off above - both built into `build/` and deleted immediately after.

## Qt's own clipboard reader must never see a foreign offer, even in a field that is not the composer

A follow-up security review found the entry above ("Qt's own clipboard reader must never see a foreign offer, even as Ctrl+V's own fallback") stopped at the composer. `Keymap.js`'s own `compose.attach` binding only ever matched in the `compose` context, so `Panel.routeKey` returned `false` for Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert everywhere else, leaving each field's own `TextInput`/`TextEdit` to handle them itself - the exact unbounded, in-process `QClipboard` read the fix above removed from the composer. The same gap covered middle-click (the Wayland primary selection, a second clipboard Qt also reads in-process) and Qt 6.9's own right-click "Paste" menu, which the fix above's own claim that "neither `Composer`'s `TextArea` nor `ListColumn`'s `Ui.TextField` wires up a `ContextMenu`" got wrong: neither wires one up *deliberately*, but Qt's own `TextField.qml`/`TextArea.qml` style implementation sets `ContextMenu.menu: TextEditingContextMenu { editor: control }` by default, and nothing in this UI had ever overridden it back to nothing - the composer's own right-click handling only happened to pre-empt it by swallowing the press first, before any default menu could appear, which is not true of a field with no right-click handling of its own at all (the palette, both search fields, every account setup field, an account's alias).

**The fix: one global key binding, one generic insert, applied to every other field by hand.** `field.paste` (`Keymap.js`) binds Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert in the `global` context; a context binding is tried first in `match()`, so `compose.attach` keeps owning both keys unchanged while composing. `Panel.routeKey` answers `field.paste` itself, rather than dispatching to a controller the way every other action does: there is no one controller to own "paste into whichever field is focused", since that field can belong to any of four different controllers depending on which overlay is open. It reads `keyArea.Window.activeFocusItem` - the one thing every `TextField` and `TextArea` already has in common, needing no reference threaded down through whichever component and controller sit in between - and hands it to a new `ClipboardPaste` (`ui/controllers/ClipboardPaste.qml`), the composer's own `clipboard.readText` round trip factored out into something every other field shares: it reads the clipboard's text through the same bounded helper call, collapses line breaks (`ui/lib/Paste.js`'s `forSingleLine`, since every field reached this way is single-line - the composer owns Ctrl+V itself, in its own context), and inserts the result at the field's own cursor, wrapped in a `try`/`catch` since the field can be destroyed - its Loader closing as its own overlay closes - before the reply arrives. `ClipboardPaste` is a plain `QtObject`; Panel.qml holds the one instance every field's paste reaches through.

**Middle-click and the right-click menu are closed per field, by hand, since neither goes through a key at all.** `MiddleClickGuard` (`ui/components/MiddleClickGuard.qml`) is a `MouseArea` with `acceptedButtons: Qt.MiddleButton`: a middle-click never reaches anything else, since the other two buttons are never delivered to it, and it swallows the one it does accept the same way the composer's own right-click handler already swallowed middle-click. Every field outside the composer gets one as a direct child. `ContextMenu.menu: null` on each field removes Qt's own right-click "Paste" outright, rather than relying on it never being reached; the composer's own `TextArea` gained the same explicit override, so it no longer depends on its spelling picker's `MouseArea` happening to pre-empt the press first.

**Every field also gained a `maximumLength`** (`ui/lib/Limits.js`: `PALETTE_QUERY`, `SEARCH_QUERY`, `CONTACT_QUERY`, `API_ID`, `API_HASH`, `SETUP_ANSWER`, `ACCOUNT_ALIAS`), so a paste or a burst of typing is capped at the field's own sensible limit, not only at whatever the helper's own `clipboard.readText` already caps at (32 KiB). `TextArea` has no `maximumLength` property at all - the composer keeps its own, larger, Unicode-aware cap (`SpellCheck.capComposerText`) instead, unchanged.

**Fields covered:** the command palette's search field (`CommandPalette.qml`), the conversation list's search field (`ListColumn.qml`), the new-chat dialog's contact search (`NewChatDialog.qml`), account setup's api_id, api_hash and phone/code/password fields (`AccountSetup.qml`), and an account's alias field (`RenameAccount.qml`). `docs/decisions.md:1514`'s old claim is corrected by this entry, not rewritten in place, so the mistake and the fix both stay on record.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/field-paste-guard.test.cjs` scans every `TextField`, `TextArea` and `TextInput` declared anywhere in `ui/` and fails the build on one missing `maximumLength`, `ContextMenu.menu: null`, a `MiddleClickGuard` child, or (for a single-line field) a fall-through to `root.routeKey` - the composer's own field is the one documented exception, confirmed present and recognised rather than silently excluded. `tests/unit/keymap.test.cjs` covers `field.paste` resolving in `search`, `palette`, `dialog`, `chooseService` and `removeAccount`, and `compose.attach` still winning in `compose`, for both keys. `tests/qml/FieldPaste` drives real Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert key events into the palette, search and setup fields against a scripted fake service, confirming each ends up holding the fake clipboard's own text - something only the helper round trip could have put there, since this offscreen test has no real Wayland clipboard for Qt's own native paste to have read instead.

## `field.paste`/`compose.attach` only ever matched Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert; Qt's own paste shortcut, and a rebindable key list, both still let the native reader through

A follow-up security review found the entry above ("Qt's own clipboard reader must never see a foreign offer, even in a field that is not the composer") still left two gaps. First, Qt's `QKeySequence::StandardKey::Paste` is bigger than the two keys `field.paste`/`compose.attach` list: on Linux it also resolves Ctrl+Shift+Insert (`QQuickTextInput`/`TextEdit` paste the primary selection for it directly), and some platform themes also bind the dedicated Paste key (`Qt.Key_Paste`, what a Sun-style keyboard or `XF86Paste` sends). Neither reached a binding, so `Keymap.match` returned no action, `Panel.routeKey` returned `false`, and the field's own native, unbounded paste ran - the same class of read the whole entry above exists to close off. Second, and lower severity but the same root cause: since `field.paste` and `compose.attach` are ordinary, rebindable entries in `Keymap.BINDINGS`, a `keys.conf` override moving either off Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert entirely would let even those two keys fall through to the same native path - the binding lookup was the only thing standing between Qt's own reader and the keyboard.

**The fix stops relying on the binding lookup to recognise a paste key at all.** Every field that reaches `Panel.routeKey` now checks `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste) || event.key === Qt.Key_Paste` itself, in its own `Keys.onPressed`, before anything else - ahead of `root.pickerOpen`'s mention-picker check and the spell-picker check in the composer, ahead of the ordinary `root.routeKey(event.key, event.modifiers, event.text)` fall-through everywhere else. A match calls `root.routeKey(event.key, event.modifiers, event.text, true)` - the same function, with a new `nativePaste` parameter - and accepts the event unconditionally, so Qt's own control never sees it regardless of what (if anything) `Keymap.match` would have resolved the literal key to. `Panel.routeKey` answers `nativePaste` before it ever calls `Keymap.match`: `context === "compose"` runs `composerController.run("compose.attach")` (the same pasted-image-first path Ctrl+V already takes there), anything else runs `clipboardPaste.pasteInto(keyArea.Window.activeFocusItem)` directly - both skip the typing guard and the scheduled-message remap below, neither of which apply to a paste key, and neither depends on `root.bindings` at all, so a `keys.conf` override of `field.paste` or `compose.attach` changes which *key* reaches this path through the ordinary lookup, but can never stop the path itself from running for whatever key Qt itself treats as paste.

**Why this could not stay a `Keymap.js` binding change alone.** Qt decides `StandardKey.Paste` from the platform's own key-binding table (KDE's, GNOME's, or the generic X11/Wayland default), not from anything this project declares; adding Ctrl+Shift+Insert and `Key_Paste` as more `field.paste` keys would still miss whatever the next platform theme binds, and would still be the kind of binding `keys.conf` can move away. Reading the real `QKeyEvent`'s own `matches()` is the one check that stays correct across platform themes without this project maintaining its own copy of Qt's table, and checking it ahead of, rather than through, the binding lookup is what keeps `keys.conf` from ever being able to defeat it.

**Fields covered:** the same seven as the entry above - `CommandPalette.qml`, `ListColumn.qml`, `NewChatDialog.qml`, `RenameAccount.qml`, `AccountSetup.qml`'s three fields - plus, for the first time, the composer's own `TextArea` (`Composer.qml`), the one field the entry above's own `field.paste` binding never had to cover, since `compose.attach` already owned Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert there; `nativePaste` is what lets the same interception also cover Ctrl+Shift+Insert and `Key_Paste` for the composer.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/field-paste-guard.test.cjs` gained a check that every field's own block - the composer's included, no longer the documented exception for this one - contains both `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste)` and `Qt.Key_Paste`, calls `root.routeKey(..., true)`, and that the `StandardKey.Paste` check appears before the plain, bindings-based fallback call textually, confirmed to fail against a field with no interception at all. `tests/qml/FieldPaste` gained real Ctrl+Shift+Insert and `Qt.Key_Paste` key events into the palette and search fields, and a step that rebinds `field.paste` to a key nothing here presses (`Ctrl+Shift+Z`) before sending a plain Ctrl+V into the setup field - all three still reach `clipboard.readText`, confirmed to fail (for the right reason, in each case) against the pre-fix code. `tests/qml/Composer` gained a check that Ctrl+Shift+Insert and `Key_Paste` both reach `routeKey` with `nativePaste` set, ahead of the mention and spell pickers; the full round trip from there to `compose.attach`'s own `media.paste` request is `tests/qml/ComposerController`'s job, not re-proven here.

## A stale `media.fetch` answer could write an unchecked path back onto a message whose media kind had already changed

A security review found that an edit changing a message's media kind mid-download could let an unsanitised file reach a native image sink. `MediaController.downloadMedia` records the message id it is fetching for and, once `media.fetch` answers, writes the result's path and verdicts straight onto the row (`timeline.setMediaPath`, `setMediaDecodeAs`, `setMediaPlayable`, `setMediaRisky`) with nothing comparing what the row's media actually is by the time the answer arrives against what it was when the request started. A service's end-to-end encrypted edit can change a message's media kind entirely - WhatsApp's own `edit()` re-normalises the whole message, and the store's `UPDATE messages SET media=?` carries it straight through - and `ui/lib/Timeline.js`'s own `mergeRow` already clears a loaded row's cached `mediaPath` and verdicts the moment such an edit lands (`mediaIdentityChanged`), but nothing stopped a download that started *before* the edit from writing its own, now-stale answer straight back over that clearing once it finally came back. A 15 MB voice note - under the automatic-fetch cap - whose bytes are a hostile JPEG, immediately followed by an edit turning the same message into a photo before the download finished, left the voice note's own unchecked path sitting on what was now a photo row; `PhotoView` loads any non-empty `mediaPath` into a native `Image` with no further check of its own, trusting `media.fetch` to have already run `imagesafe` against the kind it was asked to fetch - which this stale answer never did for the kind now showing.

**The fix compares the message's media identity before the request and after the answer, not just once.** `ui/lib/Timeline.js` gained `mediaSignature` (a comparable snapshot of a media object's own `MEDIA_IDENTITY_KEYS` fields, or `null` for no media) and `mediaSignaturesMatch`; `mediaIdentityChanged` is rewritten in terms of both, rather than duplicating the same field list a second way. `MediaController.downloadMedia` takes `Timeline.mediaSignature(timeline.media(id))` before it ever calls `media.fetch`, and the answer runs through a new `_answerIsStale` check before anything is written back: when the row is still loaded and its current media signature no longer matches the one recorded at the start, the answer is for media nobody asked `media.fetch` to check against the row's *current* kind, and is dropped outright - no waiter's `done` callback runs, this controller's own cached risky verdict for the id is forgotten (`delete root._riskyVerdicts[id]`), and a fresh, silent, automatic fetch starts for whatever the row's media now actually is, the same fetch a newly shown row's own prefetch would ask for on its own.

**A row that is simply not loaded at all is not the same case, and must not be treated as stale.** `checkRiskyVerdictSurvivesTheListBeingCleared` (already covering a different, legitimate scenario - the conversation list cleared, or another conversation opened, while a download is still in flight) expects the helper's own risky verdict to still land in `_riskyVerdicts`, and a deliberate open still to ask before opening, even though the row itself is gone by the time the answer arrives: the verdict belongs with the download, not with a row that may or may not come back. `timeline.media(id)` reads `null` for an unloaded row, and `_answerIsStale` treats that - no current media to compare against at all - as "not stale", running the normal success path unchanged; `timeline.setMediaPath` and the other setters are themselves safe no-ops once `_index[id]` is gone, so the only real effect in that case remains recording the risky verdict and answering the waiters, exactly as before this fix. Only an actual mismatch on a row that is still loaded - the one shape the exploit above needs - is ever treated as stale.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/timeline.test.cjs` gained direct coverage of `mediaSignature` (`null` for no media, every `MEDIA_IDENTITY_KEYS` field read with a missing one defaulting to `null`) and `mediaSignaturesMatch` (both `null`, one `null`, the same attachment, a different kind, different bytes of the same kind), alongside the existing `mediaIdentityChanged` cases, now proven to still hold after the rewrite. `tests/qml/MediaController` gained `checkStaleMediaFetchNeverWritesBackAfterAnEditChangesKind`, reproducing the exploit directly: a voice note's `media.fetch` still in flight, an edit landing mid-flight that turns it into a photo, the stale answer for the voice kind arriving and being confirmed never to set a path or verdict on the now-photo row, a second, fresh fetch confirmed to have started on its own, and that fetch's own answer confirmed to land once it comes back - run against the pre-fix code first and confirmed to fail for exactly the reason described above.

## Account setup's and rename's own fields were gated per overlay, not per step - and WhatsApp can open the setup overlay unasked

A security review (both the regression pass and an independent sharp-edges pass over the same commit) found `AccountSetup`'s three fields - `apiIdField`, `apiHashField` (the credentials step) and `answerField` (the phone, code and password steps) - and `RenameAccount`'s `aliasField` were each only ever hidden per step with `visible:`, inside an overlay `Overlays.qml` gates as a whole with a Loader on `accountController.open`/`renaming`. Hiding a field does not destroy it: on supported Qt versions the underlying `TextInput` still reads every foreign Wayland clipboard offer, with no size or time limit, for as long as the whole overlay stays open - every step but the one the field belongs to. `docs/decisions.md` already treats "a field that is alive but not being typed into is still a live sink" as settled (see "The search field and the composer's input exist only while actually being edited" above); these two dialogs were never brought up to that standard, because the whole-overlay Loader looked, at a glance, like the same fix.

**This is reachable with no user action at all.** `backend/internal/connector/whatsapp/connector.go`'s `connectOrPair` starts pairing and sends unsolicited `auth.step` QR events whenever a session is not paired - logged out remotely, or the Manager restarting after a stopped status - and `AccountController._showStep` opens setup and jumps to the `qr` stage for any `auth.step` if setup is not already open. So WhatsApp's own connector, not the user, can put all three `AccountSetup` fields on screen, hidden, for as long as the QR keeps refreshing and the window stays open - the exact "untrusted service as attacker" shape `THREAT_MODEL.md` already names.

**The fix gives each field its own Loader, active only for the exact step that shows it, inside the component itself - not a bigger Loader around the whole dialog.** `AccountSetup`'s `credentialsLoader` is active only while `stage === "credentials"` and loads a small `ColumnLayout` holding `idField`/`hashField` (exposed back out as `idField`/`hashField` aliases); `answerLoader` is active only while `root._field !== null` (the phone, code and password steps) and loads `answerField` directly. `RenameAccount`'s `aliasLoader` is active only while `root.open && root.stage === "alias"`. Each field keeps every paste guard it had before (`maximumLength`, `ContextMenu.menu: null`, `MiddleClickGuard`, the `StandardKey.Paste`/`Qt.Key_Paste` interception ahead of `routeKey`) unchanged, just moved inside the Loader's `sourceComponent`. Nothing is lost switching steps: `root.submit()` and `root._focusStep()` read the field's current value through `root._credentials`/`root._answer`/`root._alias` (each a plain `property var` pointed at the matching Loader's own `item` - `var`, not an alias, for the same reason `Composer.qml`'s own `input` property is `var`: `Loader.item` has no declared type for qmllint to check members against), and every caller that used to reach the field by its old top-level id now goes through that Loader's item instead, guarded against it being `null` for the instant before a freshly active Loader finishes loading.

**`tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs` gained the same two checks it already applies to `ListColumn`'s and `Composer`'s own fields.** `AccountSetup.qml` and `RenameAccount.qml` were added to the file's self-gated list, so `fieldSelfGated` now requires every field in each of those two files to sit inside its own Loader rather than letting the caller-side `Overlays.qml` Loader stand in for it - confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code, which had no inner Loader at all. A second new check reads each field's own Loader's `active:` binding directly and requires it to name the real step condition (`stage === "credentials"`, `root._field !== null`, `stage === "alias"`), the same way the existing `EDIT_INTENT_FLAGS` check already holds the search field and the composer to their own edit-intent flag rather than accepting any non-literal expression: a field moved back to gating on the whole overlay being open, rather than its own step, would still read as "not a bare `true`" to `fieldSelfGated` alone, so this is what actually catches that regression.

**`tests/qml/OverlayLifecycle`'s own baseline was itself asserting the bug.** Its `checkAccountSetupGated` expected `apiIdField`, `apiHashField` *and* `answerField` to all exist together once account setup opens on the credentials step - true before this fix, and wrong: `answerField` belongs to the phone/code/password steps, not credentials, and existing at all then was exactly the gap being closed. It now expects only the credentials step's own two fields. `tests/qml/AccountSetup` and `tests/qml/RenameAccount` each gained a check that walks every step (chooseService, waiting, qr and credentials for setup; choose for rename) and asserts no field exists at all outside the one step that asks for it - not merely that it is invisible - plus null-safe lookups (`Check.find(...)?.activeFocus ?? false`) wherever a step without a field used to assume one would always be found.

## A reply quote's own Text, like its SenderName, is re-capped on every read, not only when it is first written

A security review found `SanitizeIncoming` capped a reply quote's `SenderName` on every read (see "A reply quote could be relabelled by a rename that happened in a different conversation" above) but never its `Text`, even though `Text` is only ever shortened to `domain.ExcerptLength` (80 characters) once, at write time, in `store.resolveReply` - and `resolveReply` skips shortening entirely whenever the incoming reply already carries a sender id, a sender name and text all filled in. A row an earlier release stored before that write-time cap existed, or one a future connector writes with every reply field already resolved, reaches `ReplyQuote.qml` at whatever length it was actually stored, where it is laid out as one elided `Text` - the same unbounded-layout shape every other cap in `limits.go` exists to rule out.

**The fix adds one line to the same `if m.ReplyTo != nil` block `SenderName`'s own cap already lives in:** `m.ReplyTo.Text = Excerpt(m.ReplyTo.Text)`, reusing the exact function and the exact 80-character bound a reply quote already gets at write time, so a row sanitized on read never looks any different from one the current write path produced.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/domain/limits_replyquote_test.go` (`TestSanitizeIncoming_RecapsReplyQuoteText`) confirms `SanitizeIncoming` cuts an oversized `ReplyTo.Text` to `Excerpt`'s own output, run against the pre-fix code first and confirmed to fail. `backend/internal/store/messages_replyquote_test.go` (`TestMessage_RecapsAStoredOversizedReplyQuoteText`) plants a row the same shape `resolveReply`'s own "keeps a reply's own sender and text when already given" test uses - a reply with sender id, sender name and text all supplied, so `resolveReply` stores `Text` at full length - and confirms a later `Message` read comes back capped, the same defence `TestMessage_CapsAStoredStickerEmoji` already proves for a legacy sticker emoji.

## A search snippet from a single long word could still carry a whole legacy message, uncapped

A differential security review comparing this range against the approved release found `scanConversationMatch` (`store/conversations.go`) capped `MatchSender` on read but never `Match`, the search snippet `conversations.list` sends to the command palette's "Messages" section. `conversationsByTitleOrMessage`'s own `snippet(messages_fts, 0, '', '', '…', 32)` already bounds a normal match to 32 tokens around the hit, but FTS5 never splits a token: a message stored as one long run of letters with no spaces comes back as that one token in full, however large it is. The approved release stored incoming text with no length cap at all (`domain.MaxIncomingTextLength` only arrived in this range, and only bounds what a connector stores going forward), so a sender who had ever sent a single-word message of, say, several megabytes left a row that still returns in full on every search for a short prefix of that word, once per chat it appears in, on every keystroke.

**The fix applies the same cap `Preview` already gets.** `scanConversationWith` already runs `domain.CapPreview` over `Preview` on every read, repairing a legacy row the same way `CapDisplayName` repairs a legacy `Title` or `PreviewSender`; `scanConversationMatch` now wraps `Match` in the same call, so a snippet is always bounded, whatever shape the underlying message's text has and however old the row is.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/store/conversations_search_test.go`'s new `TestConversations_CapsAnOversizedSingleWordMatch` stores a message whose text is one word followed by `domain.MaxPreviewLength*2` more letters with no spaces, searches for the word's own prefix, and confirms `Match` comes back at or under `domain.MaxPreviewLength` UTF-16 units while still starting with the matched word - run against the pre-fix code first and confirmed to fail with a 606-unit snippet. The existing `TestConversations_BoundsAMatchedMessagesSnippet`, which only ever used words separated by spaces, already passed before this fix and still does: it covers the ordinary 32-token window, not the single-token gap this entry closes.

## MP3 is never played in-window at all, not even the narrow tag-free case

A differential security review found that `validateMP3`'s own frame-chain walk could only ever confirm `mp3FramesToConfirm` (three) consecutive frame headers, each exactly where the previous one's own declared bitrate and sample rate predicted - the only thing a constant-bitrate stream's own header fields can confirm without walking the file's entire, variable-length frame chain (see the package doc comment and "An MP3 only has to look valid for its first three frames" below, among the gaps this entry replaces). Nothing checked that the rest of the file kept the same shape. Qt's FFmpeg backend does not stop at three frames: `mp3_read_probe` scores a file by how well its *whole* length matches MP3 framing, and a file crafted as three valid MP3 frames followed by, say, an MPEG-TS stream carrying H.264 or HEVC scores low for MP3 and high for MPEG-TS, so FFmpeg demuxes and decodes the rest as video instead - entirely bypassing `videosafe`, which never saw this file at all, since nothing routes an audio message through it.

**Chasing this is the same open-ended problem the ID3v2 frame walker already lost once.** The "MP3: ID3v2 frames are now walked, not skipped" and "audiosafe's ID3v2 frame walker and FFmpeg's own demuxer parsed a v2.4 frame's own size differently" entries above both describe this package's own parser and FFmpeg's drifting out of agreement about the same bytes, each time closed by narrowing what an MP3 is allowed to carry rather than by matching FFmpeg's parsing more closely. A frame-chain walker that only ever confirms a short prefix of the file is the same shape of gap one level up: matching FFmpeg's own whole-file probing closely enough to rule out every way the two could still disagree is not a one-time fix, it is an open-ended chase with no way to prove it is finished.

**The fix taken instead: MP3 (and the related MP2/MP1 shapes the same frame header's version and layer bits describe) is never reported playable, full stop.** `audiosafe.Validate`'s `default` case - reached by anything that is neither a `OggS` Ogg file nor an ISO BMFF box - now returns `false, nil` directly, with no frame-chain walk of any kind. The whole former MP3 walker (`mp3.go`: `validateMP3`, `mp3HasForbiddenTag`, `confirmMP3Frames`, `mp3FrameHeader`, and `limits.go`'s MP3-only tables and constants) is deleted; nothing else in this package used it. Such a file still opens fine in the user's own player, the same fallback every other unsupported codec or container already takes - a shared MP3 or song simply never played in-window to begin with once the tag-free case became this narrow (see "Most real MP3s will now be refused in-window" below), and voice notes, the case this package actually needs to keep working, are Opus (inside Ogg or M4A) on both WhatsApp and Telegram, never MP3.

**The notification-sound picker's filters and refusal message follow the same change.** `app.soundFilters` (`filepick.go`) drops `*.mp3` from the glob it offers the "sound" purpose's file dialog, since audiosafe now refuses every MP3 outright and offering the extension would only invite a pick that always fails. `errSoundNotPlayable`'s own fixed sentence (`notificationsound.go`) now names the formats that do work ("try an Ogg/Opus or M4A file") rather than just saying no, the same "fixed sentence, never an echo of the file" rule it already followed, so the refusal the UI surfaces through `Rpc.errorText` is actionable rather than a dead end.

**Tests.** `mp3_test.go`'s `TestValidate_MP3IsNeverPlayable` replaces every former MP3 table test with one covering a well-formed constant-bitrate stream with no tag at all, one with a trailing ID3v1 tag, one with a leading ID3v2 tag, and plain bytes that only look like the start of one - all refused now, where the first two used to validate as playable (run against the pre-fix code first and confirmed to fail for exactly that reason). `FuzzValidateMP3` keeps seeding MP3-shaped bytes, now checking only that `Validate` never panics or errors on input that merely starts with a frame sync or a legacy tag marker, matching the no-panic-only contract `FuzzValidateOgg` and `FuzzValidateMP4Audio` already use. Confirmed against real files, built into `build/` and deleted immediately after: an `ffmpeg`-encoded MP3 is refused, and an `ffmpeg`-encoded Opus (M4A) and Ogg/Opus file both still validate as playable.

## Every message's media view was built for every message, hidden rather than absent - a never-shown thumbnail decode could freeze the shell

A security review found `MessageBubbleContent` built all seven of a message's media views - `LinkPreview`, `PhotoView`, `InlineVideo`, `FileView`, `StickerView`, `PollView`, `VoiceNotePlayer` - for every message, each handed `root.media` regardless of the message's own kind, and told apart only by a `visible: root._mediaKind === "..."` binding. Hiding a view does not stop it loading: a Qt `Image` decodes its `source` whether or not it, or its parent, is visible, so a plain photo message still built a `LinkPreview` carrying its own `Image`, bound to the same media. A batch of photo messages, each carrying a thumbnail within every existing limit (1024 px side, 1 MP, the 100-scan progressive-JPEG cap, the 64 KiB inline-thumb cap), still cost a full progressive decode per bubble at roughly 100 scans over 49,000 blocks - and every one of those decodes ran synchronously on the shell's own UI thread, since `LinkPreview`'s `Image` was the one `Image` in the bubble with no `asynchronous: true`, unlike `PhotoView`, `InlineVideo`, `StickerView` and `PhotoViewer`. Opening a chat full of such photos froze the whole desktop for an extended stretch, and the freeze repeated on every scroll that rebuilt a bubble, and again after a restart, since the thumbnails are stored. `InlineVideo`'s own thumbnail decode had the same always-built shape (bounded and already asynchronous, but still a needless decode of a photo or sticker's thumb on every such message) - the file's own stated rule, that media must never reach "an invisible sibling" view, had only ever been applied to `path`, never to `media` itself.

**The fix stops building the view at all, rather than merely hiding it.** Each of the seven views now sits inside its own `Loader`, `active` only while `root._mediaKind` matches that view's own kind, with the view itself declared as the Loader's `sourceComponent` rather than a direct child. An inactive `Loader` holds no item: nothing is bound, nothing decodes, and `visible: active` keeps an inactive Loader out of the `Column`'s own layout the same way the old `visible: false` did. `LinkPreview`'s own `Image` also gained the `asynchronous: true` every other received-content `Image` already had, so even the one view that does match now decodes off the UI thread.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/async-media-decode.test.cjs` scans `ui/` for an `Image` showing received content (a `data:image` or `file://` source, excluding a locally generated QR code) with no `asynchronous: true`, for an `AnimatedImage` with no `playing` gate (the one defence available for a type with no `asynchronous` property at all), and scans `MessageBubbleContent.qml` specifically for one of the seven media view types declared with no `Loader` among its ancestors - each confirmed to fail against the pre-fix source first. `tests/qml/MessageDelegate`'s `checkPhoto` gained a check that a photo message builds no `stickerView`, `inlineVideo` or `linkPreview` at all, and `checkLinkPreview` now expects a message without a link preview to leave no `linkPreview` view behind rather than one left existing but invisible. `tests/qml/VoiceNoteRouting`'s `checkDelegate` was rewritten the same way: it now asserts the two views that do not match a message's kind were never built, rather than asserting a shared `photoView`'s `path` stayed empty while hidden - that whole class of question no longer applies once the non-matching views do not exist to ask it of.

## `videosafe`'s avcC reader never checked configurationVersion, cross-checked its own profile/level bytes against the SPS, or refused unparsed trailing bytes

A security review found `readAVCDecoderConfig` (`backend/internal/videosafe/h264sps.go`) read only `hdr[5]` (the declared SPS count) and `hdr[4]` (the NAL length-prefix width) out of avcC's own six-byte header, and otherwise trusted the box completely once its one declared SPS parsed and bound cleanly. Four separate gaps followed from that: `hdr[0]` (`configurationVersion`) was never checked, even though FFmpeg's own `ff_h264_decode_extradata` only treats avcC as avcC at all when this byte is 1 - for any other value it falls back to reading the whole box, and every sample, as an Annex B start-code stream, so a `0` here alone defeats every other bound this package places on avcC's own SPS. `hdr[1:4]` (`AVCProfileIndication`/`profile_compatibility`/`AVCLevelIndication`) were read into the decoder config but never compared against the SPS's own copy of the same three fields, even though both copies exist in the same box. `hdr[4]`'s low two bits (`lengthSizeMinusOne`) accepted value 2 (a 3-byte length prefix), the one value ISO/IEC 14496-15 never defines. And nothing stopped reading once the one declared SPS was parsed: avcC's own `numOfPictureParameterSets` field and list, and anything after it, were never walked at all, so a sender could leave a small, container-matching SPS as avcC's first declared one and hide a second, unbounded SPS in the exact trailing bytes FFmpeg's own avcC-mode decoder also reads from the same box - the same decode-bomb shape every other bound in this package exists to close off, reopened in a part of avcC nothing here had ever looked at.

**The fix adds every one of these as an explicit precondition `readAVCDecoderConfig` must pass before accepting avcC at all**, rather than trusting any byte in the box this package had not itself derived from the SPS or walked structurally: `configurationVersion` must be exactly 1; `lengthSizeMinusOne` must be 0, 1 or 3 (`avcCProfileBytesMatchSPS` and the length check both run before the SPS is even parsed); avcC's own profile/compatibility/level bytes must equal the same three bytes read straight off the SPS's own emulation-prevention-stripped bytes, byte-aligned since nothing before them in the bitstream is variable length; and `avcCTrailingDataAllowed` walks avcC's own `numOfPictureParameterSets` field and that many length-prefixed PPS entries - never itself judging a PPS found this way, the same way this package never trusted avcC's old SPS list for anything beyond finding where it ends - then refuses outright if any byte remains, unless `profileIDC` is one of the Annex A profiles `highProfileChromaFields` already names (the same list `parseSPSFields` uses to decide whether the SPS itself carries these fields) and what remains is exactly that profile's own four-byte chroma/bit-depth extension, with chroma_format 1, both bit depths 0 and a zero SPS-extension count - the one shape `parseSPSFields` already requires the SPS's own copy of these same fields to hold, so a genuine encoder's avcC can never disagree with its own SPS here.

**Why the Annex A profile list, not ISO 14496-15's own narrower one.** The avcC specification's own chroma-extension condition names profiles 100, 110, 122 and 144 (widely understood as a typo for 244, mirroring the SPS-side Annex A list this package already uses for the same purpose); rather than maintain a second, slightly different profile list whose only use is deciding when to expect four more bytes, `avcCTrailingDataAllowed` reuses `highProfileChromaFields` outright, so the one list stays the single source of truth for which profiles this package ever expects the extension fields from, on either side of avcC.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/videosafe/avccconfig_test.go`'s `TestValidate_MP4AVCCStructuralChecks` covers every new bound through the public `Validate` API, each built by mutating one byte of an otherwise valid, container-matching avcC: `configurationVersion` 0, a disagreeing profile byte, a disagreeing level byte, `lengthSizeMinusOne` 2, and unparsed bytes appended past an otherwise complete, zero-PPS avcC - each confirmed to fail for the expected reason against the pre-fix code first. `backend/internal/videosafe/avccconfig_internal_test.go`'s `TestReadAVCDecoderConfig_TrailingData` is an internal test for the same reason the file's other avcC-level tests are: reaching the high-profile extension's own byte layout needs a container-level high-profile SPS builder this package has no other use for. It covers avcC's own PPS list (none, one, and one whose declared length runs past avcC's own length) and the chroma extension (accepted when it matches the SPS exactly, refused when its chroma_format or SPS-extension count disagree, and refused when a high-profile avcC carries trailing bytes that are not the extension at all) - the "correct chroma extension" case is a positive control proving the fix does not reject a genuine high-profile encode, not only that it rejects a crafted one. `FuzzValidateMP4` gained a seed for `configurationVersion` 0 and one for unparsed trailing bytes past an otherwise valid avcC. `backend/internal/videosafe/helpers_test.go`'s `buildAVCDecoderConfig` (the one builder every other avcC-producing fixture in this package already goes through) was changed to derive its profile/compatibility/level bytes from the SPS it builds, rather than a value fixed independently of it, and to always append the now-mandatory zero `numOfPictureParameterSets` byte - without this, every existing "valid" fixture in the package would have started failing the new checks it was never built to satisfy. `avccconfig.go` itself is a new file, split out of `h264sps.go` (which keeps only pure SPS bitstream parsing) once avcC's own box-level reading grew enough new checks to need its own home; `bitreader.go` is the generic Exp-Golomb bit reader both files share, split out the same way, once the package's own file-length limit needed the room.

**Real files.** `ffmpeg`-encoded H.264 was built into `build/` and checked against `videosafe.Validate` directly, then deleted: 720x1280 and 1080x1920 (level 4.2) High profile, 1920x1080 High profile with 4 reference frames, 640x360 Baseline and 848x480 Main - all five still validate as playable, confirming the new profile/level cross-check and trailing-data bound do not reject an ordinary phone-style encode, High profile's own chroma extension included.

## A sample shaped like an avcC record could replace avcC's own trusted SPS and PPS

A security review found that `avcsamples.go`'s in-band NAL scan, added to close a previous gap (see "A second SPS inside an avc1 sample's own compressed data..." above), still read every sample strictly as this package's own length-prefixed NAL stream and never considered that FFmpeg's H.264 decoder reads the exact same raw sample bytes a second way before it ever gets that far. FFmpeg's `h264_decode_frame` takes an "avcC in packet" path - `is_avc && buf[0]==1 && buf[2]==0 && (buf[4]&0xFC)==0xFC && is_avcc_extradata(buf)` - that, when it matches, hands the whole sample to `ff_h264_decode_extradata`, replacing the decoder's trusted SPS and PPS sets with whatever the sample itself declares, regardless of the track's own avcC. The validator made this easy to reach: `avcNALAllowed` judged only NAL types 7, 8, 13 and 15, leaving type 0 (and the reserved types 24-31) to its `default: return true` case, with the NAL payload itself never read. A track whose avcC declares a 2-byte length prefix lets a sample starting `01 PP 00 …` read, under this package's own length-prefix parsing, as a single ordinary NAL of length `0x01PP` and type 0 - "unjudged, pass" - while the exact same bytes, read as FFmpeg reads them before applying any length prefix at all, are a structurally valid `AVCDecoderConfigurationRecord`: `configurationVersion` 1, a zero `profile_compatibility` byte (the very byte this package's own parsing had just read as the NAL length's low byte), reserved length-size bits all set, and SPS/PPS count and length fields that stay consistent within the sample. One sample built this way, anywhere in the track, replaces the trusted SPS a later slice NAL could then redirect the decoder to decode at - the same memory-exhaustion shape the avcC check and the first in-band-SPS fix both exist to close off, reopened through a reading of the bytes this package never considered.

**The fix adds two independent defences, since either alone could miss a shape the other catches.** `scanAVCSample` now calls `sampleShapedLikeAVCDecoderConfig` on a sample's full raw bytes before applying this package's own length-prefix parsing at all, and refuses the sample outright if it matches: the same four-part structural check FFmpeg's own heuristic applies (`buf[0]==1`, `buf[2]==0`, `buf[4]&0xFC==0xFC`, then a consistent walk of a one-byte, 5-bit-masked SPS count and a one-byte, unmasked PPS count, each followed by that many 2-byte-length-prefixed entries that stay within the sample). Independently, `avcNALAllowed` now refuses NAL type 0 and every reserved type (24-31) outright, rather than leaving them to the same `default: return true` every ordinary, judged-safe slice type also falls through to - closing the gap even for a length-prefix width (such as the previously-untested 1-byte case) where the "shaped like avcC" check's own fixed byte positions do not line up the same way.

**Why both, rather than picking the simpler one.** For the specific 2-byte-length-prefix shape the review demonstrated, refusing NAL type 0 alone is already sufficient - FFmpeg's own `buf[2]==0` condition forces the byte this package reads as that NAL's header to be exactly 0, which is always type 0 under a 2-byte prefix. But that is a property of this one length-prefix width, not of the exploit in general: nothing rules out a crafted sample under a different length-prefix width presenting as some other, still-"unjudged" NAL type while the same raw bytes still satisfy FFmpeg's own structural check. Reading the sample once as FFmpeg itself would, ahead of and independent of this package's own length-prefix interpretation, is the only check that does not depend on which NAL type the bytes happen to decode to under this package's own parsing.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/videosafe/avccshapedsample_test.go`'s `TestValidate_MP4InBandAVCCShapedSample` reproduces the review's own mechanism directly, in its own file since the shape it builds is as much about avcC's own header bytes as the sample itself: a track declaring a 2-byte length prefix (`buildAVCDecoderConfigWithLengthSize`, a `helpers_test.go` builder), carrying one sample built byte for byte as the dual-reading shape described above, padded to the length its own (misread) NAL length field declares - confirmed to validate as playable against the pre-fix code first, and refused after the fix. `TestValidate_MP4InBandParameterSets` gained "in-band NAL type 0" and "in-band NAL type 24 (reserved)" cases, each a plain, single-length-prefix sample isolating `avcNALAllowed`'s own new refusal from the structural check above. `FuzzValidateMP4InBandSamples` gained seeds for all three shapes. `mp4AVCVideoWithSamples` was refactored onto a new, more general `mp4AVCVideoFixtureWithAVCC` (avcC's own body given directly, rather than always derived from width/height) so the shaped-sample test and `buildAVCDecoderConfigWithLengthSize` could build a track with a non-default length-prefix width without duplicating the rest of the fixture's box layout; `avcsamples_test.go`'s own sample-*layout* cases (as opposed to NAL-content cases) moved to a new `avcsampleslayout_test.go` once this package's own file-length limit needed the room.

## `videosafe` and `audiosafe` judged only moov's trak children, never moov itself - a compressed moov (cmov) skipped every check this package runs

A differential security review found `isoBMFFPlayable` (`videosafe/isobmff.go`) and `validateISOBMFFAudio` (`audiosafe/isobmff.go`) both collected only `moov`'s `trak` children (`c.children(moov.off, moov.len, "trak")`) and never looked at, or judged, any other box moov might carry. Nothing in either package rejected or even recognized a `cmov` ("compressed movie") box: a QuickTime-era box that holds a `dcom` compression type and a `cmvd` payload which, once inflated, is itself a second, ordinary `moov` body - `mvhd`, `trak`, the works. FFmpeg's own mov demuxer (`mov_read_cmov`, compiled in whenever it is built with zlib, as Arch's is) allocates the uncompressed size the file itself declares, inflates the zlib data into it, and parses the result as a second `moov`, so every `trak` inside becomes an extra stream with any codec and any dimensions this package never saw. A file whose outer `moov` carried one small, perfectly valid `avc1` track - passing every check this package runs - plus a sibling `cmov` box describing a few megabytes of compressed data that inflates to roughly 2 GB of zeros (memory exhaustion that can take the desktop down) and an inner track at an unvetted codec and size, was reported playable: the walk that collected `trak` children never looked at `cmov` at all, so there was nothing to refuse.

**The fix switches from "collect what I want and ignore the rest" to an explicit allowlist, applied at every level this package already reads into.** `onlyChildrenAllowed` (new in both packages' own `isobmff.go`) walks every immediate child of a container and refuses the whole file the moment one is not in the given set - the same "cannot read it, cannot trust it" rule `children` and `firstChild` already apply to a malformed box, extended to a box this package simply does not expect to find there. It now runs at the top level (`topLevelMoov`, folding in the existing `moof`/`mfra` fragmented-MP4 refusal), on `moov`'s own children, and - new in this fix - on `trak`, `mdia`, `minf` and `stbl` too, each gated behind finding the box it is checking in the first place (`videosafe`'s new `trackChildBoxes` and `audiosafe`'s new `soundCodec`, both split out of their own `trackInfo`/`audioTrackInfo` to keep the branching inside this package's own complexity limit). `audiosafe` only checks `minf`/`stbl` once a track's own handler type is already known to be `"soun"`, exactly mirroring the shape its own `audioTrackInfo` already had: a track of any other kind is refused by `isoAudioTrackAllowed` regardless of what either box holds, so checking them earlier would only add a second way to reject the same file for a different reason.

**What each allowlist actually names, and why.** Chosen from what FFmpeg's `mov.c` default parse table treats as a structural no-op or a box genuine encoders are known to write, cross-checked against `ffmpeg`-encoded H.264/AAC MP4s (plain, `+faststart`, and combined audio+video) and an AAC M4A (see "Real files" below): top level - `ftyp`, `moov`, `mdat`, `free`, `skip`, `wide`, `uuid`, and `meta` (an iTunes-style cover art atom can sit bare at the top level too, see `hasCoverArtAnywhere`); `moov` - `mvhd`, `trak`, `udta`, `meta`, `iods` (an MPEG-4 Initial Object Descriptor some encoders still write), `free`, `skip`; `trak` - `tkhd`, `mdia`, `edts` (an edit list - AAC's own encoder-delay priming writes one on every file `ffmpeg` produced for this fix), `udta`, `meta`; `mdia` - `mdhd`, `hdlr`, `minf`; `minf` - `vmhd`/`smhd`, `dinf`, `stbl`; `stbl` - `stsd`, `stts`, `stsc`, `stsz`/`stz2`, `stco`/`co64`, `stss`, `ctts`, `sgpd`, `sbgp`. `cmov` and `mvex` (a fragmented MP4's own sample tables, carried inside `moov` rather than at the top level) are deliberately absent from the `moov` list, the same as every box none of these lists name: refused the same way as any other unrecognized child, not called out as a special case, since the point is a real allowlist rather than a list of the one or two named exploits.

**Tests.** `videosafe/isobmff_allowlist_test.go` (new, split out once `isobmff_test.go` needed the room) covers a `cmov` and an `mvex` box alongside an otherwise valid track at the `moov` level, an unrelated unrecognized box at the same level (to prove this is a real allowlist, not a two-item denylist), and an unrecognized box as a direct child of `trak`, `mdia`, `minf` and `stbl` in turn - every case built and confirmed to still validate as playable against the pre-fix code first, then refused after the fix. `audiosafe/isobmff_test.go` gained the matching cases for the one-audio-track file this package accepts. Both packages' `FuzzValidateMP4`/`FuzzValidateMP4Audio` (and, for `videosafe`, a new `FuzzValidateMP4Allowlist`) gained seeds for the `cmov` and nested-unknown-box shapes.

**Real files.** `ffmpeg`-encoded H.264 MP4s (plain, `-movflags +faststart`, and combined H.264+AAC) and an AAC M4A were built into `build/` and checked against `Validate` directly, then deleted: all four still validate as playable, confirming the new allowlists do not reject an ordinary phone- or `ffmpeg`-style encode. Dumping each file's own box tree directly is what fixed the final allowlists above: `edts`, `ctts`, `stss`, `sgpd` and `sbgp` all turned up in real encoder output that an earlier, narrower guess at the allowlist would have refused.

## A dependency could print to stdout, injecting a non-JSON-RPC line into the protocol stream

A security review found that `go.mau.fi/libsignal` (an indirect dependency of the WhatsApp connector, through `whatsmeow`) falls back to its own built-in default logger whenever nothing configures one, and that default logger's `Warning` and `Error` levels write with a plain `fmt.Println` - straight to the process's own stdout, the exact stream `protocol.md` reserves for JSON-RPC traffic alone. Nothing in `backend/` called `logger.Setup` to replace it. The trigger is any malformed or undecryptable WhatsApp message, real or from a modified client, reaching `SessionCipher`'s or `ProtoBufferSerializer`'s own error-logging calls; the review judged this low severity in practice (no key material is logged, the error text carries no message content or JIDs, and `ui/lib/Rpc.js` already turns a non-JSON line into a dropped `invalid` message rather than crashing), but a stray print reaching stdout is still a structural gap protocol.md's own rule exists to close off, and nothing stops some other dependency, now or later, from doing the same thing less harmlessly.

**The fix makes the real stdout file descriptor physically unreachable by anything but the protocol writer, rather than only silencing the one dependency found.** `claimProtocolStdout` (new, `backend/stdout.go`) runs first thing in `exitCode`, before `store.Open`, the connectors or anything else initializes: it duplicates the file descriptor `os.Stdout` currently names (`syscall.Dup`) for the protocol server's own exclusive use, then redirects that same descriptor, and the `os.Stdout` package variable every dependency shares, to `os.Stderr` (`syscall.Dup2`). After this runs, `fmt.Println` (which always writes through the `os.Stdout` variable) and a direct write through the original descriptor number both land on stderr instead; only the one `*os.File` the function returns still reaches the original stream, and that is the one `exitCode` hands to `server.New` as the protocol's own output. `quietLibsignalLogger` additionally calls `go.mau.fi/libsignal`'s own `logger.Setup` with a small adapter (`libsignalLogger`) that drops Debug and Info outright and writes Warning and Error to stderr with their own caller and message text - never message content, a session key or a JID - closing the one dependency the review found by name as well, and incidentally closing a second, unrelated gap: that library's own lazy default-logger initialization (`ensureLogger`) has no synchronization, which `backend/internal/connector/whatsapp/device_test.go` already works around by not running its own device tests in parallel; configuring the logger up front before any goroutine can reach that lazy path removes the race rather than merely avoiding it in one test file.

**Tests.** `backend/stdout_test.go`'s `TestClaimProtocolStdout_RedirectsStrayWritesAwayFromTheOriginal` builds two `os.Pipe`s - one standing in for the real stdout, one for the redirect target - calls `claimProtocolStdout` on the first pipe's own write-end descriptor, then writes through the returned file, through `fmt.Println`, and through the original write-end file directly, confirming only the first write reaches the original pipe and both of the other two land on the redirect pipe instead. `TestClaimProtocolStdout_ReportsAnInvalidDescriptor` covers the one error path. `TestLibsignalLogger_DropsDebugAndInfo` and `TestLibsignalLogger_WritesWarningAndError` cover the adapter directly; `TestQuietLibsignalLogger_ConfiguresThePackageLogger` confirms it actually installs itself as `go.mau.fi/libsignal`'s own shared logger, not only that the right value would do so if installed.

## `videosafe` and `audiosafe` read a track's own sample count but never checked it, or any of its other sample tables, against anything at all

A security review found that `isoBMFFPlayable` enforced `maxSampleCount` on a video track's own declared sample count (`isoVideoTrackAllowed`) but never on an audio track's - `trackInfo` read it (`sampleCount`, `isobmff.go`) and then simply discarded the result for a `"soun"` track. Worse, neither package ever read stts, ctts, stss, sgpd or sbgp at all, and no trak's own edit list (`edts`/`elst`) was ever checked. FFmpeg's own mov demuxer sizes real allocations from every one of these: `mov_build_index` allocates proportional to the *total sample count implied by stts's own per-entry sums*, not stsz's declared count, so a file could declare a small, capped stsz sample count while a single stts entry still claimed tens of millions of samples - exactly the review's own exploit: a ~1 KB voice note with `stsz` sample_size=1, sample_count=85,000,000 and a matching single `stts` entry, which `AudioPlayableForMedia` reported playable and `VoiceNoteRuntime`'s `MediaPlayer` then opened, allocating roughly 2 GiB inside `omarchy-shell`. The same shape reaches `videosafe` too, since an audio track riding inside an otherwise-valid H.264 video never had its own tables checked either, and a video track's own stts/ctts/stss/sgpd/sbgp were never checked regardless of its (already-capped) sample count.

**The fix gives every accepted track - audio and video alike - the same sample-table consistency check, in both packages.** `videosafe`'s existing avc1-only sample-layout code (`avcsamples.go`'s `sampleSpans`, built from `sampleSizeTable`/`chunkOffsetTable`/`sampleToChunkTable`) is now also run from a new `sampletables.go`, for every track regardless of codec, cross-checking stsz/stz2 against stco/co64 and stsc the same way the avc1 in-band scan already relied on. New in both packages: `sttsConsistent` and `cttsConsistent` sum every entry's own `sample_count` field (the exact field the review's exploit abuses) and require the total equal the track's own stsz-declared count; `stssConsistent` bounds stss's own entry count by the sample count and requires every named sample number to be in range and strictly increasing; `sbgpConsistent` sums its own run-length entries the same way stts does; `sgpdConsistent` walks version 1's own description list (fixed-length or, when `default_length` is 0, each description's own 4-byte length prefix) without ever reading a description's payload, bounded by `maxSampleGroupEntries`; and `editListAllowed` requires at most one `elst` entry (`maxELSTEntries`), with that one entry's own segment duration bounded by `maxDuration` - the "duration cap" every other table's own cap ties to `maxSampleCount` instead. `audiosafe` gained this whole layout/consistency layer from scratch (`sampletables.go`, `samplegrouptables.go`): it had none of `avcsamples.go`'s own table-reading code at all, since nothing there ever read past an audio track's own codec and sample count before. Every new cap (`maxSTTSEntries`, `maxCttsEntries`, `maxStssEntries`, `maxSbgpEntries`, `maxChunkCount`/`maxAVCChunkCount`, `maxStscEntries`/`maxAVCStscEntries`) is tied to the existing `maxSampleCount`, the same reasoning `maxAVCChunkCount` already used: none of these tables can legitimately need more rows than there are samples. `isoVideoTrackAllowed`'s own, now-redundant `sampleCount <= maxSampleCount` check was removed, since `trackInfo`/`audioTrackInfo` enforce it earlier, for every track kind, before either package's own kind-specific checks run at all.

**Tests.** `videosafe/sampletables_test.go` and `audiosafe/sampletables_test.go` each reproduce the review's own exploit shape directly (`TestValidate_MP4Audio(?:STTSMismatch|SampleCountExceedsCap)`): a small stsz sample count (or a tiny fixed-size one) alongside a mismatched stts/absurd declared count, confirmed refused; `TestValidate_MP4VideoTrackSampleTables` confirms the same stts mismatch is refused on the video track too, independent of the avc1 in-band scan. `TestValidate_MP4AudioSampleTableConsistency` (both packages) and `TestValidate_MP4AudioSampleLayoutVariants` (`audiosafe`, mirroring videosafe's own avc1-track layout tests) cover every table's own consistent and inconsistent shapes one at a time - including sbgp/sgpd's version 0/1 layouts and sgpd's variable-length description shape - each confirmed to validate as playable before being mutated into a refused one. `TestValidate_MP4EditList` covers `editListAllowed`'s own entry-count cap and duration bound, version 0 and 1 layouts, and both too-short malformed cases. `TestValidate_MP4AudioStz2Refused` (both packages) replaces a now-obsolete test that used to assert an audio track using stz2 (unsupported, same as video) validated as playable - it did, before this fix, purely because nothing ever checked an audio track's own sample tables at all. `FuzzValidateMP4` and `FuzzValidateMP4Audio` each gained seeds for the stts-mismatch and multi-entry-edit-list shapes.

**Real files.** `ffmpeg`-encoded phone-style H.264+AAC MP4s (720x1280 High, 1080x1920 High level 4.2, 1920x1080 High with 4 reference frames, 640x360 Baseline, with and without `-movflags +faststart`) and separate AAC M4A and Opus/Ogg files were built into `build/`, confirmed to still validate as playable and to decode cleanly under `ffmpeg -f null -`, then deleted. Dumping their own box trees directly is what confirmed the `sgpd`/`sbgp` version and offset layouts this fix assumes (`ffmpeg`'s AAC encoder writes a version-1 `sgpd` for its own `roll` grouping type, covering encoder-delay priming, and a version-0 `sbgp` alongside it) - both already covered by `allowedStblChild`, but never read until this fix.

## An avc1 sample entry's own avcC was found by type alone, and neither package checked what sat inside an mp4a or Opus entry at all

A security review found `avcConfigDetails` located `avcC` with `firstChild` - a search for one box type among however many others an entry might carry - never checking the rest of the entry at all. FFmpeg's own mov demuxer reads every child of a sample entry it recognizes, and three of them replace the H.264 configuration this package already validated with one it never saw: an `esds` or an `hvcC` (especially one placed *before* `avcC`, the exact order FFmpeg's own demuxer reads first, logging "ignoring multiple glbl" and keeping the earlier one) replaces the decoder's trusted SPS/PPS entirely, and a second `avcC` is read the same way a second `stsd` entry already was (see "`sampleEntryCodec` ... entry_count" above) - never checked, since this package only ever looked for *the* `avcC`, not *all* of them. Separately, neither package checked an `mp4a` entry's own `esds`, or an Opus entry's own `dOps`, for anything beyond the stsd codec fourcc itself; `esds` in particular can select any audio decoder FFmpeg supports, AAC or otherwise, regardless of what the track's own codec fourcc claimed.

**The fix applies the same real-encoder allowlist discipline `onlyChildrenAllowed` already applies to every container box in this package, one level deeper: to a sample entry's own children.** New in both packages' `sampleentry.go`: `avcSampleEntryChildrenOK` (videosafe only, since audiosafe has no video) walks every child of an avc1 entry past its own fixed `VisualSampleEntry` fields, refusing outright anything outside `allowedAVC1Child` (`avcC`, `pasp`, `colr`, `btrt`, `fiel` - confirmed against real `ffmpeg`/`libx264` output; `clap` is deliberately absent, since nothing in that output ever needed it) and requiring `avcC` to appear exactly once - closing the gap whether the replacement configuration sits before or after the one `avcC` this package trusts. `avcConfigDetails` now calls it before ever looking for `avcC` with `firstChild`, so a second avcC or a replacement configuration is refused before the one real `avcC` is even found. For `mp4a`/Opus, `findOneRequiredChild` does the equivalent walk against `allowedMP4AChild` (`esds`, `btrt`) or `allowedOpusChild` (`dOps`, `btrt`) respectively, and `esdsDescribesAAC` then reads the one `esds` found far enough to confirm it actually describes AAC: a hand-rolled MPEG-4 descriptor walk (`parseESDescriptor`, `parseDecoderConfigDescriptor`, `readMP4DescriptorHeader` - ISO/IEC 14496-1's own tag-length-value shape, assuming none of `ES_Descriptor`'s own optional flag bits are set, the shape every encoder this package has seen write an audio `esds` uses) down to `objectTypeIndication` (must be `0x40`, MPEG-4 Audio) and an `AudioSpecificConfig` whose `audioObjectType` (`allowedAACObjectType`: 2/AAC LC, 5/SBR, 29/PS), sample rate (one of the 13 standard table entries, or an explicit 24-bit rate within `[8000, 192000]`) and channel configuration (1-7) this package accepts. Opus's own `dOps` is checked only for its declared version (must be 0, the one value the ISOBMFF Opus amendment defines) - this package has no way to judge an Opus packet's own content any more deeply than that.

**`btrt` had to be added to both audio allowlists after real-file testing.** The first pass only allowed `esds`/`dOps` alone, matching the sharp-edges review's own literal suggestion ("one `esds` or `dOps`"); every one of the real `ffmpeg`-encoded files below has its AAC `mp4a` entry carrying a `btrt` (bitrate) box right after `esds`, the exact same box `ffmpeg` already writes after `avc1`'s own `avcC`, and the first pass refused every one of them. This is why the real-file check is run on the actual fix, not assumed from the specification alone.

**Tests.** `videosafe/sampleentry_test.go` and `audiosafe/sampleentry_test.go` cover `avc1`'s own children (`avcC` alone; `avcC` with `pasp`/`colr`/`btrt`/`fiel`; an `esds` or `hvcC` alongside `avcC`, in both orders; a second `avcC`; an unrecognized box - videosafe only) and `mp4a`/Opus's own AAC/dOps checks: every disallowed object type, sample rate (including the explicit-rate escape, both in and out of range), channel configuration, and malformed ES_Descriptor/DecoderConfigDescriptor shape (no DecoderSpecificInfo, a wrong nested tag, a descriptor length that never terminates its own continuation bytes, a multi-byte descriptor length past the single-byte encoding, an esds past `maxESDSLength`) - each confirmed to fail for the expected reason against the pre-fix code first. `FuzzValidateMP4`/`FuzzValidateMP4Audio` gained seeds for the avc1-with-esds and mp4a-non-AAC shapes.

**Real files.** The same seven `ffmpeg`-encoded files the sample-table entry above lists were built into `build/`, confirmed to still validate as playable and decode cleanly, then deleted - this is what found the `btrt`-alongside-`esds` gap above, after the first pass refused every one of them.

## A photo, sticker or GIF's own pixels are decoded and re-encoded in Go, never just structurally rewritten

Every review of `imagesafe`'s own PNG, JPEG, GIF and WebP walkers (see "Untrusted images are sanitized in the helper" and the long run of entries fixing a specific gap in one of them - repeated scans, fill bytes, a frame never checked against its canvas, a Graphics Control extension kept by shape alone, and others) was chasing the same open set: predicting, in Go, exactly what libjpeg-turbo, libpng, libwebp or Qt's own GIF reader would do with a sender's still-compressed pixel data, one mismatch at a time. Seventeen release-push review rounds over five days found a new one almost every time, about half of them media decoding inside the shell. That is the shape CVE-2023-4863 (a libwebp heap overflow, CVSS 8.8, on CISA's known-exploited list since 2023) and Chromium's own "Rule of 2" are both about: untrustworthy input, an unsafe language, and high privilege should never combine in one process, and no amount of pre-validating a sender's bytes in Go closes that so long as the sender's own entropy-coded pixel data - the actual JPEG scan, PNG `IDAT`, WebP `VP8`/`VP8L` bitstream, GIF LZW stream - still reaches a C decoder unchanged.

**The fix moves the trust boundary from "this container looks structurally sound" to "this decoded and re-encoded cleanly in memory-safe Go."** `imagesafe.Reencode(data, profile)` (`backend/internal/imagesafe/reencode.go`, replacing `Sanitize`'s own old body, which is now a thin wrapper discarding the extra return) detects the format by magic bytes exactly as before, then for every profile but `AnimatedGIF`: reads the declared width and height from a cheap `image.DecodeConfig` and checks them against `profile` *before* decoding a single pixel (`reencodeStill`) - the same "cheap check first" the old header-only reads already did, now guarding a real decode rather than a chunk walk - decodes the image fully (`decodeImage`, recovering from any panic a decoder raises on crafted input as a plain refusal, never a crash), checks the *actual* decoded bounds against `profile` again (since nothing but a real decode can confirm a declared size was not lying in the other direction), and writes a fresh encode of the result: JPEG at quality 90 when `hasAlpha` finds no pixel that is not fully opaque, PNG (`png.BestSpeed`) when it finds one anywhere - a sticker's own cut-out shape, say. `hasAlpha` type-switches on the decoded image's own concrete type first (`*image.Gray`, `*image.Gray16`, `*image.CMYK`, `*image.YCbCr` - everything `image/jpeg` or a grayscale `image/png` ever decodes to - can never carry alpha, so these skip the pixel scan entirely) before ever falling back to scanning every pixel's own `.RGBA()` for one that is not `0xffff`; it deliberately never compares `color.Model` values with `==` for this, since several of the standard library's own singletons (`color.GrayModel` and friends) wrap a `ModelFunc` - a function type - and comparing two interface values that both hold one panics at runtime ("comparing uncomparable type"), a mistake caught before it ever shipped. A GIF decodes through the same path as every other format here: `image.Decode`, unlike `gif.DecodeAll`, only ever reads the first frame, so a GIF under `Thumbnail` or `FullImage` becomes just another still image, never the animation `AnimatedGIF`'s own path (next paragraph) still needs. An animated WebP has no Go decoder at all - `golang.org/x/image/webp` (BSD-3, pinned at v0.41.0) decodes a still WebP but returns `webp: invalid format` for an animated one once `image.Decode` tries to read past its `DecodeConfig`-only header - so it is refused outright, never silently reduced to one frame the way a GIF is.

**The one profile left untouched: `AnimatedGIF`, still rewritten block by block.** `Reencode` dispatches to the unchanged `sanitizeGIF` (`gif.go`, not touched by this change at all) exactly when the detected format is GIF *and* the caller's profile equals `AnimatedGIF` - the one combination `connector.SanitizeIfGIF` (a real GIF animation, confirmed by its own magic bytes) and `connector.RevalidateDownloadedImage`'s GIF branch ever use. `AnimatedImage` needs the real container and every frame's own timing, which reducing to one re-encoded frame would throw away entirely, and `sanitizeGIF`'s own structural rewrite - never decoding a pixel, only walking and bounding blocks - was never the part any review found still open; every finding against it (frame-exceeds-canvas, the Graphics Control shape gap, the decode-rate bound) is already fixed in code this change leaves exactly as it was. `AnimatedGIF` paired with anything that is *not* actually GIF-format bytes (both connectors can reach this: a document a sender's own mimetype or filename merely claims is a GIF) now goes through the same decode-and-re-encode path as `Thumbnail`/`FullImage` instead of the deleted structural PNG/JPEG/WebP walkers - strictly safer than what it replaces, never less, since the file was never a real animation to begin with.

**What this deletes.** `backend/internal/imagesafe/png.go`, `jpeg.go` and `webp.go` are gone outright - every hand-written chunk, segment and RIFF walker, and every helper only they used (`pngKeptChunks`, `jpegKeptMarkers`, `jpegMaxScans`/`jpegMaxFillBytes` and the rest, `vp8xDimensions`/`vp8lDimensions`, `assembleWebP`, …) - along with the review-fix entries specific to bugs in that code ("A progressive JPEG's repeated scans...", "Extra `0xFF` fill bytes before a JPEG marker...", and WebP's own metadata-stripping and flag-clearing logic): none of it has anything left to walk once Go's own standard decoders, not this package's, are what actually reads a sender's bytes. `withinTotal` (`imagesafe.go`), only ever called by the now-deleted `sanitizeWebP`, is deleted too as dead code. `OutputFormat` (new, exported: `FormatJPEG`, `FormatPNG`, `FormatGIF`, `FormatUnknown`, each with `Extension()` and `MIME()`) is `Reencode`'s one addition to the exported surface, since a caller can no longer assume a photo stays whatever format it arrived as - the one new thing every caller needs to know that `Sanitize`'s old, format-preserving contract never had to express. `Dimensions(io.Reader)` (new) reads width and height from a file's own header alone, the cheap half of what `Reencode` does, for a caller that already trusts the bytes (its own prior `Reencode` output) and only needs its size again, not another full decode.

**Fixtures needed real, decodable bytes for the first time.** Every `imagesafe` test and fuzz seed that used to pass by structure alone - a hand-built WebP with a plausible header but arbitrary payload bytes "a real decoder would need but this package's own parser never reads" (the old `buildWebPExtended`/`buildWebPLossless`'s own words) - now has to actually decode, since `Reencode` runs a real decoder over it. Three small, real WebP fixtures (`backend/internal/imagesafe/testdata/webp_{opaque,alpha,animated}.webp`, generated once with ImageMagick's own libwebp-backed coder and committed, under a few hundred bytes each) replace the hand-built ones for the cases that need genuinely valid lossy, lossless-with-alpha and animated bitstreams; `gotd/td`'s own `thumbnail.ExpandTo` test fixture (`telegram/media_test.go`'s `stripped()`, previously five arbitrary placeholder bytes) is now the exact byte sequence that package's own `TestExpand` already proves expands into a real, fully decodable JPEG, for the same reason.
