## A photo or sticker's display copy is built once and served from the cache forever after, never re-decoded on every view

`imagesafe.Reencode` being a real decode is also why it must never run on every `media.fetch` the way the old `Sanitize`-based checks did: decoding a 12 MP photo and re-encoding it at quality 90 measures 70 ms + 118 ms on ordinary laptop hardware, and - unlike the old structural rewrite, which left a legitimate image's own pixels untouched - re-encoding is lossy. Re-running it on a file it already produced, every time a message bubble scrolls back into view, would both cost real, repeated CPU and visibly degrade a photo a little more on every scroll, generation loss stacking the same way repeatedly re-saving a JPEG always has.

**The fix: a display copy, built once, kept under a name no release before this one ever wrote, and never checked again once it exists.** `app.Commands.displayCopy` (`backend/internal/app/media.go`) names a message's own copy `cacheIdentity(m) + ".view"` (`displayCopyID`) - distinct from `cacheIdentity(m)` alone, the slot an original's own bytes, downloaded or outgoing, already occupy - and fetches it through `cache.FetchNamed`, whose `fill` closure calls the new `connector.ReencodeImageFile(originalPath, dst, profile)` (wrapping `imagesafe.Reencode` with the file I/O `EnsureImageSafe` used to do) exactly once, on a cache miss, and whose `nameFor` closure reads back the `imagesafe.OutputFormat` that same call just produced to pick `.jpg` or `.png` - so the name on disk always matches what is actually inside it, never guessed from the kind of message it illustrates. A cache *hit* is served as is, with no re-check of any kind: width and height are read back from the result's own header on every call, hit or miss, through `imagesafe.Dimensions` (`dimensionsOf`), cheap enough to pay unconditionally, unlike `Reencode`'s own full decode. This is safe specifically *because* the name is new: no release before this one ever wrote a `.view.jpg` or `.view.png` file, so finding one there is proof, by construction, that this release's own `Reencode` produced it - there is no "maybe an older release's weaker check already passed this" case left to worry about, the exact question the sweep below used to exist to answer.

**`servedPath` (`app/media.go`) now hands the UI the display copy, never the original, for a photo or sticker, incoming or outgoing alike.** `tryDisplayCopy` wraps `displayCopy` with the one piece of cleanup it needs: on failure, it removes the *original* it tried to build from (never the display-copy slot, which was never written) before reporting the error, so a bad original - a sender's malformed bytes, say - is never left sitting at the fixed name a next attempt's own cache lookup would otherwise hit again, silently skipping the network retry `recoverUnsafeImage` depends on; `servedPath` calls this on its own first attempt, and `recoverUnsafeImage` calls it again on the one retry a download gets, so the original is cleaned up on every failure, not just the first. An outgoing message's own bubble goes through exactly the same `displayCopy` call as an incoming one - the original it builds from is just a local attachment path instead of a freshly downloaded one, already re-encoded once at attach time (next paragraph) - rather than a parallel, special-cased path for "this machine's own content," which was the source of at least one earlier finding (the composer's own attachment preview wrongly treated as exempt from checking, see "Untrusted images are sanitized" above) this design has no equivalent surface for: there is exactly one function that ever produces the one file the UI ever loads.

**Outgoing images still re-encode once, at attach or paste time, never twice for no reason.** `connector.SanitizeOutgoingImage`/`SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` now call `imagesafe.Reencode` instead of the deleted `Sanitize`'s structural rewrite, but keep their own existing call sites and timing exactly as they were: `app.PasteImage` and `app.sanitizeAttachedPhoto` each run it exactly once, when the user pastes or attaches a photo, before the composer ever shows a preview built from it. The *bubble* for that same message, once sent, still goes through its own `displayCopy` the first time anything views it - a second re-encode, from the already-once-re-encoded attachment - rather than somehow reusing the attach-time result directly: keeping exactly one `displayCopy` code path for every direction a photo can arrive from was judged worth that one extra, one-time re-encode per outgoing photo, rather than two separate "how is this photo's display copy produced" answers to keep in sync. `SaveMedia` (unchanged) is what every connector's `FetchMedia` now calls for a downloaded photo, sticker or GIF too, exactly the same call a video or voice note already used: the original no longer needs its own check at write time, since nothing ever serves it to the UI unchecked - `Reencode`'s own check, run once when the display copy is first built, is the one that actually matters now, not a second one at a time that can never be re-run anyway.

**This makes two whole layers of defense obsolete, deleted in the same change rather than left behind as dead weight.** "A legacy full image, sticker or GIF file is swept once per on-disk format version" and "Every photo or sticker `media.fetch` ever returns is re-checked against `imagesafe` on that same call" (both above) each existed to answer "was this file already on disk checked by *this* release's own rules" - the sweep by walking every existing file once after an upgrade, the per-serve re-check by never trusting a cache hit's own age at all. Once a display copy's name is itself the proof that this release's own `Reencode` produced it, neither question has anything left to answer: there is no "this file predates the current checks" case a `.view.*` name could ever describe, so there is nothing left for a sweep to find or a per-serve re-check to confirm. `backend/medialegacy.go` (`revalidateLegacyMedia`, called from `serve` before `srv.Start`) and `backend/internal/connector/revalidate.go` (`RevalidateDownloadedImage`/`RevalidateOutgoingImage`) are deleted outright, along with `cache.Cache`/`cache.Outgoing`'s own `Revalidate`, `FormatVersion` and `SetFormatVersion` (confirmed unused by anything else in `store` or `cache`'s own callers before deleting them) and every test specific to that machinery. `connector.EnsureImageSafe`, the per-serve re-check itself, is gone too, replaced by `displayCopy`/`tryDisplayCopy` above.

## A stored thumbnail is still re-encoded on every read, measured cheap enough to keep doing exactly that

"A legacy thumbnail is re-sanitized on every read" (above) already established that `scanMessage` runs every stored thumbnail through `sanitizedThumb` on every read, never trusting a migration to have been the last word - and that this was free to do unconditionally, because the old `Sanitize` bounded itself to a chunk walk plus a header-only `DecodeConfig`. Once `sanitizedThumb` calls `imagesafe.Reencode` instead, that is no longer true by assumption; it needed measuring, not assuming, the same way `displayCopy`'s own cost above did.

**Measured: a page of 50 messages, each with a thumbnail, through the store's own public `Messages` call (`backend/internal/store/thumbbench_test.go`, `BenchmarkMessages_PageOfThumbnails`).** A typical stripped thumbnail - 40×30, `gotd/td`'s own real shape, the one most messages actually carry - costs 6.6 ms for the whole page, about 130 µs each: imperceptible against a page load that already does a SQLite round trip and a JSON unmarshal per row. The pathological case - every one of the 50 thumbnails simultaneously at `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s own absolute cap, 1024×1024, something no real client has a reason to ever send since a thumbnail is never drawn anywhere near that large - costs 357 ms for the page, about 7 ms each: noticeably slower, but still a single, bounded, one-time cost per page load, not a loop or a cost that scales with anything other than the fixed cap `imagesafe.Thumbnail` already enforces regardless of this change.

**Decision: keep re-encoding on every read, rather than moving to write-time-only plus a store migration.** The typical cost is cheap enough that nothing about this needed to change; the pathological cost, while real, is bounded by a cap this package already enforced before `Reencode` existed; and every message a conversation ever lists must still pass through `scanMessage` regardless of how its thumbnail was first written, the same "cheap enough to run unconditionally, and simpler than trusting every write path to remember" reasoning the original migration entry gave for never moving this to write-time-only in the first place. Moving to write-time-only would trade a small, bounded per-read cost for a new migration (`migrations` in `store/migrate.go`, appended, never edited in place) to re-encode every already-stored thumbnail once, and a new invariant - every write path remembers to re-encode, forever - to keep that migration from immediately going stale the moment one write path forgets, which is exactly the class of mistake "A legacy thumbnail is re-sanitized on every read" was written to stop relying on. `scanMessage` still never writes anything back on the read path it runs on - only `verifyThumb`'s own write-path call persists a change - so the "never write re-encoded bytes back on read" guarantee holds exactly as it always has.

## A thumbnail's data URI names the MIME type its own bytes actually are, never assumed from the message it illustrates

Every `Image` that sources a thumbnail as a `data:` URI (`PhotoView.qml`, `PhotoViewer.qml`, `StickerView.qml`, `LinkPreview.qml`, `InlineVideo.qml`) used to hardcode `image/jpeg` or, for `StickerView.qml` alone, `image/png` - reasonable while `imagesafe.Sanitize` only ever rewrote a thumbnail's container, never its pixels, so a WhatsApp or Telegram preview stayed whatever format the sender's own client chose to send. Now that `sanitizedThumb` calls `imagesafe.Reencode`, a thumbnail's own format is decided by `hasAlpha`, not by which kind of message it illustrates: an opaque photo preview becomes JPEG and a sticker's cut-out shape becomes PNG almost always, but neither is guaranteed - an opaque sticker (a plain rectangle, say) reencodes to JPEG exactly like a photo would.

`ui/lib/Media.js`'s new `thumbMime(thumb)` sniffs the base64 string's own leading characters rather than trusting either guess: `iVBORw0KGgo` is the fixed base64 encoding of every PNG's own 8-byte signature, so a thumb starting with it is `image/png`; anything else, including no thumb at all, defaults to `image/jpeg`, the more common of the two and `Reencode`'s only other possible output for a `Thumbnail`-profile image. All five QML files now build their data URI from it (`"data:" + Media.thumbMime(thumb) + ";base64," + thumb`) instead of a hardcoded string. `ui/lib/Setup.js`'s own `qrSource` keeps its own hardcoded `image/png` unchanged - a sign-in QR code is a PNG this process generates itself with `rsc.io/qr`, never a sender's bytes, so it was never in scope here.

## A `file://` source's own suffix never needs to match its content for Qt to render it, confirmed rather than assumed

`displayCopy`'s own name always matches what `Reencode` actually wrote (above), but an outgoing attachment on disk is a different story: `cache.Outgoing.Store` names it after whatever the user attached (`photo.png`, say), and `connector.SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` can change that same file's content to JPEG without ever renaming it, if the photo turns out to have no real transparency. Whether `ComposerController`'s own chip preview - a plain `file://` `Image` source, the same as any other - would still render a JPEG file still named `.png` needed an answer before deciding that was safe to leave alone, not an assumption either way.

**Confirmed empirically, not just read from documentation:** `tests/qml/PhotoView/shell.qml` gained `mismatchedExtensionPhoto`, a real PNG file committed as `mismatched.jpg` (alongside a matching-extension `opaque.jpg`, for the ordinary case), loaded exactly the way `PhotoView`'s own `Links.fileURL(path)` binding always has; `waitForMismatchedExtensionFileToRender` polls its `Image` until `status` leaves `Loading`, and the test fails outright - confirmed by deliberately pointing it at a missing file first - if it ever reports `Error` rather than `Ready`. It passes: Qt's `QImageReader`, reached through `QQuickImage`, decodes a `file://` source by sniffing its actual header bytes, falling back to that whenever a suffix-based guess does not already confirm it, not by trusting the suffix alone. This is also why `fixedMediaName`'s own `".jpg"` for every photo - unconditional, regardless of whether a connector's download or this helper's own `Reencode` output happens to be JPEG or PNG - was never a bug needing a fix of its own: the original it names is never read by anything but this helper's own Go code, which sniffs by magic bytes exactly like `QImageReader` does, never by extension either.

**What this means for naming an outgoing attachment.** `cache.Outgoing.Store` keeps the user's own chosen name unchanged, extension included, even once `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` may have changed what is actually inside it: the composer's own preview, `MediaController`'s copy action and the upload itself all either sniff content already (`sniffContentType`, `imagesafe.LooksLikeImage`) or, like the chip preview, go through exactly the `QImageReader` path this test just confirmed tolerates the mismatch. Renaming the file to match would have meant threading a new path back through every place `media.FileName`/`OriginalPath` already assume the attachment keeps the name it was given at `prepareAttachment` time, for a problem this test shows does not exist.

## Go's own progressive JPEG decoder has no limit on repeated scans, reopening the exact gap `imagesafe` used to close structurally

Removing `imagesafe/jpeg.go`'s own marker walker (see "A photo, sticker or GIF's own pixels are decoded and re-encoded in Go," above) removed `jpegMaxScans` along with it, on the reasoning that Go's own `image/jpeg` decoder, not this package's hand-written one, now does the only parsing that matters. That covered memory safety - Go's decoder cannot corrupt memory the way libjpeg-turbo's crafted-input bugs did - but not CPU cost: a follow-up review of `$GOROOT/src/image/jpeg/scan.go`'s `processSOS` found it has no limit of its own on how many start-of-scan (`0xFF 0xDA`) markers a file may declare, and each one makes it re-walk every MCU block of the image again - the identical shape "A progressive JPEG's repeated scans..." (above) first found and fixed, just moved from this package's own parser into the standard library's, where nothing was left checking it at all. Worse than before the original fix: `store`'s own `sanitizedThumb` now calls `Reencode` on every read of a stored thumbnail ("A stored thumbnail is still re-encoded on every read," above), so a hostile thumbnail pays this cost again on every conversation load, not once at write time.

**Measured, not estimated.** A crafted, genuinely-decodable 1024×1024 progressive JPEG (`cjpeg -progressive`, a real 10-scan baseline with one of its own tiny `Ah=0` AC scans - 12 bytes of header and entropy data together - spliced in 9,990 more times) is 144,440 bytes, comfortably under every profile's own `MaxInputLen`, and takes Go's `image/jpeg.Decode` **1.69 seconds** to decode successfully (1.691s, 1.688s, 1.698s over three runs, on ordinary laptop hardware). The same image with its own ordinary 10 scans decodes in 7.9-8.4 ms; with 101 scans (the cap's own boundary), 23.4-23.5 ms: cost scales linearly with scan count, confirming `processSOS` really does repeat full-image work per scan, not with the file's own size - 144 KB of attacker bytes buys 1.69 seconds of CPU, paid again on every thumbnail load.

**The fix: count scans before decoding, the same bound as before, now guarding the standard library's decode instead of this package's own parser.** `jpegMaxScans` (100, unchanged - libjpeg-turbo's own `-maxscans` default, generous next to the roughly ten scans a real progressive encode uses) is back, in `reencode.go`, checked by `reencodeStill` right after the existing header-only dimension check and before `decodeImage` ever runs. `countJPEGScans` counts occurrences of the literal byte pair `0xFF 0xDA` anywhere in the input with one linear pass - no marker-structure parsing, no tracking of fill bytes or restart intervals - because an over-count only ever rejects more input, never less, which is all a cap needs here. This also means the exact fill-byte bypass "Extra `0xFF` fill bytes before a JPEG marker bypassed the scan and table caps" (above) found in the old parser cannot recur here by construction: that bug came from a scanner that first classified a byte as "marker or not" and could be fooled into skipping past a real marker hidden behind fill bytes; this one classifies nothing and checks every adjacent byte pair, so `FF FF DA` is still found as one `FF DA` occurrence (at the second and third bytes) regardless of how many fill bytes come first.

**Tests.** `TestReencode_RejectsJPEGWithTooManyScans` and `TestReencode_AcceptsRealisticProgressiveScanCount` (`imagesafe/reencode_test.go`) cover the cap's two sides with real, decoder-accepted fixtures rather than synthetic marker splicing: `testdata/jpeg_progressive_10_scans.jpg` (a genuine 32×32 progressive JPEG, 10 scans, 614 bytes) must still decode and re-encode normally, and `testdata/jpeg_progressive_101_scans.jpg` (the same image, with one of its own tiny scans duplicated 91 more times to reach 101 - confirmed, before this fix, to decode successfully, so the test proves the new count check itself is what rejects it, not an unrelated decode failure) must not. The existing `insertFakeMarkersWithFill`-based fuzz seed was left as is: it splices unstructured marker bytes that never form a decodable file at all, so it was never a usable fixture for this cap's "must still accept" side - only for confirming Go's own decoder never panics on the shape, which it already did.

**The other three registered decoders - PNG, GIF's first-frame path and still WebP - were checked the same way and found not to have an equivalent gap.** Read directly, not assumed: `image/png/reader.go`'s own interlaced decode loops `pass < 7` - a fixed constant, never attacker-controlled, and Adam7's seven passes together still cover very close to exactly width×height pixels, never more; every IDAT chunk is read as one continuous `zlib.Reader`, bounded by the pixel count `withinCaps` already checked before any decode starts. `image/gif/reader.go`'s own `decode` returns as soon as its first image descriptor is read (`!keepAllFrames && len(d.image) == 1`), so there is no multi-frame cost to inflate in the first place; `newImageFromDescriptor` explicitly rejects a frame descriptor declaring a size larger than the logical screen already read (`left+width > d.width || top+height > d.height`), so a frame cannot smuggle a bigger decode past the header check either; its single LZW stream is then read for exactly `len(m.Pix)` bytes, once. `golang.org/x/image/webp`'s own chunk loop decodes at most one `VP8X`, one `ALPH` and one `VP8`-or-`VP8L` chunk before returning - a second image or alpha chunk is rejected outright by a state flag (`wantAlpha`, `seenVP8X`), never decoded twice - and VP8's own internal partition count, the one place the format allows splitting a frame's entropy-coded data at all, is hard-bounded to 8 by the decoder's own `[8]partition` array, nowhere near large enough to turn into a repeat-cost multiplier the way an unbounded JPEG scan count was. None of the three has a "cheap, repeatable unit that each costs a full image's worth of decode work" shape for a cap to bound.

## `connector.ReencodeImageFile` read a source file into memory before checking its size

A follow-up review found `ReencodeImageFile` (`connector/media.go`) called `os.ReadFile(srcPath)` before anything checked `srcPath`'s own size against `profile.MaxInputLen` - the one check `EnsureImageSafe`, the per-serve re-check this function replaced (see "A photo, sticker or GIF's own pixels are decoded and re-encoded in Go," above), always ran first. `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile`, a few lines away in the same file, already got this right for an outgoing attachment; `ReencodeImageFile` did not, for a downloaded or legacy file reaching the same display-copy path.

**The fix.** `ReencodeImageFile` now calls `os.Stat(srcPath)` first and returns `ErrMediaTooLarge`, wrapped, the moment `info.Size() > profile.MaxInputLen` - before `os.ReadFile` is ever called - mirroring `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile`'s own existing check exactly.

**Tests.** `TestReencodeImageFile_RejectsOverProfileSizeBeforeReading` (`connector/media_test.go`) writes a file over `imagesafe.Thumbnail.MaxInputLen` holding no real image bytes at all, and confirms `ErrMediaTooLarge`, not `ErrUnsafeImage`: the latter would mean the oversized file was read and handed to `Reencode` anyway, only to be refused once there, exactly the extra cost this check closes.

## `tryDisplayCopy` removed an outgoing photo's own attachment on a cache failure that said nothing about its bytes

A follow-up review found `tryDisplayCopy` (`app/media.go`) removed `originalPath` on *any* error from `displayCopy` - a transient cache I/O failure (a full disk, say) included, not only imagesafe refusing the bytes. For an incoming photo this risked only an extra, avoidable re-download; for an outgoing message still pending, `originalPath` is the attachment the send itself still needs to upload, and removing it on a cache hiccup would strand that send with nothing left to retry.

**The fix.** `tryDisplayCopy` now checks `errors.Is(err, connector.ErrUnsafeImage)` before removing anything: only a genuine refusal of the bytes themselves still removes `originalPath`, exactly as before; every other failure - a cache error, most concretely - returns the error unchanged, leaving the file exactly where it was.

**Tests.** `TestFetchMedia_KeepsTheOriginalWhenTheDisplayCopyFailsForAnUnrelatedReason` (`app/media_test.go`) sends a photo, then fetches it through a second `Commands` sharing the same store and outgoing directory but backed by a cache that fails every `FetchNamed` before its `fill` closure (the re-encode) ever runs, and confirms the attachment is still there afterward. The existing `TestFetchMedia_RemovesAnUnsafeLegacyCacheFileAndRefetches`, `TestFetchMedia_RemovesAnUnsafeStickerCacheFileAndRefetches` and `TestFetchMedia_NeverServesAnUnsafeFileWhenNoSafeReplacementExists` continue to cover the other side - removal when the bytes genuinely fail the check - unchanged and still passing.
## Untrusted video, voice notes, animations and stickers are re-encoded inside a bubblewrap sandbox before the shell ever sees them, rather than pre-checked in Go

`videosafe` and `audiosafe` try to make a sender's own video and audio container safe to hand to QtMultimedia by predicting, in Go, exactly what FFmpeg's C decoders will do with it. Seventeen release-push review rounds (28 Oct - 2 Nov) found a new mismatch between that prediction and FFmpeg's own parser almost every time, about half of them media decoding in the shell; none of the seventeen passed all four reviews run against them. This is the class of bug the approach cannot fully close, no matter how many more allowlists and sample-table checks it grows: Go is predicting a C parser it does not run. The alternative every other project in this position has converged on - Chromium's "Rule of 2" (never combine untrustworthy input, an unsafe language and high privilege in one step), Firefox's sandboxed decoder process, GNOME glycin's bwrap-sandboxed image loaders - is to run the untrustworthy parser itself inside a sandbox that bounds what it can reach and for how long, and trust only what it hands back out. For media, that means: re-encode every received video, voice note, animation and sticker with the system's own FFmpeg, run entirely inside a sandbox, and let the shell play only the file that sandboxed FFmpeg itself produced - never a sender's own bytes. `backend/internal/sandbox` runs one command inside that sandbox; `backend/internal/transcode` is the one thing it is built to run. This phase builds both, standalone: neither is wired into `app`, `server` or a connector yet, and `videosafe`/`audiosafe` are not touched. A later change does the wiring and can then delete most of both packages.

**What `sandbox.Runner.Run` actually executes.** `prlimit` (setting the resource limits below), running `bwrap`, running the caller's own binary - verified, piece by piece and end to end:

```
prlimit --cpu=900 --as=4294967296 --fsize=536870912 --nofile=64 -- \
  bwrap --ro-bind /usr /usr --symlink usr/lib /lib --symlink usr/lib /lib64 --symlink usr/bin /bin \
    --proc /proc --dev /dev --tmpfs /tmp \
    --ro-bind <input> /work/in --bind <output-dir> /work/out \
    --unshare-all --die-with-parent --new-session --clearenv \
    --setenv PATH /usr/bin:/bin --setenv LANG C.UTF-8 --chdir /work \
    --seccomp <fd> \
    -- <binary> <args>
```

`/usr` is bound read-only, with `/lib`, `/lib64` and `/bin` symlinked into it the way a real root does, so a normal dynamically-linked binary finds its shared libraries; `/proc` and `/dev` are fresh, and `/tmp` is an empty tmpfs private to the job. Nothing else of the host filesystem is visible at all - not bind, not read-only, not present under any name - confirmed directly: a file that exists on the host but was never bound in is simply not there (`cat` on it fails with "No such file or directory", not a permission error, because the path never existed in the sandbox's own mount namespace to begin with). The one input file and the one output directory a job needs are bound at the fixed, sandbox-internal paths `sandbox.InputPath` (`/work/in`) and `sandbox.OutputDirPath` (`/work/out`); a caller's own arguments always name those fixed paths, never a sender-controlled file name, so argv itself - readable by any other local user through `/proc`, per `.golangci.yml`'s `forbidigo` rule - never carries anything a sender wrote. `--unshare-all` drops every namespace this process has permission to drop, including network: confirmed directly, a sandboxed `bash`'s own `/dev/tcp` connect to a public IP fails with "Network is unreachable". `--clearenv` plus two `--setenv` calls are the only environment a job ever sees. `--die-with-parent` is doing two distinct jobs: if this helper's own process dies unexpectedly, the kernel's `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` on the `prlimit`/`bwrap` child (set by `Run` itself) kills it, and `bwrap`'s own flag then cascades that into its internal fork and the sandboxed command, so nothing survives a helper crash. For the ordinary case - `Run`'s own `ctx` ending while the helper is still very much alive - `Run` sends `SIGKILL` to the whole process group `Setpgid` put the job in. Confirmed that this reaches the actual sandboxed command even though `bwrap`'s own internal fork moves to a *different* process group of its own (`--new-session` calls `setsid`): killing just the outer `bwrap`/`prlimit` process is enough, because `--die-with-parent`'s own cascade - not the process-group signal fanning out - is what takes down the rest. `Setpgid` is kept anyway: it is what makes "kill this one job" unambiguous, and it costs nothing given `--die-with-parent` is already required for the crash case.

**Resource limits (`sandbox/limits.go`), and why each number is what it is.** `--cpu=900` (seconds): transcode's own longest wall-clock timeout is five minutes at two threads, which at full use of both could reach 600 CPU-seconds; 900 leaves this as a backstop that should never fire before the caller's own context deadline does. `--fsize=536870912` (512 MiB): well above what any of transcode's own profiles should legitimately write (its largest, Video, measures in the tens of MiB even at its own ten-minute cap), so transcode's own tighter per-profile check is what should normally catch an oversized output first; hitting the kernel's own limit first kills the job with `SIGXFSZ`, which `Run` reports as `Result.TooLarge`. `--nofile=64`: modest, confirmed sufficient for every profile this helper runs, each of which opens at most its one input, its one output and a handful of library files. `--as=4294967296` (4 GiB) is the one limit a first, smaller choice got wrong, and why is worth keeping: FFmpeg links an unusually large number of optional libraries (CUDA, Vulkan, VapourSynth and others its own `-version` output lists), and the dynamic linker maps all of them into the process before a single frame is decoded - a cost that exists whether or not the job ever touches any of them. Measured directly: re-encoding a 720x1280 clip with audio at two threads (transcode's own Video profile) failed inside `libx264`'s own allocator ("malloc of size 3582272 failed") under a 2 GiB cap, with or without `bwrap` in the picture, and succeeded under 3 GiB; 4 GiB leaves a full GiB of headroom above that measured point. `-max_alloc` (transcode's own, separate, 512 MiB ffmpeg flag, bounding one allocation through FFmpeg's own `av_malloc`) did not help here, confirming the `libx264` failure was against the process's own address space, not an FFmpeg-internal allocation it tracks itself.

**Seccomp: included.** `elastic/go-seccomp-bpf` (pure Go, Apache-2.0, already satisfies this helper's `CGO_ENABLED=0` build) assembles a named deny list - `ptrace`, `process_vm_readv`/`writev`, `keyctl`/`add_key`/`request_key`, `bpf`, `perf_event_open`, `userfaultfd`, `mount`/`umount2`/`pivot_root`/`unshare`/`setns`, `kexec_load`/`kexec_file_load`, `init_module`/`finit_module`/`delete_module`, `io_uring_setup`/`enter`/`register`, `personality` - into `golang.org/x/net/bpf.Instruction`s, which `bpf.Assemble` turns into `bpf.RawInstruction`s. Those already match the kernel's own `struct sock_filter` byte for byte (`code uint16, jt uint8, jf uint8, k uint32`, 8 bytes, no padding), so writing each one in order with `encoding/binary` (little-endian; every machine this helper ships for is) is the whole job - no hand-rolled BPF, exactly as planned if the library could not do this, except it can. The bytes go to `bwrap` through an `os.Pipe`: written and the write end closed before `Run` even starts the process, so the data (a few hundred bytes, far under a pipe's default 64 KiB buffer) is already sitting in the kernel buffer for `bwrap` to read via `--seccomp <fd>` once it execs. Confirmed, with the real filter loaded through the real `bwrap`: an ordinary read inside the sandbox still works, and `unshare -U true` - one of the denied syscalls - is refused outright. Every denied syscall, and a syscall made through the wrong architecture, returns an errno (`ActionErrno`) rather than killing the process - not the first choice, but the one that does not reach systemd-coredump; see the entry below for why that distinction turned out to matter a great deal. The denylist is defence in depth behind the namespace isolation above, which already makes most of these fail or do nothing; it is not this job's only or even its main defence.

**The five profiles (`backend/internal/transcode`).** Every profile shares the same input-side arguments - `-nostdin -y -v error -threads 2 -max_alloc 536870912 -protocol_whitelist file -i /work/in` (the whitelist means a crafted container's own protocol reference, if it had one, cannot make FFmpeg's demuxer reach anywhere but the one local file it was given) - and the same stripping - `-map_metadata -1 -map_chapters -1 -sn -dn` - before its own, fixed codec arguments:

- **Video**: first video stream, first audio stream if any (`-map 0:v:0 -map 0:a:0?`), scaled to fit 1280x1280 without ever upscaling (`scale='min(1280,iw)':'min(1280,ih)':force_original_aspect_ratio=decrease`, which can only shrink, never enlarge, since both targets are already capped at the source's own size) then rounded to even dimensions, `yuv420p`, `-fpsmax 30`, `libx264` main profile/veryfast/crf 23, AAC stereo 96k, capped at ten minutes (the same ceiling `videosafe.maxDuration` already used), `+faststart`, MP4.
- **Animation**: the same video settings, `-an` - no audio at all - for a GIF or a "GIF video".
- **Audio**: first audio stream only (`-vn`), downmixed to mono-or-stereo (`aformat=sample_fmts=s16:channel_layouts=mono|stereo`), `pcm_s16le` at 24 kHz, WAV, same ten-minute cap.
- **Sound**: first audio stream only, the same downmix, `pcm_s16le` at 48 kHz, WAV, capped at 30 seconds - Qt's `SoundEffect` only plays uncompressed WAV. See the later entry below on why both now write WAV and PCM rather than a compressed codec, and how the helper itself, not the sandboxed ffmpeg, is what actually produces the file it returns.
- **StillFrame**: first video stream, one frame (`-vframes 1`), scaled to fit 1024x1024, PNG - for an animated WebP sticker, which neither Go's standard library nor `x/image/webp` can decode at all.

Rotation relies entirely on FFmpeg's own autorotate (the default): confirmed directly, a 1280x720 source tagged with a 90-degree display-matrix rotation decodes to a physically rotated frame before this package's own scale filter ever sees it, producing a 720x1280 output with no rotation side data of its own - exactly what a sender's phone video needs, with no special-casing in this package at all. Injecting that rotation into a test fixture turned out to need its own two-step form: FFmpeg's `-display_rotation` is read-side only on this build (passing it as an output option is rejected outright), so a fixture is built plain, then remuxed with `-display_rotation:v:0 90 -i plain.mp4 -c copy` to inject the side data stream-copy carries forward - worth recording here since it is exactly the kind of thing to get wrong quietly in a test rather than in the profile itself.

`Transcoder.run` turns a `sandbox.Result` into one of four sentinel errors - `ErrUnavailable` (checked via `sandbox.Runner.Available` before a job ever starts), `ErrTimeout`, `ErrTooLong` (either the sandbox's own `--fsize` backstop or this package's own, tighter per-profile size check after the fact) or `ErrFailed` - and separately confirms the one file a profile's own fixed arguments name actually exists, is non-empty and is within that profile's own cap: FFmpeg reporting success is not, on its own, proof it wrote a usable file.

**Tests.** `sandbox`'s own `buildArgv`/`bwrapArgs` are pure and tested exhaustively against a full, literal expected argv; process start/wait/kill/classify logic is tested through an injectable `command` seam (and an equally injectable `lookPath`, so these tests need neither `bwrap` nor `prlimit` actually installed) against trivial real `/bin/sh` scripts - including a real `Setpgid` kill of a process that itself started a grandchild, proving the kill reaches the whole group. A black-box suite in `sandbox_test.go` then runs the real pipeline end to end - a trivial command, the network-unreachable case, the file-outside-bound-paths case, and the real seccomp enforcement case - each skipping rather than failing when the environment cannot actually run it, so the unit tests above are what keep both packages at their coverage gate everywhere else. `transcode`'s own profile argument lists are each checked by exact equality through a hand-written fake satisfying its own small, unexported `runner` interface; its shared `run`/`resultError`/`checkOutput` logic is covered once, through `Video`, for every outcome (unavailable, sandbox error, timed out, too large, non-zero exit, missing output, empty output, oversized output, success). Real, skip-if-unavailable integration tests in each profile's own file generate their fixtures with the host's own, unsandboxed FFmpeg - a 720x1280 clip with audio, the rotated clip above, 1920x1080, HEVC (skipping itself if `libx265` is not built in), an animated GIF, an animated WebP, an Opus voice note, an M4A and an MP3 - run them through the real `Transcoder`, and check the result with `ffprobe`; everything they create lives under `build/` and is removed once the test ends, win or lose.

## A review of the sandbox found a real seccomp bypass, a nested-namespace hole, a symlink trap, a denylist still too narrow, and no limit on concurrent jobs

A review of the sandbox and transcode packages above found five more gaps, each confirmed for real before being fixed, not assumed from reading the code alone.

**1. `elastic/go-seccomp-bpf`'s own `Policy.Assemble` jumps straight to `DefaultAction` on a foreign architecture, skipping every syscall the policy names at all.** Traced through `filter.go`: when the loaded `seccomp_data.arch` does not equal the architecture the policy was built for, the `JumpIf` it emits skips forward by exactly `len(x32Filter)+len(instructions)` instructions - landing precisely on the policy's own final `Ret(DefaultAction)`, past every syscall-specific check the policy names, denied or not. With this package's own `DefaultAction` of `Allow`, that is a real, working bypass of the whole denylist on any architecture but the one the filter was built for, not a theoretical one: confirmed with a probe (a static binary making an identical `unshare(CLONE_NEWUSER)` call two ways) that the native x86_64 path, through the normal `syscall()` wrapper, was killed by the filter exactly as `deniedSyscalls` promises, while the same call made through the i386 ABI (`int $0x80`, reachable on any kernel built with `CONFIG_IA32_EMULATION`, which this one is) sailed through and succeeded outright.

**The fix adds this package's own architecture gate in front of the library's program, rather than patching the library.** `archGate` (`sandbox/seccomp.go`) is three `golang.org/x/net/bpf` instructions - load the arch field, jump past a kill if it equals the wanted one, kill otherwise - prepended to the library's own assembled policy by `seccompInstructions` (new; `seccompProgram` now calls it rather than assembling the policy directly). Because `golang.org/x/net/bpf`'s own jump instructions are relative, prepending instructions ahead of an already-assembled program needs no renumbering. The gate checks against this *build's* own native architecture (`arch.GetInfo("")`, the same resolution the library already does internally for its own policy), not a literal x86_64 constant: the probe's own finding is specific to x86_64/i386, but the identical shape of hole exists on an arm64 release through `CONFIG_COMPAT`'s own 32-bit ARM syscalls, and gating on this build's own native arch closes both with one piece of code rather than one that silently only ever protected the architecture it was written against.

**Tests.** `TestSeccompInstructions_RefusesAForeignArchitectureRegardlessOfSyscall`, `..._RefusesEveryDeniedSyscallOnTheNativeArch` (every entry in `deniedSyscalls`, not a sample), `..._AllowsOrdinarySyscallsOnTheNativeArch` (`read`/`write`/`mmap`) and `..._X32SyscallNumberIsRefusedWithENOSYS` all assemble `seccompInstructions` and run it through `golang.org/x/net/bpf`'s own software VM against hand-built `seccomp_data` packets - proving the *assembled program's* behaviour, not just that assembly succeeds. The VM's own `LoadAbsolute` always reads multi-byte fields big-endian (`vm_instructions.go`'s `loadCommon`, the same convention real packet-filter BPF uses) regardless of what a real kernel does with the native-endian `seccomp_data` it actually hands the filter, so the test packets are built big-endian specifically for this VM, not to mirror the kernel's own layout - noted directly in `seccompDataPacket`'s own comment so a future reader is not misled into "fixing" it to match the kernel instead. The manual probe (source kept out of the repository; it is a one-off check, not a fixture) was re-run through the real, fixed `Runner.Run` after the fix: both the x86_64 and the i386 path now report the sandboxed process refused with `EPERM` (an ordinary non-zero exit, no signal at all - see the entry below for why that matters), where before the fix the i386 path alone reported the call succeeding.

**2. `--unshare-all` alone does not stop a sandboxed job from opening a second, nested user namespace.** Confirmed directly: inside a sandbox built exactly as this package's own verified command describes, `unshare -U true` - creating a new user namespace nested inside the sandbox's own - succeeds. A nested user namespace is a privilege-regaining trick in its own right (a process can hold real capabilities, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` included, over resources inside a namespace it itself created), so leaving this open meant the sandbox's own isolation was weaker than its own flags implied, regardless of whatever the seccomp filter happened to deny.

**The fix adds `--unshare-user --disable-userns` to `bwrapArgs`.** `--disable-userns` ("disable further use of user namespaces inside sandbox") needs `--unshare-user` given explicitly first - bwrap does not treat `--unshare-all` as satisfying that on its own. Confirmed, with the two flags added, that the identical `unshare -U true` now fails with `ENOSPC` ("No space left on device" - the kernel's own, if oddly worded, error for `/proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces` reaching its own per-namespace limit of zero, which is what `--disable-userns` sets), and that removing just these two flags again reproduces the original success - so the fix, not something else already in the argv, is what closes it.

**Tests.** `TestBuildArgv_FullSpec` (exact-equality) now expects both flags in the argv; no new test exercises the kernel behaviour itself beyond the manual confirmation above, since `sandbox_test.go`'s own `TestRun_SeccompDeniesUnshare` already proves a job calling `unshare` at all does not succeed (killed by the syscall denylist before the namespace question is ever reached) - this fix is about the syscalls the denylist does *not* and cannot safely cover (`clone`, used constantly for ordinary threading), not a second way to catch the one it already does.

**3. `checkOutput` followed a symlink instead of refusing one.** It used `os.Stat`, which resolves through a symlink to whatever it ultimately points at. A compromised or merely misbehaving ffmpeg could leave `out.mp4` as a symlink to any file on the host - `/etc/shadow`, another user's data, a file elsewhere in this helper's own data directory - and `checkOutput` would report it existing, non-empty and within size exactly as if ffmpeg itself had written video there; whatever this helper does next with "the transcoded file" would then open, copy or serve that target instead.

**The fix switches to `os.Lstat` and requires a regular file.** `info.Mode().IsRegular()` refuses anything else - a symlink, but also a FIFO, a socket or a device node - with `ErrFailed`, the same sentinel a missing or empty output already uses. This is not a race to close: `transcode`'s own package doc comment now records why, since it is worth having written down rather than re-derived next time someone wonders whether a check-then-use gap exists here. bwrap gives the sandboxed job its own PID namespace, and destroying a PID namespace - killing every process still in it - is unconditional kernel behaviour the instant that namespace's own pid 1 (bwrap's internal fork) exits, which is exactly what `Run`'s own wait blocks on; by the time `Run` returns to `checkOutput`, there is no process left anywhere that could still be swapping the symlink's target underneath this check.

**Tests.** `fakeRunner` (`transcode`'s own test helper) gained `writeOutputSymlinkTo`, making it create a symlink instead of writing a file; `TestVideo_OutputIsASymlink` drives this through the public API and expects `ErrFailed`. `TestCheckOutput`'s own table gained a `"symlink"` case directly against the unexported function, for the same reason its existing cases check small boundary values directly rather than through a multi-megabyte real file.

**4. The syscall denylist was narrower than the attack surface a sandboxed media decoder actually has.** Widened `deniedSyscalls` with the newer mount API (`fsopen`, `fsconfig`, `fsmount`, `fspick`, `move_mount`, `open_tree`, `mount_setattr` - the same concern `mount`/`umount2` already covered, before the kernel grew a second way to do it), a set of legacy or privileged-only calls with no legitimate reason to run inside a media transcode (`modify_ldt`, `uselib`, `syslog`, `acct`, `quotactl`, `swapon`, `swapoff`, `reboot`, `iopl`, `ioperm`, `chroot`, `fanotify_init`), ways to turn a file descriptor or handle back into information about the host (`kcmp`, `name_to_handle_at`, `open_by_handle_at`, `lookup_dcookie`), and ways to change the system clock (`clock_settime`, `clock_adjtime`, `settimeofday`, `adjtimex`). Deliberately left out: anything NUMA-related, and anything glibc or one of ffmpeg's own linked libraries (of which, per the package doc comment above, there are a great many on this build) might reach during ordinary startup - this list only grows from a syscall the real integration tests below still pass with it denied, never from a guess at what "looks dangerous."

**Tests.** `TestSeccompInstructions_KillsEveryDeniedSyscallOnTheNativeArch` iterates the list itself, so every addition is proven killed (and every name proven to resolve on this architecture at all - an unrecognised name fails the whole policy to assemble, which this test would also catch) without a matching line added by hand. Every real, skip-if-unavailable integration test in `transcode` (the full list is in the entry above) was re-run after widening the list and still passes, confirming none of the added names collides with anything ffmpeg itself, or a library it links, actually calls doing real work.

**5. Nothing serialised jobs: two callers could run two sandboxed ffmpeg processes through the same `Transcoder` at once.** `sandbox`'s own resource limits (`maxCPUSeconds`, `maxAddressSpaceBytes` and the rest, in the entry above) are each sized for one job having the host to itself at its own two threads - not for several sharing them, which would make every one of those limits mean something different than its own justification claims under real concurrent load.

**The fix adds a one-slot semaphore to `Transcoder`, held for an entire job.** `slot` is a buffered channel of 1; `acquire` (called first thing in `run`, before `Available` or `Run`) takes the one token, blocking until it is free or `ctx` ends first, and a deferred `release` puts it back once `checkOutput` has run, win or lose. A caller whose own `ctx` ends while still waiting for the slot gets `ErrTimeout`: the same sentinel a job that ran out of time once it had actually started already returns, since both describe the same thing to a caller - this call did not finish in the time it was given - and nothing a caller would do differently for "waited too long for a turn" versus "ran too long once it got one" justifies two different sentinels to check with `errors.Is`.

**Tests.** `TestTranscoder_RunsOneJobAtATime` uses a new fake, `blockingRunner` (`transcode`'s own `helpers_test.go`), whose `Run` does not return until the test lets it: a first job (`Video`) is started in a goroutine and left blocked inside `Run`; a second job (`Audio`, a different profile, to prove the slot is shared across all five rather than only repeats of one) given a context that ends in 100ms reports `ErrTimeout` well before that deadline's own ceiling, proving it never got anywhere near `Run` at all; releasing the first job and then running a third against the same `Transcoder` succeeds promptly, proving the slot was actually freed rather than merely never granted a second time by accident. Run with `-race`.

## `ActionKillProcess`, and `kill -XFSZ` in a test, both reached the desktop as a "Process crashed" notification

Fixing the seccomp bypass above (finding 1 of the review two entries up) by switching to `ActionKillProcess` seemed like the obvious choice at the time: a transcode job reaching a denied syscall is already behaving nothing like ffmpeg, so ending it outright reads as the more decisive, more obviously correct response than merely returning an error code and hoping ffmpeg's own error handling does something sane with it. It is also wrong, for a reason that has nothing to do with sandboxing at all: `SIGSYS` - the signal seccomp's kill actions use - is one of the signals whose default disposition is to core-dump, and a desktop running `systemd-coredump` as its `core_pattern` handler turns every core-dumping signal into a "Process crashed" notification, confirmed directly - `coredumpctl list` showed entries for `/usr/bin/unshare` (`SIGSYS`, from the real seccomp-enforcement test) and `/usr/bin/bash` (`SIGXFSZ`, from a unit test simulating what prlimit's own `--fsize` limit would do) every single time either test suite ran. Thirteen such entries turned up in one ten-minute stretch of ordinary test iteration - this was never a rare edge case, it was happening on every run.

**The first attempt - `prlimit --core=0` - does not fix it, and that is worth recording so it is not tried again as the whole fix.** `--core` bounds how large a core file may be *written*; confirmed directly, with `--core=0` in place, a process the kernel still delivers a core-dumping signal to still makes `systemd-coredump` log the event (`COREFILE` reads `none` rather than `present`), and the crash notification still appears for that log entry regardless of whether any data was actually saved. The piped `core_pattern` handler is invoked by the signal itself, not by whether a core file will end up on disk.

**The actual fix avoids the signal arriving at all.** `env --ignore-signal=XFSZ,XCPU` (GNU coreutils; resolved through `lookPath`, the same as `bwrap` and `prlimit`) becomes the outermost command in `buildArgv`'s own chain - `env ... -- prlimit ... -- bwrap ... -- <binary>` - setting both signals' disposition to `SIG_IGN` before anything else in the chain execs. A signal set to `SIG_IGN`, unlike a caught one, stays `SIG_IGN` across every `execve()` in the chain (POSIX), confirmed directly down to the final sandboxed process's own `/proc/self/status` (`SigIgn` showing both bits set). With `SIGXFSZ` ignored, a write past prlimit's own `--fsize` limit returns `EFBIG` to the write() call instead of killing the process - confirmed directly: `dd` writing past a 10 MiB test limit printed "File too large", exited 1, and left a file at exactly 10485760 bytes, with zero new `coredumpctl` entries. With `SIGXCPU` ignored, the *soft* CPU limit's own repeating warning signal is silently dropped and the process simply keeps running past it (as intended - `maxCPUSeconds` is a hard ceiling, not a target); the *hard* CPU limit is still enforced by an unconditional, uncatchable `SIGKILL` the kernel sends regardless of any ignore-signal setting, and `SIGKILL` does not core-dump by default, so nothing needed there. `--core=0` is kept anyway, as a backstop for the one case nothing above touches: a genuine crash in ffmpeg itself (`SIGSEGV`, `SIGABRT` and the like), which still reaches the desktop as a notification - that one is a real crash, not a limit or a filter this package set, and is the only case left where there was nothing to avoid, only a core file left pointlessly behind to also refuse.

**`Result.TooLarge` is re-derived from what the job leaves behind, since it can no longer be derived from how it died.** `classifyWaitErr` lost its own `SIGXFSZ`-detection branch entirely (there is no longer a signal to detect) and now just returns the process's own exit code, trusting `*exec.ExitError.ExitCode()`'s own documented `-1` for a signalled process rather than inspecting `syscall.WaitStatus` by hand. `outputReachedSizeLimit` (new, `process.go`) instead walks the job's own output directory once the job has already failed and reports whether any regular file there (`Lstat`-equivalent via `os.DirEntry.Info()`, so a symlink is judged by its own size, never dereferenced) has reached `maxOutputFileBytes` - confirmed, from the `dd` test above, that the file this leaves behind lands at exactly the limit, so no tolerance margin was needed. `wait` only runs this check when the job's own exit code is non-zero: a successful job's output legitimately reaching this size is not evidence of anything.

**A second, independent fix bounds `transcode`'s own worst case so the limit above is rarely worth reaching at all.** `Video`'s and `Animation`'s `-crf 23` setting has no bitrate ceiling of its own - real, ordinary footage compresses well under one, but the highest-motion, least compressible input a sender could construct does not have to. `videoRateControlArgs` (new, `transcode/args.go`) adds `-maxrate 2500k -bufsize 5000k`, the VBV constraint x264 enforces by spending extra quantisation rather than ever exceeding it, to both profiles. The arithmetic this bounds the worst case to: `videoDurationSeconds` (600s) × (`maxVideoBitrateKbps` 2500 kbit/s + Video's own fixed 96 kbit/s AAC) ÷ 8 ≈ 194.7 MB, comfortably under Video's own 200 MiB `maxOutput`; Animation's own worst case (no audio track) is lower still, which is why it now reuses Video's cap directly (`animationMaxOutputBytes = videoMaxOutputBytes`) rather than keeping a separate, smaller number that this same change would otherwise have made too tight.

**Tests.** The now-pointless `kill -XFSZ`-based test is gone; `TestRun_ReportsTooLargeWhenOutputReachedTheSizeLimit` instead pre-creates a sparse file (`os.Truncate`, never actually writing `maxOutputFileBytes`'s worth of real data) at exactly the limit alongside a failing fake command, with `TestRun_NotTooLargeWhenOutputIsSmallerThanTheLimit` and `TestRun_NotTooLargeWhenTheJobSucceededEvenIfTheOutputIsLarge` covering the two ways that must *not* count. `TestOutputReachedSizeLimit_MissingDirectory` and `..._IgnoresASubdirectory` cover `outputReachedSizeLimit`'s own edge cases directly. `TestResolveTools_PartialFailure` became a table test over all three tools `env`, `bwrap` and `prlimit` can each independently fail to resolve, after the new `env` lookup going first silently stopped the old, single-case version from ever reaching the other two. `video_test.go`/`animation_test.go`'s own exact-argv checks gained the two new flags; every real integration test in both packages was re-run after every change above and still passes.

**Verification.** `coredumpctl list --since=-10min` was checked immediately before and immediately after one full `-race` run of every test in both `sandbox` and `transcode` (67 tests, real `bwrap`/`prlimit`/seccomp and real `ffmpeg` paths included, nothing skipped): no coredump entries either time.

## The sandbox bounds what ffmpeg can reach, not what it can claim to have written; `transcode` now parses and rebuilds the output itself

The sandbox work above closes how far a hostile media file can take ffmpeg - no network, no filesystem but its own input and output directory, a bounded syscall set, bounded CPU/memory/time. It does nothing about what happens after ffmpeg exits. `checkOutput` only ever asked whether a regular, non-empty, non-oversized file existed at the fixed path a profile's own arguments named; it never asked whether that file's *contents* were the kind of thing ffmpeg's own arguments should have produced. A sandboxed ffmpeg that is fully compromised - not merely fed a file it mishandles, but taken over outright by an attacker with arbitrary code execution inside the sandbox - can still write anything at all to `sandbox.OutputDirPath`: there is nothing about running inside bwrap that stops a process from writing a crafted MP4, or any other file, as its own "re-encoded" output. `checkOutput`'s checks would all still pass, and the shell would then open that file exactly as if ffmpeg itself had produced it honestly.

**The pattern every other project in this position uses closes this by returning decoded data, never a file the client must parse.** Chromium's and Firefox's sandboxed decoder processes, and GNOME glycin's bwrap-sandboxed image loaders, all hand back pixels or samples, not a container for something else to open. QML's `MediaPlayer` and `SoundEffect` cannot play raw decoded frames or samples directly, so this package's own version of "return data, not a file" is: for audio, have ffmpeg emit PCM and have the helper itself be the only thing that ever writes the WAV container the shell opens; for video, let ffmpeg produce an MP4 as before, but treat it as only an *intermediate* - parse it, validate every structural claim it makes against exactly what this package's own fixed ffmpeg arguments should produce, and have the helper rebuild a brand-new MP4 from the validated pieces. Either way, a fully compromised ffmpeg can still reach only Qt's own H.264 and AAC decoders (for video) or nothing that parses a container at all (for audio), because the container the shell actually opens is always one this helper wrote itself from values it already checked, never a file copied through from the sandbox. That residual reach into Qt's own decoders is accepted, not closed any further: narrowing it the same way again would mean sandboxing Qt's own playback itself, out of scope here.

**Audio and Sound: ffmpeg returns PCM; the helper writes the WAV file itself.** `audioArgs`/`soundArgs` (`transcode/audio.go`, `transcode/sound.go`) now ask ffmpeg for `-af aformat=sample_fmts=s16:channel_layouts=mono|stereo` (a mono source stays mono; anything wider is downmixed to stereo) and `-c:a pcm_s16le -bitexact`, at 24 kHz for Audio and 48 kHz for Sound - uncompressed samples, nothing left for a muxer to get creative with. `wav.go` (new) then reads only the handful of fields a canonical PCM WAV needs from that intermediate: the RIFF/WAVE magic, a `fmt ` chunk declaring PCM, 1 or 2 channels, exactly the sample rate the profile asked for and 16 bits, then a `data` chunk whose own length is checked against the file's actual size (never trusted past where the file really ends), is non-zero and is a whole number of sample frames. A `LIST` or `fact` chunk between `fmt ` and `data` is skipped, up to four of them; anything else - a second `data` chunk, any chunk this package has no reason to ever see from its own fixed ffmpeg invocation - is refused outright, same as a wrong sample rate, wrong bit depth or wrong channel count. The helper then writes its own fresh 44-byte canonical header built only from those validated values, followed by the data chunk's own bytes copied through with `io.CopyN` rather than read into memory at once, and removes the intermediate. Audio's own output cap rose from 10 MiB to 64 MiB to fit a full ten minutes of stereo PCM (57.6 MB exactly, at 24 kHz); Sound's existing 8 MiB cap already covered 30 seconds of stereo PCM at 48 kHz (5.76 MB) with room to spare.

**Video and Animation: ffmpeg's MP4 is an intermediate; the helper parses, validates and rebuilds it with `github.com/Eyevinn/mp4ff` (MIT, v0.61.0).** Two flags were added to both profiles' own fixed ffmpeg arguments: `-bf 0` (no B-frames, so decode order is always presentation order and no sample ever needs a composition-time offset) and `-level:v 4.0` (an explicit ceiling, matching what this package now also checks on the way back in). Checked directly: with these settings, libx264 writes no Access Unit Delimiter NAL at all - that is already its default behaviour, nothing in this package's own arguments has to ask for it - but does still write one SEI NAL, carrying its own version-identification string, attached to the very first sample of every file. `remux.go`, `remuxsamples.go` and `remuxbuild.go` (new) make that detail moot either way: every sample's own NAL units are walked and only slice data (type 1 or 5) is ever kept, with type 6 (SEI) and type 9 (AUD) dropped and anything else at all - another in-band SPS or PPS included - refused outright.

Before any sample is even read, the file's own track structure is validated: exactly one `avc1` video track, whose `avcC` has exactly one SPS and one PPS at NAL length size 4 (`mp4ff`'s own decoder already refuses any other length size while parsing the file at all); the SPS, parsed with `mp4ff`'s own `avc` package, must be AVC Main profile, level 4.0 or below, 4:2:0, 8-bit, frame-only, with even dimensions no larger than 1280 on either side and equal to the sample entry's own declared width and height. Video allows at most one `mp4a` audio track; Animation allows none at all. A permitted audio track's `esds` must declare AAC-LC per its own `AudioSpecificConfig`, at 44.1 or 48 kHz with 1 or 2 channels, agreeing with the sample entry's own declared rate. Per sample: a nonzero duration, no composition-time offset (an all-zero `ctts`, or none at all, is accepted; a nonzero one is refused), a size within a named cap (4 MiB for one video sample, 32 KiB for one AAC frame), a sample count within a profile's own duration cap times its own frame rate plus a little slack, and - for the very first video sample only - an IDR among what the NAL filter kept. Any failure, anywhere, including a panic while parsing (not expected of `mp4ff`, but not trusted either), is recovered and reported as `ErrFailed`; none of this package's own four sentinel errors ever carries the reason across the protocol boundary.

What survives validation is written to a brand-new file: `ftyp` (`isom`/`iso2`/`avc1`/`mp41`), `moov` before `mdat` (faststart), `mvhd`/`tkhd`/`mdhd` with timescales and durations computed from the validated samples, a fresh `avc1`/`avcC` built from the validated SPS and PPS bytes (never copied from the file being rebuilt), `stss` listing exactly the samples the NAL filter found an IDR in, and, for audio, a fresh `mp4a`/`esds` built from the validated `AudioSpecificConfig` - no `udta`, `meta` or edit list of any kind. Sample data is read through `mp4ff`'s own lazy-mdat decoding and copied by offset and size, one sample at a time, to a scratch file on disk; only once every sample's own final size is known does the new file's own `moov` get built and its one chunk per track get its real offset, so the rebuilt file's own byte layout is never held in memory as a whole either, before the scratch file's own bytes are copied into the new `mdat` and removed.

**A follow-up review found mp4ff's own moov decoding bypasses the lazy-mdat mode entirely.** `mp4.WithDecodeMode(mp4.DecModeLazyMdat)` only ever skips `mdat`'s own payload; `DecodeMoov` still reads a moov box's entire body into memory regardless, to decode its own sample tables, and mp4ff's own per-table checks (`stsz`'s own declared sample count against its own box size, for example) bound each table to its own box, never the box itself. A hijacked ffmpeg could write an intermediate whose moov alone fills most of `checkOutput`'s own 200 MiB cap, costing the helper hundreds of megabytes before any check above - `classifyTracks` included - ever runs.

**The fix walks the file's own top-level boxes by hand before `mp4.DecodeFile` ever sees it.** `checkTopLevelBoxes` (new, `remux.go`) reads only each top-level box's own header with `mp4.DecodeHeader` and seeks past its body without ever reading it. It refuses more than one `moov` box, a `moov` over 2 MiB, more than eight top-level boxes in total (an ordinary faststart file has four: `ftyp`, `moov`, `free`, `mdat`), or any box whose own declared size reaches past the file's actual end - checked entirely in `uint64`, since `mdat`'s own large-size form lets a box claim any 64-bit value, and converting that down to a signed one first could wrap negative and let a too-large claim slip past a plain comparison instead of being caught by it. The 2 MiB moov cap comes from a direct measurement: Video's own worst case - ten minutes at 1280x720/30fps with 48 kHz AAC, the longest and most densely sampled file this package's own ffmpeg arguments ever legitimately produce - has a 519,147-byte moov; 2 MiB leaves roughly 4x that as headroom.

**Tests.** `TestCheckTopLevelBoxes` builds its own top-level boxes by hand, one 8-byte header plus filler at a time, and covers an oversized moov, a moov at exactly the cap (accepted), two moov boxes, more top-level boxes than the cap allows, and a box whose own declared size reaches past the real file's end, both as the first box and as a later one. `TestCheckTopLevelBoxes_SeekErrors` drives the same checks through a wrapping `io.ReadSeeker` that fails its own Seek calls on demand, for the three Seek calls a real file essentially never fails. Every existing `transcode` test - the real-ffmpeg integration suite and `FuzzRemux` included - still passes with this check running in front of every `mp4.DecodeFile` call.

**What remains.** A fully compromised sandboxed ffmpeg can still reach Qt's own H.264 and AAC decoders - but only through a container this helper wrote itself from values it already checked, never a byte of ffmpeg's own container. That residual risk is accepted, not closed further here.

**Measured costs.** Audio's own uncompressed PCM at 24 kHz costs about 2.9 MB per minute mono, 5.8 MB per minute stereo; rebuilding the WAV header and copying the data costs a few milliseconds regardless of length (7 ms measured for a full minute of mono). Rebuilding a video's own MP4 costs about 60 ms per minute of 1280x720 footage with one audio track; a full ten-minute clip at Video's own worst-case size (195 MB, close to its 200 MiB cap) took under a second (916 ms measured) end to end.

**Tests.** `wav_test.go` covers `rebuildWAV` and its own helpers directly: valid mono and stereo input, every rejected `fmt` field (wrong tag, rate, bit depth, channel count), a truncated file, a non-RIFF file, a data chunk declared past the file's own end, an odd or non-frame-aligned data length, a `LIST`/`fact` chunk skipped and too many of them refused, an unrecognised chunk refused rather than skipped, and the output-size cap. `remux_test.go`, `remuxsamples_test.go` and `remuxbuild_test.go` build adversarial MP4s with `mp4ff` directly (real SPS/PPS byte pairs generated once with the host's own ffmpeg and hardcoded as fixtures) and cover: a valid file round-tripping with its edit list dropped and SEI/AUD stripped but durations preserved; every refusal above as its own named case; a hand-patched avcC length-size byte; a malformed box size; the field-level checks (`checkSPS`, `checkAudioSpecificConfig`, `filterVideoSample`) directly against hand-built values; and the scratch-file and write-failure paths (`appendSample`, `copyScratch`, `encodeOutput`) through fakes that fail on demand. `FuzzRemux` seeds from a valid fixture and from arbitrary bytes, asserting `remuxMP4` never panics and that its own output, whenever it succeeds, always itself passes the same validator a second time. Real-ffmpeg integration tests (skipped when ffmpeg is unavailable) cover an Opus voice note, an M4A, an MP3 and a 5.1 source for Audio; a rotated clip, an HEVC source, 1080p, a GIF and an animated WebP for Video and Animation - each checked with `ffprobe` for exactly one `h264`/Main-profile video stream, an optional `aac` stream, no other stream of any kind, and no metadata tag beyond the MP4's own mandatory ftyp brand fields. A QML test (`tests/qml/MediaPlayback`) plays two tiny fixtures this exact pipeline produced - a short MP4 with AAC and a short mono WAV - through a real `QtMultimedia` `MediaPlayer` and confirms each reaches `PlayingState` with a position that advances and a duration greater than zero, proving Qt actually plays what this package writes, not only that `ffprobe` can read it.

## Video, voice notes, audio messages and stickers now play through the sandboxed transcoder too; `videosafe`, `audiosafe` and `decodeAs` are deleted outright, not just unwired

"Untrusted video, voice notes, animations and stickers are re-encoded inside a bubblewrap sandbox before the shell ever sees them" (above) built `sandbox` and `transcode` standalone and said plainly that neither was wired into `app`, `server` or a connector yet, and that `videosafe`/`audiosafe` were untouched until "a later change does the wiring and can then delete most of both packages." This is that change. `app` now consumes the sandboxed transcoder through a consumer-owned interface, `media.fetch` gained a `play` param and a `playPath` result, and every Go-side predictor of what FFmpeg's or Qt's own decoders would do with a sender's video or audio bytes - `videosafe`, `audiosafe`, `imagesafe`'s own `AnimatedGIF` sanitizer, and every function that threaded a `decodeAs`/`playable` verdict through the protocol - is deleted, not merely left unreachable.

**The interface, and how release and fake builds each wire it.** `app.Transcoder` (`backend/internal/app/app.go`) is the consumer-owned interface interfaces.md asks for: five methods, `Video`/`Animation`/`Audio`/`Sound`/`StillFrame`, each `(ctx context.Context, in, outDir string) (string, error)` - past the usual one-or-two-method guideline, justified in its own doc comment because each is a genuinely different ffmpeg invocation and output shape, not one method away from being parameterised into fewer. `backend/nofake.go` wires the real thing, `transcode.NewTranscoder(sandbox.NewRunner(workRoot), ffmpeg)`, with `ffmpeg` and `bwrap` resolved once through `exec.LookPath`; `backend/fake.go` (`//go:build fake`) wires a `passthroughTranscoder` whose five methods all just copy the input to a new name under `outDir`, so `make build-fake` and every `app` test exercise the real `Transcoder` interface and its own call sites with no sandbox, no `ffmpeg`, and no `/tmp` involved either way. `workRoot` (`$XDG_DATA_HOME/omamessenger/work`) is removed and recreated at `0700` once, at startup (`main.resetWorkRoot`): every job already cleans up its own job directory as it finishes, but a crash mid-job could leave one behind, and startup is the one point that can be swept without racing a job still running.

**Play copy naming, and why its separator has to be `-` for the same reason `displayCopyID`'s already is.** `app.Commands.playCopy` (`backend/internal/app/play.go`) picks the profile a message's own kind plays through - `Animation` for a video a sending service marked to autoplay silently (`domain.Media.Animated`, a "GIF video"), `Video` for any other video, `Audio` for a voice note or an ordinary audio message, nothing for anything else - then builds the file through `transcodedPlayCopy`, the same `cache.FetchNamed` shape `displayCopy` already uses for a photo or sticker: the id is `cacheIdentity(m) + "-play"`, and the name actually written is that id plus whatever extension the profile's own output turns out to have, read back with `filepath.Ext` after the job runs rather than assumed up front, since a profile's own output container is free to change independently of this package (as, concurrently, one did). The separator is a `-`, never a `.`, for exactly the reason `displayCopyID`'s own `-view` already is (see "A photo or sticker's display copy is built once and served from the cache forever after" above): `cache.findByID` matches a stored file name against an id by requiring the next character after it to be `.`, so a derived copy named `<id>.play.mp4` could shadow a lookup for the bare original id `<id>` instead of the file that is actually at that slot. `TestFetchMedia_PlayCopyNameNeverMatchesTheOriginalsOwnLookup` (`play_test.go`) proves this directly rather than only by the naming rule.

**Nothing about building a play copy is ever an error the UI sees.** A transcode failing, timing out, the transcoder being unavailable (`ffmpeg` or `bwrap` missing from `PATH`), or the message's own claimed duration already over the profile's ten-minute cap (`playDurationCapSeconds`, 600, duplicated from `transcode`'s own limit rather than imported, since `app` cannot reach across the depguard boundary to an unexported constant) all just mean `FetchMedia` returns no `playPath`; each is logged once to a safe failure category only (`play-unavailable`/`play-timeout`/`play-too-long`/`play-failed`, never a path or ffmpeg's own stderr) for `Doctor`'s recent-errors check, and the UI falls back to opening the original externally, the same fallback a video with no `playable` verdict already took. `doctor.playbackCheck` adds a fixed "In-window playback" check, ok or not, from whether both tools resolved at startup - the same shape `Facts.PlaybackAvailable` carries straight through from `main.newTranscoder`'s own lookup, computed once and never re-checked mid-run.

**The protocol.** `media.fetch` gained one optional param, `play` (bool, default `false`), and one optional result field, `playPath`, set only when `play` was `true` and the transcode actually succeeded. `path` keeps exactly its old meaning - the original download for a video, voice note, audio message or file, a display copy for a photo or sticker - and is never what a `MediaPlayer` loads any more. `playable` stays, kept only so the result's shape still carries a boolean, now simply `playPath != ""`. `decodeAs` is gone outright: there is no format a sender's claimed kind can make the UI pick a different decoder for any more, since a video - a "GIF video" included - always gets its play copy from the `Animation` or `Video` profile, never `AnimatedImage`. `server.Protocol` is `4`. `docs/api.md`'s `media.fetch` row, `server/methods.go` and its test were updated in the same change, per protocol.md.

**A sticker or photo format Go cannot decode at all now still gets a display copy.** An animated WebP - the one real case `imagesafe.Reencode` always refused outright, with nothing before this change able to produce anything else for it - falls back, inside `displayCopy`'s own fill (`reencodeForDisplay`, `app/media.go`), to `Transcoder.StillFrame` (one decoded frame, scaled to fit 1024x1024, PNG) exactly when the ordinary re-encode fails with `connector.ErrUnsafeImage` and a transcoder is actually wired in, then re-encodes that one frame through the same `ReencodeImageFile` call every other display copy already goes through - so the file the UI ever loads is still this release's own `Reencode` output, never ffmpeg's frame handed across directly. A failure that is not `ErrUnsafeImage` skips the fallback outright, and the fallback's own failure reports the *original* refusal, not its own: that is the one a caller actually needs to see.

**Copying media, an outgoing attachment's own play copy, and notification sounds all follow the same original-bytes-never-reach-the-UI rule.** `CopyMedia` (`app/mediacopy.go`) now calls `FetchMedia(ctx, messageID, false, true)`, and `copySource` picks `path` for a photo or sticker but `playPath` for a video - never the raw original - so the clipboard never carries anything this helper did not itself re-encode; a video whose play copy could not be built is refused outright (`ErrInvalidInput`), never silently copied as its own unre-encoded original instead. An outgoing attachment gets a play copy the identical way, through the same `playCopy` call `FetchMedia` already makes for an incoming message. A custom notification sound (`app/notificationsound.go`) is re-encoded too, now through `Transcoder.Sound` (uncompressed PCM WAV - Qt's `SoundEffect` only plays that) rather than validated structurally by the now-deleted `audiosafe`: `storeAndTranscodeSound` accepts anything ffmpeg itself can decode, MP3 included (see below), and `errSoundNotPlayable`'s fixed sentence and `soundFilters`' own glob list were both widened from "an Ogg/Opus or M4A file" to name MP3, WAV, Ogg, M4A and FLAC.

**What this deletes, confirmed by grep rather than assumed finished.** `backend/internal/videosafe` and `backend/internal/audiosafe` (`audiosafetest` included) are gone in full; `connector.SanitizeIfGIF`, `VideoPlayable`, `AudioPlayable` and the helpers only they used; `app.DecodeAsForMedia`, `VideoPlayableForMedia`, `AudioPlayableForMedia` and the `videoVerdicts` cache that backed them; `imagesafe`'s `AnimatedGIF` profile, `FormatGIF` and `gif.go`'s own block-level GIF rewriter; and both connectors' GIF-specific image-profile branch (Telegram's `documentImageProfile`, WhatsApp's `imageProfile`), so a real GIF image now routes through `imagesafe.Reencode` exactly like any other sticker or photo, and a "GIF video" routes through the `Animation` transcode profile exactly like any other video. `isGIFImage`/`gifFileName` stay in both connectors unchanged: classification only, choosing `domain.Media.Kind`/`Animated`/`FileName` for routing and labelling, never consulted again once a file is on disk - the same restriction they were already under before this change. `.golangci.yml`'s depguard lost the `videosafe`/`audiosafe`/`audiosafetest` allow-list entries outright and gained `transcode$` where `audiosafe$` used to sit in `app`'s own list; `.testcoverage.yml` lost its now-nonexistent `audiosafetest` exclude path. A repository-wide grep for every one of these names, and for `decodeAs`, after deleting them, found nothing left referencing any of them in `backend/`, `ui/`, `tests/` or `docs/` outside this entry's own prose and the superseded entries' own history below.

**Tests.** `app`'s own `fakeTranscoder` (`transcoder_fake_test.go`) is the one new double every `app` test needing a transcoder now shares - an injectable `err` and a call log, with `StillFrame` always succeeding with a real one-pixel PNG regardless of input, which is why `TestFetchMedia_RemovesAnUnsafeLegacyCacheFileAndRefetches` and its two siblings now set `f.transcoder.err` deliberately, to keep that unconditional success from rescuing a fixture each of those tests means to stay permanently refused. `play_test.go` covers `playCopy` end to end - play only when requested, `Animation` versus `Video` versus `Audio` profile selection, no error surfaced on transcoder failure, transcoding a message's play copy exactly once, an outgoing attachment, the duration cap, play never applying to a photo or sticker, and the naming test above. `media_displaycopy_test.go` gained the `StillFrame` fallback's two tests; `mediacopy_test.go` gained the clipboard side (`TestCopyMedia_WritesAVideosPlayCopyToTheClipboard`, `..._RejectsAVideoWithNoPlayCopyAvailable`); `notificationsound_test.go` was rewritten around the same fake rather than `audiosafetest`'s own fixtures. `server/server_test.go` and `doctor_test.go` cover the protocol and doctor surface; `backend/fake_test.go` (new, `//go:build fake`) covers `newTranscoder`/`passthroughTranscoder`/`passthroughCopy` directly; `main_test.go` covers the startup work-root reset. On the UI side, `ui/lib/Timeline.js` and `Media.js`'s node tests move to `mediaPlayPath`/`mediaPlayable`/`mediaPlayKnown`/`mediaPreparing` and `copyable`'s now-single-argument shape; `InlineVideo`, `FullVideoPlayer`, `PhotoViewer`, `MediaController` and `MessageTimeline`'s own QML tests were each rewritten to assert a play copy, never `path`, is what reaches a `MediaPlayer`'s source; `tests/qml/Flows`' own GIF-autoplay smoke check now waits for the video runtime loader to go active, since there is no `AnimatedImage` left to wait on animating.

## A crafted intermediate's own chunk sizes could force a multi-gigabyte allocation, hide a huge coded picture behind a small cropped one, or put a sender's file name into the sandbox's argv
