A security review of `transcode` and `sandbox`, under the same threat model as the sandbox work above - a fully compromised sandboxed ffmpeg, every byte it writes hostile - found three gaps that model already covers in principle but these checks had missed in practice.

**`wav.go`'s `fmt` chunk size was trusted for an allocation before anything bounded it.** `parseFMTChunk` read the chunk's own 32-bit declared size straight off disk and allocated a buffer of exactly that size before validating anything about it, so a WAV file a few dozen bytes long claiming a `fmt` chunk near 4 GiB made the helper allocate that much before ever reading a byte of it. `maxFMTChunkBody` (64 bytes - a canonical PCM `fmt` chunk is 16, plus at most a `cbSize` extension some encoders add) is now checked first. The same audit found `skipChunk` reading and discarding a skippable chunk's (`LIST`/`fact`) own declared size through `io.CopyN(io.Discard, ...)` rather than seeking past it: bounded today by `checkOutput`'s own whole-file cap running before `rebuildWAV` ever does, but not in principle, and not free. `skipChunk` now takes an `io.Seeker`, not an `io.Reader` - a type-level guarantee it can no longer read a skipped chunk's bytes at all - and seeks past `size` (plus the pad byte an odd size carries) instead; an overrun is caught the moment the next read, ordinarily the following chunk's own header, comes up short, rather than by reading however much a crafted chunk claims.

**A crafted SPS could declare a huge coded picture and crop it down to a small, already-accepted display size.** `mp4ff`'s own `avc.SPS.Width`/`Height` are already cropped to the display size by the time this package ever sees them; `checkSPSDimensions` only ever checked that cropped size, never the coded picture underneath it - the size Qt's own H.264 decoder actually allocates per reference frame, cropping or not. `checkSPSCoded` (new, `avcvalidate.go`) recovers the coded size from the SPS's own crop offsets (`cropUnits`, repeating mp4ff's own unexported formula, since it is not itself exposed) and refuses anything not a whole number of macroblocks, over 1280 on either side, or over level 4.0's own 8192-macroblock `MaxFS`. `checkSPSCrop` refuses any crop on the left or top edge at all, and 16 luma samples or more on the right or bottom - x264 only ever rounds a coded picture up to a whole macroblock there, cropping the remainder off exactly one of those two edges. `checkSPSRefFrames` bounds `num_ref_frames` and, when the VUI declares bitstream restrictions, `max_dec_frame_buffering` (the SPS is now parsed with `parseVUIBeyondAspectRatio` true, where it previously stopped right after the aspect ratio and so never saw this field at all) to 4 - the exact figure x264 writes at this package's own fixed `-preset veryfast -bf 0`, at every resolution these profiles produce, measured directly rather than guessed. `checkPPS` (new) refuses `num_slice_groups_minus1 != 0` directly, rather than relying only on that already being impossible under the Main profile `checkSPSCoding` separately requires. The validated SPS/PPS bytes are still copied through unchanged into the rebuilt file exactly as before - mp4ff cannot write a fresh SPS/PPS of its own - now that every field affecting decoder memory has actually been checked first. `avcvalidate.go` is a new file: the AVC/AAC sample-entry validation this adds pushed `remux_test.go` over this project's 400-line file cap, and the validation itself is a cohesive concern separate from `remux.go`'s own box-walking and container rebuilding.

**`sandbox.Spec.Input` put the caller's own file path into bwrap's argv.** Every other `Spec` field is a literal the caller's own code chose; `Input` was the one exception, carrying an outgoing attachment's real file name or a user-picked notification sound straight into `--ro-bind <path> /work/in`, readable by any local user through `/proc/*/cmdline` for the job's whole life - exactly what the package's own doc comment already says must never happen. `transcode.placeJobInput` (new, `jobinput.go`) now hard-links the caller's input into the job directory under the fixed name `sandbox.InputBaseName` first, falling back to an ordinary copy - capped at 512 MiB, checked by `Stat` before a single byte is read - only once linking has already failed (ordinarily because the two are on different filesystems); that fixed path, never the caller's own, becomes `Spec.Input`. `Spec.validate` now also refuses any `Input` whose base name is not `InputBaseName`, so a future caller cannot reintroduce the leak by mistake even if it forgets to call `placeJobInput` itself. The placed copy is removed immediately after the sandboxed job finishes, success or failure, since nothing past that point still needs it.

**Tests.** `wav_test.go` gained a cap-before-read case for the oversized `fmt` chunk (a fake reader that fails the test if it is ever asked to fill a buffer anywhere near the declared size, rather than refusing the chunk first) and seek-based cases for `skipChunk`, including a multi-gigabyte declared size that completes instantly rather than reading any of it. `avcvalidate_test.go` covers `checkSPS`/`checkPPS` against hand-built `avc.SPS`/`avc.PPS` values for a huge coded size cropped down to 1280x720, a left-edge crop, a top-edge crop, a right crop at and just under the 16-sample cap, too many reference frames (with and without the VUI's own `max_dec_frame_buffering`) and a declared slice group; every existing real-ffmpeg integration test, across every profile and resolution this package produces, still passes against all of these checks together. `jobinput_test.go` covers the hard-link path, the size-capped copy fallback (against a sparse file, so the cap is proven to run before any real read happens), a missing source and an already-existing destination. `transcode_test.go` gained a case confirming `Spec.Input`'s own base name, never the caller's original path, is what reaches the fake runner, and a case confirming the job directory is still removed when the input file cannot be placed at all (so the sandboxed runner is never even reached).

**This supersedes, wholesale, every entry above that names `videosafe` or `audiosafe`.** Both packages are deleted outright, not just superseded in behaviour, so every review fix specific to their own container parsing, avcC/SPS cross-checks, sample-table consistency, Ogg/MP4/MP3 shape detection or resource-limit handling - "`videosafe` was bounding a video by its container's own dimensions...", "A second SPS inside an avc1 sample's own compressed data...", "A playable MP4/M4A file must now declare exactly the tracks...", "`videosafe`'s in-band sample scan could be made to do near-endless work...", "`videosafe`'s SPS check bound only the cropped size...", "`audiosafe`'s ID3v2 frame walker and FFmpeg's own demuxer...", "`videosafe`'s avcC reader never checked configurationVersion...", "`videosafe` and `audiosafe` judged only moov's trak children...", and "`videosafe` and `audiosafe` read a track's own sample count..." (all above, with their own "Tests." paragraphs) - now describes code that no longer exists, kept only as the record of what the old approach's own gaps were and why it was replaced, exactly as the sandbox entry above already said it would be. "A video's actual bytes decide whether it animates as a GIF," "A video's own container and stream headers are validated before anything plays in-window" and "A voice note's own bytes decide whether it plays in-window" are superseded the same way for their own `decodeAs`/`playable`/`canPlayInline`/`AnimatedImage`-for-GIF-video mechanism specifically - the discipline all three actually established, deciding from a file's own bytes rather than a sender's claimed name or attribute, is exactly what this change keeps, just moved from a Go-side predictor to ffmpeg's own re-encode. "An animated GIF's total decoding work is bounded," "A GIF's own frame delays are rewritten..." and "A GIF's Graphics Control extension..." describe `imagesafe`'s own `AnimatedGIF` profile and `gif.go`, both deleted; a GIF image still reaches `imagesafe.Reencode` like any other sticker, just with no dedicated profile of its own any more, since `Reencode`'s first-frame-only decode already treats every format but the deleted one as a plain still image. "MP3 is never played in-window at all" is reversed outright: ffmpeg decodes MP3 as readily as anything else the `Audio` profile re-encodes, so a shared MP3 (never a voice note - still Opus on both services) now gets a play copy the same as any other audio message. "A message stored before the GIF-classification fix is reclassified by a migration" stays true about the migration itself; only its own mention of `DecodeAsForMedia` and `InlineVideo`'s decode-once-confirmed rendering is superseded.

## An extended WebP's own inner VP8L or VP8 chunk could declare a size its outer VP8X canvas never did

A security review of `imagesafe/reencode.go`'s `reencodeStill` found that golang.org/x/image v0.41.0's own `webp.DecodeConfig`, for an extended (VP8X) WebP, returns only the VP8X chunk's own declared canvas size without ever looking at the inner VP8 or VP8L chunk that actually carries the pixels. `reencodeStill`'s pre-decode check (`withinCaps` against that returned size) therefore saw whatever tiny size the VP8X header claimed, while the real decode that followed - `image.Decode`, inside `decodeImage` - read the inner chunk's own, completely independent declared size instead. A VP8X canvas declaring 1x1 wrapping an inner VP8L chunk declaring 16384x16384 passed the pre-decode check instantly, then made `golang.org/x/image/vp8l`'s own decoder allocate a 4-byte-per-pixel buffer sized for that inner claim - 1,073,741,824 bytes - before `reencodeStill`'s post-decode confirmation (the same `withinCaps` call, now against the real decoded bounds) ever got a chance to reject it. A file barely fifty bytes long did this; `store/thumbsafe.go` re-encodes a stored thumbnail on every read, so a hostile one would pay this cost again on every conversation load.

**The fix: upgrade golang.org/x/image to v0.47.0, which closes exactly this gap upstream.** `go.mod`/`go.sum` now pin v0.47.0 (BSD-3-Clause, same licence, already on `ALLOWED_LICENSES`); `make license-check` passes unchanged. `webp/decode.go`'s own `decode` function, in the non-`configOnly` path only, now peeks a VP8L chunk's own 5-byte header (`vp8l.DecodeConfig` on a `bufio.Reader.Peek`, never a full decode) or reads a VP8 chunk's own frame header before decoding either, and refuses the file outright once its size disagrees with a VP8X canvas already seen - before `vp8l.Decode`'s or `vp8.Decoder.DecodeFrame`'s own pixel-buffer allocation ever runs. `DecodeConfig` itself (the `configOnly` path `reencodeStill`'s pre-decode check calls) is unchanged and still returns only the VP8X canvas for an extended WebP - the fix is entirely in the path that matters, the one that actually allocates and decodes pixels. No change was needed in this package's own code: `reencodeStill` already calls `image.Decode` (never a WebP-specific entry point of its own) for the confirmation decode every format goes through, so the upgrade alone closes the gap for every caller.

**Why no defence-in-depth check of this package's own, on top of the library fix.** Catching a VP8X/inner-chunk mismatch here directly would mean this package parsing the RIFF container and the VP8X chunk itself - exactly the hand-written chunk walking this package's whole design (see its package doc comment) exists to avoid, in favour of decoders the standard toolchain already maintains. A second, narrower parser here would duplicate the exact logic just fixed upstream, with its own chance of the same class of bug, for no caller this package has that the library's own fix does not already cover. This relies on the library fix alone, deliberately.

**Tests.** `TestReencode_RejectsWebPInnerChunkLargerThanDeclaredCanvas` (`reencode_test.go`) hand-builds the minimal crafted file: a VP8X chunk declaring a 1x1 canvas (`vp8xPayload`) wrapping a VP8L chunk (`buildVP8LPayload`, new in `helpers_test.go`) whose own bitstream header declares 16384x16384, built bit by bit with a small `vp8lBitWriter` since golang.org/x/image has no WebP encoder at all to build one with directly - trivial, one-symbol Huffman trees for red, blue, alpha and distance (needing no bits at all to decode, so the file can simply end there) and a real two-symbol tree for green, so the very first pixel the decoder tries to read needs a bit the file never supplies, failing fast rather than running a 16384x16384 decode to completion. Confirmed red first: against the pinned v0.41.0, the test failed on its own allocation ceiling, `Reencode` having allocated 1,073,790,320 bytes before rejecting the file. Pinned at v0.47.0, the same test passes: 12,944 bytes allocated, four orders of magnitude under the test's 8 MiB ceiling. `FuzzReencode` gained the same crafted file as a seed.

## `cache.Sounds` served whatever file sat under a notification sound's fixed name, trusting that only its own transcoder had ever written it

A security review found `app.playableSoundPath` assumed a file at `cache.Sounds.Path(key)` was always this helper's own transcoded WAV output, confirming only that the file existed (`os.Stat`) before handing its path straight to the UI's `SoundEffect`. That assumption held for the released code's own write path, but not for every build that had ever run on a developer's machine: git history of `sounds.go`/`notificationsound.go` shows `Sounds.Path` has named a sound store file `<key>.sound` since the feature's very first commit (`40ef1db`), including the version before the sandboxed-transcoder rewrite, whose own `storeAndValidateSound` copied a user's raw chosen file into that exact path first and only ran `audiosafe.Validate` against the copy already sitting there - never re-encoding it. An early development build (never released, but installed while this feature was being built) could leave that raw, unvalidated file behind under the same name a later, upgraded build still resolves to and trusts by name alone.

**The fix: rename the one file this code ever writes, and sweep away anything else.** `cache.Sounds.Path` now names a stored sound `<key>.wav`, not `<key>.sound` - a name no build before this one, released or not, ever wrote, and also an honest one: `Store` only ever receives the sandboxed transcoder's own WAV output (see "Video, voice notes, audio messages and stickers now play through the sandboxed transcoder too" above). `Sounds.Sweep(ctx)` (new) removes every file in the sound store's own directory that is not named `<key>.wav`, called once from `serve`, right after `newMediaCaches` builds the sound store and before `wire`/`srv.Start` let any request reach it - the same ordering the now-deleted `medialegacy.go`'s own one-time sweep used, so there is no window where a stale file could be resolved before the sweep has run. A failed sweep is logged, never fatal: the rename alone already makes an old file impossible to resolve by name, so `Sweep` is defence in depth, not the mechanism the fix actually depends on.

**Tests.** `TestSoundsPath_UsesTheWavExtension` pins the new name directly. `TestSoundsSweep_RemovesAnOldStyleRawFile` plants a file at the old `<key>.sound` name `Store` itself no longer ever writes and confirms `Sweep` removes it; `TestSoundsSweep_KeepsAStoredSound` confirms a sound actually stored through `Store` survives; `TestSoundsSweep_ToleratesAnAreaNeverCreated` and `TestSoundsSweep_RefusesACancelledContext` mirror `cache.Outgoing`'s own sweep tests. `TestResolveNotificationSound_NeverServesAnOldStyleRawFileLeftUnderTheSameKey` (`notificationsound_test.go`) reproduces the actual bug end to end without needing any of this package's private naming scheme: it resolves a real, freshly transcoded sound, derives the sibling legacy path by swapping the resolved path's own extension for `.sound`, writes unvalidated raw bytes there, and confirms a second resolve still returns the original transcoded path and content, unchanged. Run against the pre-fix code, where both paths were identical (the extension swap landed back on the same file), this test failed, reading back the raw bytes it had just written over the transcoded one; after the fix it passes.

## A WhatsApp sender's own encrypted message content could make a mention or a reply reference an implausibly long id

A security review found `domain.CapMentions` already bounded how many mentions a message carries, and each one's own `Name`, but never its `UserID`; `domain.SanitizeIncoming` capped a reply quote's `SenderName` and `Text` but never its `RemoteID`. Both ids are read straight out of a WhatsApp message's own end-to-end encrypted content - `ContextInfo.MentionedJID` for a mention, the quoted message's own `ContextInfo.StanzaID` for a reply - which the service itself never inspects or bounds, and whatsmeow's own `types.ParseJID` (confirmed in the module cache) just splits the string on `@` and keeps the user part exactly as given, with no length check of its own. A message with 64 mentions, each carrying a 64 KB `UserID`, becomes around 4 MB before JSON encoding and around 24 MB after - Go's own `encoding/json` escapes `<` as `<` - on one stdout line the UI's `SplitParser` buffers whole and `JSON.parse`s on its own thread; and since `store.scanMessage` already calls `SanitizeIncoming` on every read, a row stored this way keeps re-sending that payload on every later `messages.list` page and edit, not only once on arrival.

**The fix: one cap for every id that is not itself display text.** `domain.MaxRemoteIDLength` (64 bytes) and `domain.CapRemoteID` drop an id past it rather than truncating - a cut id could read as a different, shorter real id it was never actually sent as, while a cleared one can never be mistaken for one, the same reasoning `CapURL` already applies to an oversized link. 64 bytes is sized from what a real id ever is, not a round number: a WhatsApp JID (`user@server`) is a phone number or LID of at most a few dozen digits, plus the longest server name whatsmeow defines (`s.whatsapp.net`, 14 bytes), so it never reaches 40; a Telegram remote id (`user:<id>:<accessHash>`, both `int64`, see `connector/telegram`'s own `remoteID`) never reaches 50; and a WhatsApp poll option's own id - hex of the SHA-256 hash its vote protocol identifies an option by - is exactly 64. `CapMentions` now clears an oversized `UserID` the same way `CapSpans` already clears an oversized span `URL`, keeping the rest of the mention: `Name`, `Offset` and `Length` still describe real, already-rendered text. `SanitizeIncoming` clears an oversized `ReplyTo.RemoteID` the same way, leaving `SenderName` and `Text` as a quote still worth showing even once it can no longer be resolved or linked back to the message it names - `store.resolveReply` already treats an empty `RemoteID` as nothing to resolve, so this introduces no new lookup or collision.

**Two lower-confidence but cheap gaps from the same review, bounded the same way.** `connector/whatsapp/react.go`'s `reactTo` keys its own per-message reaction cache (`c.reactions`) on `conversationRemoteID + "/" + messageRemoteID`; for a live reaction, `messageRemoteID` is `ReactionMessage.Key.GetID()`, read from the reacting sender's own message with no length check of its own. An implausibly long one is now refused before it is ever tracked, the same treatment an oversized reaction emoji already got. `connector/whatsapp/pollstore.go`'s `putPoll` and `setVotes` wrote every option a poll creation message claimed, and every option id a decrypted vote claimed to select, straight into this account's own SQLite database before `domain.CapMedia`/`CapPoll` ever got a chance to bound either on the way back out: a poll creation message's own option count, and a vote's own selected-option count, both carry no limit of their own on the wire (`vote.go`'s `handlePollVote` hex-encodes whatever byte strings `PollVoteMessage.SelectedOptions` claims, never provably the 32-byte hash a real vote always hashes to). Both functions now bound their own insert loop to at most `domain.MaxPollOptions` rows, and skip any option id past `domain.MaxRemoteIDLength`, before the loop starts, rather than relying on a cap that only ever ran on the way out.

**Telegram was audited against the same review and does not share either gap.** Its own mention `UserID` (`connector/telegram/mentions.go`) and reply `RemoteID` (`normalize.go`) are built from `fmt.Sprintf`/`strconv.Itoa` over `int64`/`int` values, never a free-form string a sender's own client could stretch, and it keeps no on-disk vote cache of its own for `MaxRemoteIDLength` to guard.

**Tests.** `domain/limits_test.go` gained `TestCapMentions_ClearsAnOversizedUserID`, the `TestCapRemoteID_*` pair, and `TestSanitizeIncoming_ClearsAnOversizedMentionUserID`, proving the guarantee through the one function `app.Ingest` calls for both a new message and an edit; `domain/limits_replyquote_test.go` gained `TestSanitizeIncoming_DropsAnOversizedReplyRemoteID`. `store/messages_sanitize_test.go` gained `TestMessage_CapsAStoredOversizedMentionUserIDOnRead` and `..._ReplyRemoteIDOnRead`, proving a row written before this fix - `store.AddMessage` never sanitizes on write, only `scanMessage` does on read - is repaired the same way a legacy sender name or reaction chip already is. `connector/whatsapp/react_test.go` gained `TestReactTo_DropsAnOversizedMessageRemoteID`; `connector/whatsapp/pollstore_test.go` gained `TestPutPoll_BoundsOptionCountBeforeStoring`, `TestPutPoll_DropsAnOversizedOptionID`, `TestSetVotes_BoundsOptionCountBeforeStoring` and `TestSetVotes_DropsAnOversizedOptionID`.

## A second sandbox review found a hijacked job could still fork, fill disk through an unbounded /tmp, or rewrite its own hard-linked input through the output bind

A further review, with an explicit threat model - a crafted file may fully compromise the sandboxed ffmpeg, and the sandbox must still bound what that buys an attacker - found four more gaps, each confirmed for real (never assumed from reading the code) before being fixed.

**1. `RLIMIT_AS` and `RLIMIT_CPU` are limits the kernel charges to one process; a hijacked ffmpeg could fork and give each child its own fresh copy of both.** Neither limit, nor the seccomp denylist above, stopped a process from calling `fork`, `vfork` or `clone` to start an entirely new process - only a new *thread*, which shares its parent's address space and is charged against the same CPU total, has no such gap. Confirmed directly, with a minimal `libseccomp` harness built against this build's own glibc (2.44): a filter allowing everything except these calls still let `pthread_create` succeed, while a plain `fork()` and a raw `clone(SIGCHLD)` (no `CLONE_THREAD`) both failed with `EPERM` as expected.

**The fix extends the seccomp filter with a dedicated set of process-creation rules (`seccomp.go`'s own `processCreationSyscalls`, `cloneRequiresThread`, `clone3Syscalls`), on top of the existing denylist.** `fork` and `vfork` are refused unconditionally, the same as any other entry in `deniedSyscalls`. `clone` is refused only when its own flags argument (arg0 on every architecture this helper ships for) does not carry `CLONE_THREAD` (`0x00010000`, from the kernel's own `include/uapi/linux/sched.h`) - built with `elastic/go-seccomp-bpf`'s own `NamesWithCondtions`/`BitsNotSet`, which the existing denylist never needed. `clone3` gets its own group, refused with `ENOSYS`, not `EPERM` - the one deliberate exception to this file's "EPERM for denied" rule. That distinction turned out to matter directly: confirmed with the same harness that denying `clone3` with `EPERM` makes `pthread_create` fail outright, while `ENOSYS` lets glibc's own fallback retry through the `clone` path above and succeed - glibc added that fallback for a kernel genuinely too old to have `clone3` at all, which is exactly what `ENOSYS` already claims, so `EPERM` never reaches the code path that would retry. With every way to start a second process refused, `maxCPUSeconds` and `maxAddressSpaceBytes` (`limits.go`) now actually bound the whole job, not just whichever process happened to hold the limit - both comments there now say so.

**Tests.** `TestSeccompInstructions_RefusesForkAndVforkOnTheNativeArch`, `..._CloneWithoutThreadFlagIsRefused`, `..._CloneWithThreadFlagIsAllowed` and `..._Clone3IsRefusedWithENOSYS` assemble the real filter and run it through `golang.org/x/net/bpf`'s own software VM, the same way the existing denylist tests do; the conditional ones needed a new packet helper, `seccompDataPacketWithArg0`, to place a 64-bit argument value at the byte offsets `go-seccomp-bpf`'s own `LdHi`/`LdLo` read on a little-endian machine. `TestRun_SeccompDeniesForkingAChildProcess` (`sandbox_test.go`) runs `bash -c 'true & wait'` through the real sandbox - `&`/`wait` needs a real child process, not a thread - and confirms it now fails; every profile's real integration test in `transcode` (each running ffmpeg at `-threads 2`, which needs real threading to still work) was re-run after this change and still passes, confirming the fix refuses process creation without refusing the threading ffmpeg actually needs.

**2. `--tmpfs /tmp` had no size of its own, so it defaulted to half of this machine's own RAM, counted against no limit above.** A hijacked job writing real data into `/tmp` (which none of this helper's own fixed ffmpeg arguments ever ask it to do, but a hijacked process is not bound by that) could fill that much disk within its own wall-clock timeout, unconstrained by `maxOutputFileBytes` or anything else in `limits.go`.

**The fix adds `--size` (bwrap 0.12+) right before `--tmpfs /tmp`.** Confirmed directly, with a 1 MiB cap, that a `dd` writing 2 MiB into the capped `/tmp` gets a short write and nothing more. `maxTmpBytes` (new, `limits.go`) is 16 MiB: generous for whatever a linked library's own startup might still touch, with nothing in any profile's own fixed arguments ever writing real output there.

**Tests.** `TestBuildArgv_FullSpec` now expects `--size 16777216` immediately before `--tmpfs /tmp`.

**3. The whole job directory was bound read-write at `OutputDirPath`, which also exposed the job's own hard-linked input for writing.** Since de6aa40 (see the entry above on `Spec.Input`), the caller's input is hard-linked into the job directory under the fixed name `InputBaseName` before that same directory is bound read-write at `/work/out` - meaning the hard link was reachable there too, read-write, under its own name, through a *different* sandbox path than its own read-only bind at `/work/in`. A fully compromised ffmpeg could `chmod` or overwrite that link - and, since it is a hard link, the bytes of whatever real file on the host it pointed at: a cached download, or a user's own notification-sound file anywhere in their home. The same writable directory also had no cap of its own on how many *new* files a job could create: `--fsize` bounds any one file, not how many a job may create and fill in turn, so a loop of create/write/close could fill the user's disk well within the job's own timeout.

**The fix replaces the whole writable directory with a single, pre-created file.** `sandbox.Spec.OutputDir` is gone; `Spec.Output` names one file, already created by the caller - `transcode`'s own new `joboutput.go` does this for every profile, empty and `0600`, named exactly what that profile's own fixed ffmpeg arguments already write to (`createJobOutput`, called from `run` right after `placeJobInput`). `bwrapArgs` binds it with three flags, not one: `--tmpfs OutputDirPath` first (a fresh, empty, writable tmpfs), then `--bind` the one file into it at its own base name, then `--remount-ro OutputDirPath`. The remount does not touch the file bind already mounted inside it - that mount keeps its own, separate read-write flag - but it does mean nothing else can be created, renamed or removed under `OutputDirPath` at all. Confirmed directly, through a real `bwrap` with this exact sequence: writing to the bound file still works, `touch`ing a second file under the same directory fails with "Read-only file system", and - standing in for the job directory's own hard-linked input living alongside a pre-created output, the exact shape the vulnerability needed - a file in that same host directory but never bound as the output stays byte-for-byte unchanged after the job runs. Disk use for a whole job is now bounded by `--fsize` on that one file (512 MiB), full stop: there is nowhere left for a hijacked process to create a second one.

**Every profile still has to actually work with this.** `StillFrame`'s PNG muxer and `Video`'s/`Animation`'s `+faststart` MP4 rewrite both reopen their own output by name after ffmpeg's first pass; with only the file itself bound, not its directory, that still has to resolve and still has to be writable. Confirmed by re-running every real, skip-if-unavailable integration test in `transcode` (fixtures generated by the host's own ffmpeg, run through the real sandboxed `Transcoder`, checked with `ffprobe`) after the change: all pass, PNG and `+faststart` MP4 included - neither muxer needs to create, delete or rename anything in its own output's directory, only open the existing path and write to the file already there.

**Tests.** `TestBwrapArgs_BindsOutputAsASingleFileUnderAReadOnlyParent` pins the exact three-flag sequence. `TestRun_OutputBindExposesOnlyTheSingleBoundFile` (`sandbox_test.go`) is the real-bwrap test above; its own sandboxed script is built entirely from bash builtins (redirection, `:`, `[`, a plain variable), never an external command like `touch` or `rm`, which the process-creation fix above would now also refuse to fork for a reason this test is not about. `createJobOutput` gained its own direct tests (`joboutput_test.go`): an ordinary create, and the already-taken-name case `O_EXCL` exists for. Every call site across both packages that built a `Spec.OutputDir`/relied on `outputReachedSizeLimit` scanning a directory moved to the single-file shape; `outputReachedSizeLimit` itself is now a plain `Stat` on one known path rather than a directory walk, simpler and more precise than before.

**4. Re-verified the sandbox's other standing promises still hold.** `TestRun_NetworkUnreachable`, `TestRun_FileOutsideBoundPathsUnreadable`, `TestRun_SeccompDeniesUnshare` (the nested-userns refusal) and the foreign-architecture seccomp tests were all re-run, unchanged, after every fix above, and still pass.

**Coverage.** Both packages' coverage gates were re-measured with `bwrap`, `ffmpeg` and `ffprobe` hidden from `PATH` entirely (a symlink farm of `/usr/bin` minus those three, built under `build/` and removed immediately after), so every real-pipeline test in both packages skips rather than runs: `sandbox` held at 91.4%, `transcode` at 90.8%, both still above the 90% gate on the unit and fake-runner tests alone.

## A field gated on its own step or dialog-open Loader was still a live sink while the window itself sat open and unfocused

A follow-up security review (after "The search field and the composer's input exist only while actually being edited" above) found the command palette's search field, the new-chat dialog's contact search, and `AccountSetup`'s and `RenameAccount`'s own per-step fields were all destroyed on close and (for the latter two) gated to the right step, but none of them cared whether the window itself was the active one. A Wayland compositor delivers a clipboard offer to a focused-but-inactive-window `TextInput` exactly as it does to a focused, active one, so alt-tabbing away - another window on top, this one unfocused but still visible - left each of these four fields alive and reading every foreign clipboard change for however long that lasted. The composer and the list search were already safe here by construction: losing the window's own activation already clears Qt's `activeFocus`, which is the one thing their own edit-intent flags (`_writingRequested`/`_editingSearch`) are cleared by. These four had no equivalent flag - only "this step/stage is showing" or "the dialog is open", neither of which reacts to the window losing focus at all - and WhatsApp's connector can put the account-setup fields on screen with no user action (a QR re-pairing after the linked device logs out remotely), so this was reachable without the user doing anything either.

**The fix threads `Panel`'s own `windowFocused` (`window.visible && keyArea.Window.active`, already forwarded through `ConversationController.windowActive` to `Overlays.windowActive` for the photo viewer's video player) into a new `windowActive` property on each of the four components, ANDed into whichever Loader already gates their field.** `CommandPalette` and `NewChatDialog` gained an inner field Loader of their own for this - neither needed one before, since the whole component was only ever instantiated already open; `AccountSetup`'s `credentialsLoader`/`answerLoader` and `RenameAccount`'s `aliasLoader` just gained `&& root.windowActive` alongside their existing step condition. None of the four *overlays themselves* - `Overlays.qml`'s own outer Loader for each - gate on `windowActive`: the dialog stays open exactly as asked, the same as the reaction picker, the close question and every other non-field overlay already do regardless of window focus; only the one field inside each is destroyed and recreated.

**What the user typed, and which of several fields they were in, both have to survive a round trip through "destroyed".** Each component already had, or gained, its own persistent draft property outside the field itself, the same reasoning `Composer.text`/`ListColumn`'s own `query` already document: `CommandPalette._query`, `AccountSetup._apiIdDraft`/`_apiHashDraft`/`_answerDraft`, `RenameAccount._aliasDraft` (seeded once from `currentAlias` as a plain property default rather than an explicit reset elsewhere, since this whole component is itself rebuilt fresh for every rename session - see "An overlay with an editable field is destroyed on close, not just hidden" above). `NewChatDialog` needed no new property at all: its search text already lived in `DialogController.query`, round-tripped through `queryEdited`/`query` exactly as before. Each field mirrors every keystroke into its own draft (`onTextChanged`) and seeds from it on creation (`text: root._xDraft`), so a window-focus blip destroying the field loses nothing - the draft, not the field, was always what actually held it. `AccountSetup.submit()` and `RenameAccount`'s save path now read these drafts directly rather than the live field's own `.text`, the same "the controller/overlay, not the field, holds the truth" principle, so submitting never depends on the field still existing at that exact instant.

**Focus is restored only to whichever field actually had it, never to the first one a step happens to show.** `AccountSetup`'s credentials step has two fields; blurring one to Tab into the other, or to click away, must not have the wrong one - or any field at all - stolen back once the window reactivates. Each tracked field's own `onActiveFocusChanged` sets a private marker the moment it *gains* focus (`AccountSetup._focusedField`: `"apiId"`/`"apiHash"`/`"answer"`/`""`; `RenameAccount._aliasHadFocus`; `CommandPalette._fieldHadFocus`; `NewChatDialog._searchHadFocus`), and clears it on a real blur - but not when the blur is itself a side effect of the window going inactive: by the time a field's own `onActiveFocusChanged(false)` runs for that reason, `windowActive` has already landed at `false` (the same property change that just deactivated the Loader destroying it), so each handler reads that first and leaves the marker alone exactly then. `onWindowActiveChanged`, deferred with `Qt.callLater` the same way `AccountSetup._focusStep` already was (so the Loader has already recreated the item by the time it runs), restores focus only when the marker still names a field that exists.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs` gained `CommandPalette.qml` and `NewChatDialog.qml` to `SELF_GATED_FILES` (both now gate their one field with their own inner Loader, needing no caller-side allow-list entry) and a new check, generic over whatever `SELF_GATED_FILES` holds rather than a fixed list of four names: every self-gated field not already covered by `EDIT_INTENT_FLAGS` (the composer and the search field, proven safe by `activeFocus` alone - see above) must have its own field-holding Loader's `active` binding name `windowActive`, checked Loader by Loader (`fieldLoaderActiveBindings`) so a file with two gated fields, like `AccountSetup`, cannot pass on only one of them being fixed; a future fifth overlay added to `SELF_GATED_FILES` is covered by this the moment it is added there. Confirmed to fail, for the right reason, against each of the four pre-fix `active:` bindings in turn, restored after each.

A new `tests/qml/CommandPalette` and extensions to `tests/qml/NewChatDialog`, `tests/qml/AccountSetup` and `tests/qml/RenameAccount` each drive their own field with `windowActive` as a fixed input property - the layer this behaviour belongs to, per ui-testing.md's own rule that a component test takes its input as given rather than recomputing it: type into the field, flip `windowActive` false and confirm the field (and, for `AccountSetup`, *only* that field) is gone without the dialog itself closing, flip it back true and confirm the same text and real keyboard focus return, deferred past the same `Qt.callLater` turn the production code itself uses. `AccountSetup`'s own addition also proves the negative: moving focus to the dialog itself first, a window toggle leaves it there rather than pulling either credentials field back. Each QML addition was confirmed to fail, for the right reason ("… still exists with the window inactive"), against the pre-fix `active:` binding it covers, then restored.

## Parallel photo decodes had no shared memory budget, and a spammed notification sound re-transcoded on every message

A security review of memory exhaustion against the desktop session - the helper runs as a child of `omarchy-shell`, so it degrading or crashing the shell it lives in is in scope the same way a crash of the shell itself is - found two gaps, confirmed by measurement rather than assumed.

**1. Nothing bounded how many of `imagesafe.Reencode`'s own full decodes could run at once, and `FullImage`'s own caps let a single one of them cost far more than necessary.** `server.Server.Handle` runs every request in its own goroutine, and nothing between it and `imagesafe.Reencode` ever limited concurrency; the UI auto-fetches every photo as it scrolls into view, so ten or twenty hostile photos in one conversation meant that many full decodes running in parallel, each one the single largest allocation any request this helper serves makes. A refused display copy made this worse still: `tryDisplayCopy` already removes a photo that fails its check, so the exact same photo was re-downloaded and re-decoded from scratch on every subsequent scroll back into view.

`FullImage` itself (`MaxSide: 8192, MaxPixels: 40_000_000`) was also far more generous than any photo a real service actually sends: WhatsApp's own HD photos cap out at 4096 pixels on the long side, and Telegram's HD toggle at 2560, with Telegram's own servers rejecting anything over 5000 pixels on either side outright. Measuring `runtime.MemStats` (GC disabled for the measured call, so nothing freed mid-decode could hide from `HeapAlloc`'s own delta - a deliberately pessimistic ceiling, not a typical-case average) against real, `cjpeg`/ImageMagick-built fixtures at the *old* cap's own shape (a 4096x4096 baseline JPEG, a 4096x4096 progressive JPEG, and - worse still - an attacker-chosen 4-component progressive JPEG, since nothing stopped one, plus a 4096x4096 16-bit-per-channel RGBA PNG) found a single decode already costing 29 MB, 130 MB, 474 MB and 404 MB respectively at exactly half the old cap's own side length; the old 8192x8192/40-megapixel cap's own worst case was correspondingly close to a gigabyte for one photo alone.

**The fix: one decode slot, a real memory budget from the header alone, a lower `FullImage` cap matching what real services send, and a bounded memory of what was already refused.**

- `imagesafe.FullImage` is now `MaxSide: 4096, MaxPixels: 4096 * 4096` (16.8 megapixels) - generous over both services' own real ceilings, nowhere near the old cap's own worst-case cost.
- `imagesafe.Profile` gained `MaxDecodeBytes` (zero means no limit, the same convention `MaxAspectRatio` already uses), checked in `reencodeStill` from the header alone, before the real decode: `estimatedDecodeBytes` (`reencode.go`) charges every pixel `bytesPerPixelExpensive` (32, calibrated with headroom against the measured peaks above) for a progressive JPEG - found by `jpegIsProgressive`, a real walk to the first SOF marker past every other segment's own declared length, not a guess from scan count - or a PNG whose IHDR declares more than 8 bits per channel, and the far cheaper `bytesPerPixelBaseline` (4) for everything else. `FullImage.MaxDecodeBytes` is `768 << 20`; `Thumbnail.MaxDecodeBytes` is `64 << 20`, since its own existing pixel caps already keep it tiny.
- `app.decodeSlots` is one: `app.Commands.acquireDecodeSlot` reserves it, waiting on `ctx`, around every one of the three full-size re-encode operations - `connector.ReencodeImageFile` (a display copy, wrapped as `Commands.reencodeImageFile`), `SanitizeOutgoingImage` (a pasted image, wrapped as `Commands.sanitizeOutgoingImage`) and `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` (an attached or re-copied photo, wrapped into `Commands.sanitizeAttachedPhoto`, now a method taking `ctx` for exactly this reason) - so only one of this helper's own largest allocations ever runs at a time, whichever of the three it is. One slot, not two: the measured worst case at the new cap already approaches `MaxDecodeBytes` on its own, so a second concurrent slot would roughly double the helper's own worst-case footprint for no benefit the UI's single-column message list ever needs.
- `app.refusedDisplayCopies` (`mediarefused.go`) remembers, per message (keyed by `cacheIdentity`, so an edit that changes a message's media kind is never stuck behind an earlier kind's own refusal), the safe reason category a display copy already failed under - including the one re-download retry `recoverUnsafeImage` already gives it - bounded at 500 entries, oldest dropped first, and cleared on restart like every other in-memory state this helper keeps. `FetchMedia` checks it first, immediately after loading the message, and fails fast with the same `*MediaFetchError` rather than downloading and decoding the same refused photo again on every scroll.

**2. A service's or the global default's notification sound was re-transcoded through the sandbox on every notification, and two concurrent transcodes of the same key could corrupt each other's output.** `resolveServiceSound` calls `storeAndTranscodeSound` on every `notification.resolveSound`, and that call ran the sandboxed transcoder's `Sound` profile unconditionally - occupying `transcode.Transcoder`'s own single job slot - even when the manifest path it was given had not changed since the last notification. A contact spamming messages therefore stalled this helper's one video or voice playback behind a sound transcode that had already been done. Separately, `cache.Sounds.Store` and `cache.Outgoing`'s own `copyToFile` both write through a `.part` file named only from the destination path - no caller-supplied uniqueness of any kind - so two `Store` calls for the exact same key running at once (two notifications resolving the same service default concurrently, say) could both be mid-copy to that identical path, each with its own file offset, corrupting whichever one lost the resulting rename race.

**The fix: remember the last transcoded source per key, serialise around it, and lock `Sounds.Store` itself by key too.** `app.transcodedSoundCache` (`notificationsound.go`) remembers, per sound-store key, the `(path, size, modification time)` `storeAndTranscodeSound` last transcoded successfully; a key's own lock (not Go's own reference-counted map growth this needs, since the key space here - one id per conversation with a custom sound, plus one per service - is small and user-chosen, never a sender's) is held across the whole check-then-maybe-transcode-then-record sequence, so two concurrent resolves for the same key never both decide "needs a transcode" before either one finishes: whichever arrives second simply waits, then almost always finds the first call's own work already covers it. The destination file's own existence is checked too, not just the source's identity, so a sound removed out from under this cache - `ResetNotificationSound`, or the store wiped by hand - is retranscoded rather than trusted by a stale match. Independently, `cache.Sounds` gained its own per-key lock (`lockKey`), used by `Store` directly: two `Store` calls for the same key now simply never run at once, regardless of which caller reaches them or whether it goes through the new cache above, closing the shared-`.part`-file race at the one place both known callers of `Store` actually meet.

**Tests.** `reencode_test.go`: `TestReencode_RejectsOverDecodeByteBudget` (and a `runtime.MemStats`-checked proof the rejection costs no real allocation), `TestReencode_ZeroMaxDecodeBytesMeansNoLimit`, `TestReencode_ProgressiveJPEGCostsMoreThanBaselineAtTheSameSize` (a real, genuinely progressive fixture against a baseline one of the exact same size) and `TestReencode_SixteenBitPNGCostsMoreThanEightBitAtTheSameSize`. `app`: `TestAcquireDecodeSlot_SerializesCallers` and `..._RefusesAnAlreadyCancelledContext` (`decodeslot_test.go`, an internal test: the three re-encode operations have no interface seam to inject a controllable fake through, so the slot's own blocking and release is tested directly rather than through a real, pointlessly slow decode); `TestRefusedDisplayCopies_RemembersAndLooksUp`, `..._EvictsTheOldestPastItsLimit` and `..._RememberingAgainDoesNotDuplicateTheEntry` (`mediarefused_test.go`, same reasoning: 501 real fetches to prove eviction would be slow and indirect); `TestFetchMedia_FailsFastOnASecondFetchAfterARefusedDisplayCopy` proves the integration through the public API instead - a second `FetchMedia` for a message whose display copy already failed costs no further download. `TestResolveNotificationSound_ServiceReusesTheSameTranscodeWhenUnchanged`, `..._ServiceRetranscodesWhenTheFileChanges`, `..._ServiceRetranscodesWhenTheStoredSoundGoesMissing` and `..._ServiceSerializesConcurrentResolves` (twenty real concurrent resolves, asserting exactly one transcoder call - the lock makes this deterministic regardless of scheduling order, not a timing-dependent proof). `cache/sounds_test.go`: `TestSoundsStore_SerializesStoresOfTheSameKey` holds a first `Store` open with a new `blockingReader` fixture until a concurrent second call for the same key is confirmed still waiting, then releases it - proving the lock rather than hoping a raw timing race would have shown corruption on this particular run. Every new or changed test was run with `-race`.

## `FullImage`'s own new, stricter cap also caught every outgoing attachment and paste, which need a different limit entirely

The fix above lowered `imagesafe.FullImage` to 4096 pixels / 16.8 megapixels to bound a *received* photo's own decode cost - correct for that case, since an attacker controls it and the UI fetches it automatically. But `connector.SanitizeOutgoingImage`/`SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` (an attachment or a clipboard paste) and `whatsapp/upload.go`'s own `withinThumbnailSourceCaps` had always shared that same profile, on the reasoning that a photo was a photo regardless of direction. Attaching or pasting is the user's own deliberate choice, made once, already behind `app.decodeSlots`' own serialisation - nothing like a sender's auto-fetched flood - and a real camera or phone photo (24, 48, even 50 megapixels) or a 5K/dual-4K desktop screenshot is routinely *larger* than any photo a messaging service ever sends over the wire. Lowering `FullImage` without giving outgoing its own profile meant none of those could be sent any more, at all - not downscaled, outright refused, since `FullImage` has never resized anything, only accepted or rejected it.

**The fix: a second profile, `Outgoing`, sized for what a camera or a screenshot actually produces, that shrinks the result instead of refusing it.** `imagesafe.Outgoing` (`MaxSide: 12000, MaxPixels: 50_000_000, MaxInputLen: 64 << 20`) accepts what `FullImage` never needed to; `connector.SanitizeOutgoingImage`/`SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` and `whatsapp.withinThumbnailSourceCaps` now check against it instead (the latter for consistency only: by the time whatsapp's own upload path ever sees an attachment, `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile` has already shrunk it, so this never actually catches anything in practice today, but a comment claiming `FullImage`'s own bound while checking something `FullImage` no longer owns would mislead the next person who reads it). `clipboard.MaxPasteLen` (64 MiB, up from 32) now matches `Outgoing.MaxInputLen` exactly - checked by a new test that fails the moment the two drift apart - so a paste of a real screenshot is not cut off by wl-paste's own read cap before `SanitizeOutgoingImage` ever gets a chance to accept it.

`Profile` gained `DownscaleTo`: zero (`FullImage`, `Thumbnail`) keeps the existing behaviour, refuse rather than resize; `Outgoing` sets it to 4096 - WhatsApp's own HD maximum; Telegram serves 2560 - so `reencodeStill`, once a decoded image has already passed every other cap, shrinks it to fit that longer side, aspect ratio kept exactly, before encoding (`downscaleToFit`, `reencode.go`), never enlarging an image already within it. `MaxDecodeBytes` stays the *same* 768 MiB budget `FullImage` uses, deliberately not scaled up for `Outgoing`'s larger pixel caps: almost every real photo is a baseline, 8-bit JPEG, charged at `bytesPerPixelBaseline`, which fits that budget with room to spare even at 50 megapixels (200 MB); only a progressive or 16-bit-per-channel shape anywhere near that size - never produced by an ordinary camera or screenshot - hits it, exactly the hostile case this is supposed to still catch.

**`hasAlpha` moved earlier, before any resize, as a direct consequence.** `downscaleToFit`'s own output is always a generic `*image.RGBA` (the one concrete type `golang.org/x/image/draw` writes into), which would otherwise force `hasAlpha`'s pixel-by-pixel scan - over the resized pixels, not even the original photo's own - for every outgoing photo, including the common case (an opaque JPEG, decoded as `*image.YCbCr`) `hasAlpha`'s own type-switch already answers for free. `encodeImage` now takes that bool as a parameter, computed by `reencodeStill` on the original decoded image before any resize runs, rather than recomputing it itself afterward.

**Measuring which interpolator to resize with.** `golang.org/x/image/draw` offers three: `ApproxBiLinear` ("fast, reasonable speed and quality"), `BiLinear` ("slow, high quality") and `CatmullRom` ("very slow, very high quality") - its own doc comment's words. Timed scaling a real 8160x6120 (49.9 MP) baseline JPEG down to 4096x3072, the shape and ratio this fix's own worst case actually produces: `ApproxBiLinear` 217 ms, `BiLinear` 1.28 s, `CatmullRom` 1.81 s - roughly six and eight times slower respectively, for a quality gap that two further compression steps (this package's own JPEG re-encode at quality 90, then almost always the receiving service's own re-compression) already narrow before anyone ever sees the pixels directly. The deciding factor is not the one photo being resized but every *other* decode waiting behind it: this runs inside `app.decodeSlots`' own single slot, so a kernel interpolator's extra second or more is also a felt delay for an unrelated incoming photo the user is simply scrolling past, not merely a slower encode of this one. `ApproxBiLinear` is what `downscaleToFit` uses.

**Tests.** `reencode_test.go`: `TestReencode_OutgoingAcceptsAndDownscalesRealPhotoSizes` (a 6000x4000, an 8000x6000 and a 7680x2160 fixture - a 24 MP camera photo, a 48 MP phone photo and a dual-4K screenshot - each accepted, each come back at most 4096 on its longer side with its own aspect ratio kept, built with a new `fastSolidJPEG`/`fastSolidPNG` - a single `draw.Draw` fill rather than a per-pixel loop, fast enough that a real multi-megapixel fixture costs a few seconds under `-race`, not tens); `TestReencode_OutgoingNeverUpscalesAnAlreadySmallImage`; `TestReencode_OutgoingRejectsOverBudgetHostileShapeAt50Megapixels` (a real progressive JPEG fixture relabelled to declare 10000x5000 - exactly 50 megapixels - via a new `setJPEGDimensions`, the same SOF-rewriting trick `setPNGDimensions` already plays for PNG, confirmed with the same no-real-allocation `runtime.MemStats` check `TestReencode_RejectsDecompressionBombWithoutAllocating` uses); `TestReencode_FullImageKeepsItsStricterCapForReceivedPhotos`. `connector/media_test.go`: `TestSanitizeOutgoingImage(File)_AcceptsAndDownscalesAPhotoOverFullImagesCap` prove the wiring reaches `Outgoing`, not `FullImage`, through the public API. `whatsapp/upload_test.go`: `TestWithinThumbnailSourceCaps_AcceptsASizeOverFullImagesCap`. `clipboard/clipboard_test.go`: `TestMaxPasteLen_MatchesTheOutgoingImageProfilesInputCap`. Every test above passes with `-race`.

## A third sandbox review found memfd_create could hold shared memory open outside RLIMIT_AS, and /dev/shm and bwrap's own root tmpfs had no cap of their own

A further review, under the same threat model as the second sandbox review above - a crafted file may fully compromise the sandboxed ffmpeg, and the sandbox must still bound what that buys an attacker - found that `RLIMIT_AS` (`limits.go`'s own `maxAddressSpaceBytes`) does not actually bound all of a job's own memory, and that two places inside the sandbox were still writable with no cap of their own at all. Each gap was confirmed for real, through a real `bwrap`, before being fixed.

**1. `memfd_create` and `memfd_secret` create an anonymous, shared-memory-backed file whose pages are never charged to the calling process's own address space.** `RLIMIT_AS` bounds a process's own virtual memory mappings; the bytes behind a memfd's own fd are charged to the kernel's shared-memory accounting instead, and are only ever counted against `RLIMIT_AS` if the process goes on to `mmap` them into its own address space, which merely holding the fd open and writing to it never does. What does still apply to a memfd is `RLIMIT_FSIZE` (`--fsize`) and `RLIMIT_NOFILE` (`--nofile`) - so, before this fix, a hijacked job could open up to `maxOpenFiles` (64) of them, each written up to `maxOutputFileBytes` (512 MiB), for close to 32 GiB through a route neither `maxAddressSpaceBytes` nor anything else in `limits.go` actually bounds.

**The fix refuses both outright, in their own seccomp group, rather than trying to cap them.** `memfdSyscalls` (new, `seccomp.go`) names `memfd_create` and `memfd_secret` - the second is kernel-locked memory meant never to reach disk or be read by anything but the process that created it, the same shape of gap as the first, not a different one - and both are added to the policy as their own `EPERM` group, the same way `processCreationSyscalls` already got its own group rather than joining the flat `deniedSyscalls` list: a dedicated name worth its own doc comment, since the reason both matter here (an `RLIMIT_AS` bypass) is not the generic "no legitimate reason to call this" that the rest of `deniedSyscalls` shares. There is no cap that would make either safe to allow - unlike /dev/shm and bwrap's own root below, there is no byte limit on a memfd's own backing pages at all - so refusing them takes this route to zero rather than adding a made-up number to the sandbox's own total. Considered and left alone: System V shared memory (`shmget`/`shmat`), which is bounded differently - by system-wide `shmmax`/`shmall` sysctls, not by this process's own fd table - and which nothing in any profile's own fixed ffmpeg arguments has ever been seen to call; denying it on top of memfd and /dev/shm's own new cap below would be defence in depth with no confirmed gap behind it.

**Tests.** `TestSeccompInstructions_RefusesMemfdSyscallsOnTheNativeArch` assembles the real filter and runs it through `golang.org/x/net/bpf`'s own software VM, the same way every other denylist test here does, over both names in `memfdSyscalls` - written and confirmed failing (undefined: `memfdSyscalls`) before the syscall group existed, the same red-then-green order every fix in this entry followed. Every real, skip-if-unavailable integration test in `transcode` was re-run after the change and still passes, confirming neither syscall is something ffmpeg, or a library it links, actually needs for ordinary startup.

**2. `--dev /dev` creates `/dev/shm` as part of `/dev`'s own tmpfs, with no size of its own.** Confirmed directly: `/dev/shm` reported the exact same size as `/dev` itself - half of this machine's own RAM, counted against no limit above, the identical gap `maxTmpBytes` already closed for `/tmp` in the second sandbox review, just one path over. Nothing in any profile's own fixed ffmpeg arguments ever asks it to use `/dev/shm`; the only thing glibc itself ever resolves there is POSIX shared memory (`sem_open`, `shm_open`), which none of this package's own callers need either.

**The fix mounts a second, explicitly sized tmpfs at `/dev/shm`, then remounts `/dev` itself read-only.** `maxDevShmBytes` (new, `limits.go`, 16 MiB - matching `maxTmpBytes`'s own number and reasoning exactly) is read by a `--size`/`--tmpfs /dev/shm` pair placed right after `--dev /dev`, replacing whatever uncapped tmpfs `--dev` itself created there; `--remount-ro /dev` follows immediately after. Confirmed directly, through a real `bwrap`, that this leaves `/dev/shm` writable up to its own 16 MiB cap (an attempted write 1 MB past it gets a short write and nothing more) while a file created directly under `/dev`, outside `/dev/shm`, now fails with "Read-only file system" - and that the remount does not affect the separate tmpfs now mounted at `/dev/shm`, the same way remounting `OutputDirPath` read-only already does not affect the file bound inside it.

**3. bwrap's own root - everywhere not explicitly bound to something else - starts out as a plain, writable tmpfs with no size of its own either.** The same gap as `/tmp` and `/dev/shm`, just with no fixed path a caller would ever think to check: a hijacked job could create and fill a file anywhere under `/`, or under `/work` outside the one output file actually bound there, up to half this machine's own RAM, bounded by nothing in `limits.go` at all.

**The fix adds `--remount-ro /` as the very last bwrap flag.** By the time it runs, every place that still needs to stay writable - `/tmp`, the new capped `/dev/shm`, and the one file bound under `OutputDirPath` - already has its own separate mount, which the remount does not touch; everywhere else under `/` stops accepting new files, renames or deletes. This has to be the last mount-affecting flag `bwrapArgs` returns, never an earlier one: remounting the root read-only before the rest of the sandbox's own setup finishes would make that setup itself fail, not just whatever a job tries to do once it starts. Confirmed directly, through a real `bwrap` built exactly this way: a write anywhere else under `/`, under `/dev` outside `/dev/shm`, or under `/work` outside the bound output file all fail with "Read-only file system", while `/tmp`, `/dev/shm` within its own cap, and the bound output file all still accept a write exactly as before.

**Tests (2 and 3 together).** `TestBwrapArgs_CapsAndIsolatesDevShm` and `TestBwrapArgs_RemountsRootReadOnlyAsTheFinalFlag` (`argv_test.go`) pin the exact flag sequences by position, the same exact-argv style every other `bwrapArgs` test here uses; `TestBuildArgv_FullSpec`'s own full literal comparison was updated to match. `TestRun_RootDevAndOversizedDevShmWritesAllFail` (`sandbox_test.go`) is the real-bwrap test: every step is a bash builtin (`printf`, redirection, `[`) rather than an external command, which the process-creation fix from the second sandbox review would now also refuse to fork for a reason unrelated to this test; it tries a write to `/`, to `/dev` outside `/dev/shm`, to `/work` outside the bound file, to `/dev/shm` within its own cap, and to `/dev/shm` past it, and checks that only the one within the cap succeeds. All three new tests were written and confirmed failing against the pre-fix `bwrapArgs` - the exact-argv tests missing the new flags entirely, and the real-bwrap test's own writes to `/`, `/dev` and `/work` succeeding when they should not have - before the fix made them pass.

**4. With every writable place now bounded, the sandbox's own total is a single, derived number.** `maxJobBytes` (new, `limits.go`) sums `maxAddressSpaceBytes` (4 GiB, the job's own address space, shared by every thread it starts - see `processCreationSyscalls` in `seccomp.go`) plus `maxTmpBytes` (16 MiB, `/tmp`) plus `maxDevShmBytes` (16 MiB, `/dev/shm`) plus `maxOutputFileBytes` (512 MiB, the one file `Spec.Output` names): 4,865,392,640 bytes, about 4.53 GiB. Kept as a sum of the four named constants rather than a rounded literal, so a future change to any one of them changes this number, and the test that pins it, together. memfd_create and memfd_secret are deliberately left out of the sum: both are now refused outright rather than capped, so adding them here would mean picking a number for a route that no longer exists at all.

**Tests.** `TestMaxJobBytes_MatchesTheDocumentedTotal` (`limits_test.go`, new file) pins the literal byte count above; confirmed failing (wrong value) and passing again by temporarily mutating its own expected constant, the same mutation check used to confirm every other pinning test in this codebase actually tests something.

**Every real-ffmpeg integration test across every profile was re-run after all four fixes together, not just once per fix.** Video (portrait with audio, large landscape, rotated, HEVC), Animation (GIF, animated WebP), Audio (MP3, Opus voice note, M4A, 5.1), Sound and StillFrame (animated WebP) all still pass - including `+faststart`'s own MP4 second pass and the PNG muxer, both of which reopen their own output file by name after ffmpeg's first pass, now with `/dev`, bwrap's own root read-only and `/dev/shm` capped at 16 MiB. None of them touches `/dev/shm` or anywhere outside `OutputDirPath`'s own bound file at all, confirmed by these same tests passing unchanged.

**Coverage.** Re-measured the same way as the second sandbox review: `bwrap`, `ffmpeg` and `ffprobe` hidden from `PATH` entirely (a symlink farm of `/usr/bin` minus those three, built under `build/` and removed immediately after), so every real-pipeline test in both packages skips rather than runs. `sandbox` held at 91.4%, `transcode` at 90.8% - identical to the second sandbox review's own figures, since every line this entry adds is covered by a pure unit test (the BPF software VM, or a direct `bwrapArgs`/`maxJobBytes` check) that needs neither `bwrap` nor `ffmpeg` - both still above the 90% gate on the unit and fake-runner tests alone.

**Verification.** `coredumpctl list` was checked before this review began and again after every test run above, across both packages, with `-race`, real `bwrap`/`prlimit`/seccomp paths and real `ffmpeg` included: the count never moved. Nothing in this entry's own fixes changes how a denied syscall or a resource limit is enforced - `memfd_create`/`memfd_secret` join existing `EPERM` groups, and every new filesystem cap fails a write with an ordinary error, never a signal - so this was expected, not merely hoped for, and confirmed the same way the `ActionKillProcess` entry above first established it matters.

## The in-app photo viewer's `Image` was gated on "not video", so an edit while it was open could hand it a file, voice note or audio message's own raw bytes

A security review found `PhotoViewer.qml`'s `Image` set its `source` (and `visible`, and the size label's text) from `!root._isVideo`, a deny-list, rather than an allow-list. `show()` only ever opens the viewer for a photo or a video, but it keeps following the open message's media live: a sender who edits it into a file, voice note or audio message while the viewer is still open on it, or while a fetch is still in flight (Telegram allows a media edit; WhatsApp's edit resends the message), leaves `_isVideo` false, so the `Image` still took `root.path` as its source. Only a photo's path is the helper's own re-encoded display copy; a file, voice note or audio message's path is the sender's original download, and Qt's `Image` decodes whatever source it is given by sniffing its own bytes, choosing the decoder from exactly the untrusted content this logic would then hand it. The viewer never shows a sticker either, despite its path also being a display copy - `MediaController.openMedia` only ever calls `photoViewer.show` for `kind === "photo"` - so the allow-list is `kind === "photo"` alone, not "photo or sticker".

**The fix: `_isPhoto` as the allow-list, and closing the viewer the instant it stops applying.** `PhotoViewer.qml`'s `Image.source`, `Image.visible`, `_haveFile` and the size label's text all read a new `_isPhoto` (`kind === "photo"`) instead of `!_isVideo`. `PhotoViewerController` gained `_showable` (true for `"photo"` or `"video"`, the only two kinds `show()` ever opens the viewer for) and an `onViewerPhotoChanged` handler that calls `close()` the moment it goes false for the open message, rather than leave the viewer's live `photo`/`path` bindings pointed at that kind's own raw download. `close()` itself no longer gates releasing the shared video-decode slot on the open message's *current* kind (an edit can have already changed it by the time this reactive close runs, which would wrongly read "not a video" and skip the release): `VideoController.deselect` is already a no-op unless the id it is asked about still holds the slot, so asking unconditionally is never wrong and is what makes the reactive close correct for a video too.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/PhotoViewer` gained `checkEditedToFileNeverLoadsItsRawBytes`: opens a photo, then edits it to kind `file` with a different path, and confirms the `Image`'s source and visibility never follow. `tests/qml/PhotoViewerController` gained `checkEditWhileOpenToUnshowableKindClosesTheViewer`: edits the open photo's media in place with `timeline.setMedia`, the same call a real edit's `message.updated` handling already makes, and confirms the viewer closes. Both confirmed to fail, for the right reason, against the pre-fix code.

## The composer's own text input relied only on losing real focus to be destroyed, which a conversation restored before the window was active, or a blocked conversation, never gives it

"A field gated on its own step or dialog-open Loader was still a live sink while the window itself sat open and unfocused" (above) left the composer and the list search out of its own `windowActive` fix, on the theory that losing real `activeFocus` - which Qt ties to the window itself losing activation - already clears their own edit-intent flag with no extra code, so alt-tabbing away while actually typing already destroys the field. A follow-up security review found that theory incomplete for the composer: `MessengerLayout`'s own startup restore calls `focusComposer()` unconditionally, including when `WindowReopen` brings the window back not yet active, and every conversation open calls it too, including a blocked one, whose composer is created with `enabled: false` (`ConversationView.qml`). Neither case ever gives the field real focus to lose in the first place, so `_writingRequested` - and the live `TextArea` it gates - stuck around regardless of the window's own focus or the conversation being blocked.

**The fix: `windowActive` and `enabled` join `_writingRequested` in the Loader's own gate, not instead of it.** `Composer.qml` gained a `windowActive` property, forwarded from `ConversationView.windowActive` (already threaded from `Panel.windowFocused`), and `inputLoader.active` is now `root.active && root._writingRequested && root.windowActive && root.enabled`. `_writingRequested` itself is left alone by a blur that is really the window going inactive (`onActiveFocusChanged` now checks `root.windowActive`, the same guard the four earlier overlays use, since by the time that handler runs for that reason `windowActive` is already false), so it survives a window-focus blip exactly as before; `onLoaded` now force-focuses whenever `_writingRequested` is still true, covering both the ordinary case and the field coming back after the window, or an unblock, catches up - `_focusPending` is gone, since `_writingRequested` alone now decides this. The draft (`root.text`, already living in `ComposerController.draft` one layer up) needed no change; cursor position did not survive the round trip before, so the input's own `cursorPosition` is now mirrored into a new `_cursorPosition` property the same way `text` already was, and restored in `onLoaded` right after the text.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/Composer` gained `beginWindowInactiveCheck`/`checkFieldRestoredWithTextCursorAndFocus` (types a draft, moves the caret, toggles `windowActive` false then true, confirms the field is destroyed without losing the draft and comes back with the same text, the same caret position and real focus) and `checkNeverCreatedInABlockedChat` (blocks the conversation, calls `focusInput()`, confirms no field is created). `tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs`'s `WINDOW_ACTIVE_REQUIRED` no longer excludes `Composer.qml` just because it is also in `EDIT_INTENT_FLAGS`: both are true now, not either/or, so it is checked by both tests at once. Confirmed to fail, for the right reason, against the pre-fix `Composer.qml` (the QML checks: the property did not exist; the unit test: `active:` named no `windowActive`), then restored.

## The `o` key opened more of a link than its own bubble ever showed, since it matched with a separate, looser pattern

A security review found `ui/lib/Highlight.js`'s `links()`, which the `o` key reads to open a message's link, matched with its own `/https?:\/\/[^\s]+/` over the message's raw text, while the bubble (`ui/lib/Format.js`'s `URL_PATTERN`, run over escaped text inside `linkify`) stops a plain autolinked URL at whitespace or an unescaped `>` that does not start one of the few entities a URL may still contain. The two had quietly drifted apart: a message reading `https://bank.com>@evil.example/login` showed only `https://bank.com` as a link - the bubble's own rule stops at `>` - but `o` opened the whole string, which a browser reads as `bank.com>` ending a URL's userinfo and `evil.example` starting the real host past it. `WindowController.openLink`'s own mismatch question (`Links.needsConfirmation`, comparing a link's shown text against its real target) never caught this either: both callers - `MessengerLayout`'s `onLinksRequested` for the single-link case, `WindowController.acceptPalette`'s "links" branch for the multi-link chooser - passed the same url twice, as `url` and as `displayText`, so the comparison was always of a value against itself.

**The fix: one shared extraction function, and the shown text carried through as its own field rather than the same value passed in twice.** `Format.js` gained `plainLinks(text)`: escapes raw text, matches the same `URL_PATTERN` `linkify` already does, trims trailing punctuation the same way, and unescapes each match back to its real characters (`unescapeHtml`, `escapeHtml`'s own inverse, also new) - the one place this match-and-trim logic lives now. `Highlight.links` calls it directly; its own separately maintained pattern, and its own copy of the trailing-punctuation trim, are gone. `MessageHighlight.openLink` now reports each link as `{url, displayText}` (equal, for a plain autolink, by `Format.plainLinks`' and `linkify`'s shared construction - but two separate fields, not one value written twice), threaded through `WindowController.linkChoices` and `Palette.js`'s "links" mode unchanged, to both call sites of `openLink`, so a future divergence between what is shown and what opens still has something real for `needsConfirmation` to catch.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` gained exhaustive `plainLinks` and `unescapeHtml` cases, including the exact `bank.com>@evil.example` string, every other character `URL_PATTERN` treats specially (`<`, `"`, `&`), and a property test that `plainLinks` always agrees with what `linkify` actually anchors. `tests/unit/highlight.test.cjs` gained the same adversarial case directly against `Highlight.links`, confirmed to fail - returning the full string - against the pre-fix pattern. `tests/qml/MessageHighlight`'s existing `checkOpenLinkAndGoToQuote` now asserts the `{url, displayText}` pair `o` reports, rather than a bare url string.

## `QKeySequence::StandardKey::Paste` turned out to depend on the desktop's own platform theme, which the two earlier paste-interception entries above did not account for

CI's QML job failed `tests/qml/Composer` ("Ctrl+Shift+Insert in the composer never reached routeKey with nativePaste") and `tests/qml/FieldPaste` ("Ctrl+Shift+Insert in the palette field never reached clipboard.readText"), while both passed, unchanged, on the developer's own machine. The two entries above ("Qt's own clipboard reader must never see a foreign offer, even in a field that is not the composer" and its own follow-up) made every field check `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste) || event.key === Qt.Key_Paste` ahead of the ordinary `field.paste`/`compose.attach` binding lookup, reasoning that "reading the real `QKeyEvent`'s own `matches()` is the one check that stays correct across platform themes without this project maintaining its own copy of Qt's table." That reasoning was wrong: `QKeySequence::keyBindings()` resolves a standard key from `QGuiApplicationPrivate::platformTheme()`, and which keys come back for `Paste` depends on that theme's own `KeyboardScheme`, which is not fixed at all. The developer's session exports `QT_QPA_PLATFORMTHEME=gtk3`; Qt's `QGtk3Theme` subclasses `QGnomeTheme`, whose `KeyboardScheme` is `GnomeKeyboardScheme`, and `QPlatformThemePrivate::currentKeyPlatforms()` (`qtbase/src/gui/kernel/qplatformtheme.cpp`) additionally ORs in the generic X11 scheme's own bindings whenever the active scheme is KDE, GNOME or CDE - which is what makes Ctrl+Shift+Insert (tagged for the X11 scheme alone in that file's own static table) resolve to `Paste` there. CI's container sets no platform theme at all; `QGuiApplicationPrivate::platformTheme()`'s own creation order (`qtbase/src/gui/kernel/qguiapplication.cpp`) tries every named theme plugin and falls back, last, to a bare `new QPlatformTheme` with no scheme override of its own, landing on the base class's `KeyboardScheme` default (`WindowsKeyboardScheme`) - whose own bindings do not include Ctrl+Shift+Insert. Shift+Insert, Ctrl+V and the dedicated `Key_Paste` are tagged for both schemes (or every scheme), which is why only Ctrl+Shift+Insert, of everything the earlier two entries added, ever showed this gap: checking `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste)` was never "correct across platform themes" - it was correct across the two platform themes those entries happened to test against, and silently dependent on whichever one is active everywhere else.

**The fix: a fixed list this project owns, instead of asking Qt at runtime.** `ui/lib/Paste.js` gained `isPasteKey(key, modifiers)`, a pure function (tested with node, see javascript.md) that recognises every key `qtbase/src/gui/kernel/qplatformtheme.cpp`'s own static table maps to `QKeySequence::Paste` under any keyboard scheme - Ctrl+V, Shift+Insert, Ctrl+Shift+Insert, F18 (a Sun keyboard's own Paste key) and macOS's Meta+Y, alongside the dedicated `Key_Paste` every scheme answers to - plus its own small `KEY`/`MOD` tables of the Qt key and modifier codes it needs, the same reason `Keymap.js` keeps its own. Every place that used to check `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste) || event.key === Qt.Key_Paste` (the composer's own native-paste interception, and the palette, search, setup, rename and new-chat fields' own `field.paste` interception) now calls `Paste.isPasteKey(event.key, event.modifiers)` instead, ahead of the same `routeKey(..., true)` fallback as before: `Panel.qml`'s own `nativePaste` handling, and the bounded helper paste it forces, are both unchanged - only how a field recognises the key changed. Meta+Y can never actually arrive on this Linux-only UI, but is included anyway: a fixed list that quietly dropped a scheme would just be a smaller version of the same bug.

**Verified both ways before and after.** `tests/qml/Composer` and `tests/qml/FieldPaste` were run as-is first (both passed, confirming the developer machine's own `QT_QPA_PLATFORMTHEME=gtk3`), then again with that variable unset (both failed, reproducing CI's exact failure text) - before any production code changed. After the fix, both were run in both environments again, alongside every other QML test directory the change touched (`AccountSetup`, `CommandPalette`, `ListColumn`, `NewChatDialog`, `RenameAccount`, `ComposerController`, `Flows`): all pass with `QT_QPA_PLATFORMTHEME=gtk3` and with it unset.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/paste.test.cjs` gained a table of cases for `isPasteKey` mirroring `qplatformtheme.cpp`'s own rows one for one, plus negative cases a scheme-dependent check could not get wrong but a careless fixed list could (a bare Insert, Ctrl+Insert - every scheme's own `Copy` - Ctrl+Alt+V, a bare V or Y). `tests/unit/field-paste-guard.test.cjs`'s scan of every field now looks for `Paste.isPasteKey(event.key, event.modifiers)` ahead of the binding-lookup fallback, rather than `event.matches(StandardKey.Paste)`; its own deliberately-broken fixture (no interception at all) still fails the check. `tests/qml/Composer` and `tests/qml/FieldPaste` keep their existing key-event steps unchanged - they still drive real Ctrl+Shift+Insert and `Key_Paste` presses - with comments updated to say isPasteKey catches them rather than Qt's own `matches()`.

## A group chat's own annotations could drift out of step with its rows after a live insert or remove, showing a message under the wrong sender's name or grouping

A security review found `MessageTimeline.qml`'s `upsert` and `remove` kept `root.annotations` - `Timeline.annotate`'s per-row output, read by position (`annotations[index]`) in `ConversationView.qml`'s delegate - in step with `messagesModel` only for an in-place update: `_reannotateTouched` rewrites the one or two positions `Timeline.applyUpsert`/`applyRemove` named as touched, but never inserted or removed an entry in `root.annotations` itself to match a row actually being inserted into or removed from `messagesModel`.
