A genuine insert or remove shifts every row at or after that position to a new index; `root.annotations` stayed the same length throughout, so every row from the change point onward silently read the annotation that used to belong to whichever row previously sat one slot nearer the front - another message's own `showSender`, `groupedWithOlder` and `showDay` flags (`MessageDelegate.qml`) - and the rows that ran off the end of the now too-short array fell back to `ConversationView.qml`'s default (`{ showDay: false, showSender: false, groupedWithOlder: false }`), hiding their sender entirely. This only ever stayed invisible by coincidence (a message always arriving at position 0, or a row's old and new neighbours happening to share the same identity) - any live message landing elsewhere among already-loaded rows, or a delete anywhere but the very end, showed it.

**The fix: splice `root.annotations` wherever `messagesModel` itself is spliced, in the same call.** `MessageTimeline.qml` gained `_insertAnnotation(at)` and `_removeAnnotation(at)`, each a `splice` on a copy of `root.annotations` mirroring the exact `messagesModel.insert`/`.remove` call beside it - a placeholder `null` for an insert, since `_reannotateTouched` right after it always overwrites that same position with the real one - and `_trimAnnotations(keep)` mirroring the tail eviction `upsert` already applies to `messagesModel` once a flood pushes the loaded window past `Timeline.MAX_LOADED_ROWS`. `upsert`'s insert branch and `remove` call the matching one before `_reannotateTouched` runs, so by the time that touched-positions recompute (deliberately still narrow - see the entry above on making one event's own work small) reads `root.annotations`, it is already the right length and shape for `messagesModel`'s own rows, and only the handful of genuinely-changed boundary rows still need a fresh `Timeline.annotateForPair` call. `Timeline.applyUpsert`/`applyRemove` themselves, and their own 50,000-message flood test, are untouched: the bug was in `MessageTimeline.qml`'s own bookkeeping around them, not their decisions.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/MessageTimeline` gained `checkAnnotationsStayAlignedThroughInsertAndRemove`, reproducing a security review's own Alice/Mallory group-chat sequence: a clean initial page (Mallory's run of two, then Alice's run of two), a revoke removing one of Mallory's messages, then a live message from Mallory arriving between Alice's two - both landing around already-loaded rows, never only at either end - checked against an independent, fresh `Timeline.annotate()` pass over the same final rows rather than hand-derived expectations, so the test catches any future misalignment, not only this exact shape of one. Confirmed to fail first - row 2 (`alice2`) read `{showSender: false, groupedWithOlder: true}`, Mallory's own stale entry, instead of its own `{showSender: true, groupedWithOlder: false}` - against the pre-fix code, then passed once `_insertAnnotation`/`_removeAnnotation` were added.

## The `o` key's own fix for matching the bubble's links (above) still did not match it, since the bubble never linkifies a whole message's text in one pass

A follow-up security review found "The `o` key opened more of a link than its own bubble ever showed" (above) incomplete: `Highlight.links` now matched `Format.plainLinks`' `URL_PATTERN` exactly, but `plainLinks` still ran that pattern over the *whole* raw message text in one pass, while the bubble itself (`Format.js`'s `messageHtml`) never does - it linkifies per formatting span (`styledSegment`), per mention segment (`mentionHtml`) and per Markdown table cell (`Markdown.js`'s `tableHtml`/`cellHtml`), each its own, separate call into `lineHtml`/`linkify`. Two shapes let the two drift apart again. First: a Telegram entity (bold, say) starting exactly at the `@` of raw text that reads as one URL, `https://bank.example@evil.example/login`, makes the bubble linkify only `https://bank.example` - complete and harmless on its own - as its own anchor, then render `@evil.example/login` as separate, inert text; `plainLinks`' single pass over the unsplit string had no notion of that boundary and rejoined both halves into the one link the bubble never showed at all, reaching `evil.example`. Second: a pipe table's `| https://bank.example.com|@evil.example/login |` splits into two cells at the `|`, each linkified on its own (the first cell alone is a complete URL; the second, lacking its own `http(s)://`, is not a link at all) - `plainLinks` matched straight through the `|` (not one of `URL_PATTERN`'s own stop characters) and again rejoined them into the link the bubble never rendered. `MessageHighlight.openLink` then reported `{url, displayText: url}` for whatever `links()` found, so `Links.needsConfirmation`'s own mismatch question had nothing to catch even when it did run - the url being compared against was always itself.

**The fix: read the bubble's own anchors directly, instead of re-deriving them with a second pattern.** `Format.js` already had `messageLinkAnchors(text, mentions, spans)` (added for `resolveClickedLink`'s own mouse-click path), which runs text through the exact same per-span, per-mention, per-table-cell rendering `messageHtml` does and collects every anchor it writes, in order, each `{url, displayText}` with `displayText` the whole covered span's own visible text, not just one cut of it. `Highlight.links` now calls this directly - `Format.messageLinkAnchors(text, Timeline.mentions(message), Timeline.formatting(message))` - rather than `Format.plainLinks(text)`, so the open key (`o`) can never reach a link the bubble itself does not also offer, under any of its rendering rules, not just the plain-autolink one. `Format.plainLinks` and its only other caller, `unescapeHtml`, are deleted outright: nothing else used either. `MessageHighlight.openLink` forwards `Highlight.links`' own result unchanged, rather than rebuilding `{url, displayText: url}` pairs from it, so a real span/mention mismatch finally reaches `Links.needsConfirmation` as one. `primaryLink` (reads one link's own url for the hint text) and `hints` (checks only `links().length`) needed a one-line update and no update at all, respectively.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/highlight.test.cjs`'s existing `links` cases were updated to the `{url, displayText}` shape, and gained the review's own two examples (the bold-entity-at-`@` case and the pipe-table case) plus a property test generating 200 varied text/mention/span combinations and checking `Highlight.links` always equals a direct `Format.messageLinkAnchors` call for the same inputs - confirmed to fail first, both on shape and (for the two new examples) on the actual url returned (`https://bank.example@evil.example/login` and `https://bank.example.com|@evil.example/login`, the two merged, dangerous links the bubble never shows) - against the pre-fix `Format.plainLinks`-based code. `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` lost its now-pointless `plainLinks` and `unescapeHtml` cases. `tests/qml/MessageHighlight` gained a step delivering a live `message.updated` event that gives the already-highlighted message a one-word text and a link-formatting span covering it, confirmed to fail first (`o` found no link at all, under the old raw-text match, for text with no `http(s)://` of its own), then to report the span's own real url and visible text once fixed.

## A link preview's own "already shown" check was a plain substring match against the raw text, which a shorter lookalike domain passes for free

The same review that found `Highlight.links`' own raw-text match drifting from the bubble (above) found `Links.previewDisplayText` - deciding whether a link preview card's own URL needs confirming before `MessageBubbleContent`'s `onOpened` opens it - made that decision with `messageText.includes(url)`: a plain substring test of the preview's url against the message's raw text. A shorter, different domain that merely reads as a textual prefix of a longer, unrelated link the text really shows - `paypal.co` is a literal substring of `paypal.com/signin` - passed this check for free, even though the sender sets a preview's own URL completely apart from the text, exactly the gap this check exists to catch for every other case.

**The fix: compare against the bubble's own rendered anchors, not the raw text.** `previewDisplayText` now takes `mentions` and `spans` alongside `url`, `messageText` and `title`, runs `Format.messageLinkAnchors` (the same function `Highlight.links`, above, and `resolveClickedLink` already use) and checks `url` for an *exact* match against one of the anchors it returns, rather than a substring test against the unrendered text. This also fixes the opposite gap for free: a link visible only through a formatting span, never typed out as a literal URL at all, now correctly counts as shown, which a substring match against raw text could never recognise either. `MessageBubbleContent.qml`'s one call site passes its own already-available `root.mentions` and `root.formatting` (the same pair `body`'s own `Format.messageHtml` call takes a few lines above).

**Tests.** `tests/unit/links.test.cjs` gained the review's own `paypal.co`/`paypal.com` case, confirmed to fail first (the old substring check said "shown", skipping the question `Links.needsConfirmation` would otherwise have asked), and a case for a span-only link, confirmed to fail first the other way (no literal text to substring-match against at all). The two existing `previewDisplayText` cases were updated to the new five-argument signature.

## The link-confirm dialog's own fix for eliding the description (far above) made a later review's "shown in full" claim false, since an end-elided line can hide the real host entirely

`LinkOpenConfirm.qml`'s own description has said "It will open:\n" + the full target URL since it was added (see the entry on `o` opening more of a link than its bubble showed), and gained `wrapMode: Text.Wrap`, `elide: Text.ElideRight` and `maximumLineCount: 8` in a later entry, purely to bound the dialog's own layout cost against an arbitrarily long URL - correct for that, but a security review found the combination means a URL built as a convincing fake host, a long run of userinfo padding, then `@` and the real host can have that real host - the only part after the final unescaped `@`, by construction always nearer the *end* of the string - pushed past where `ElideRight`'s own "…" cuts in, while the fake prefix stays fully visible. The dialog went on claiming "shown in full" while showing, in the cases that matter most, only the part engineered to mislead.

**The fix: a dedicated line for the host, which nothing ever elides, and the full URL below it elides in the middle instead of the end.** `Links.js` gained `urlHost(url)`: the authority host exactly as the URL spells it (scheme and userinfo stripped, a punycode or literal IPv4/IPv6 host shown as is, never decoded) - whatever sits after the *last* unescaped `@` in the authority, the same rule a browser uses to resolve one, so no amount of padding before it changes the answer; `""` for a link with no `"//"` authority at all (`mailto:`). `ChoiceDialog` (the shared frame every yes/no/choice question builds on) gained `emphasisText`, a line shown directly under the title that wraps but is never elided, and `descriptionElide`, defaulting to the existing `Text.ElideRight` so every other question's plain-sentence description is unchanged. `LinkOpenConfirm` sets `emphasisText` to `"Really opens: " + Links.urlHost(target)` (hidden for a link with no separate host), and `descriptionElide` to `Text.ElideMiddle`, so the full URL below keeps text from both ends in view too, rather than only the end a middle-elided line already protects less critically than the dedicated line above it does completely.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/links.test.cjs` gained `urlHost` cases for userinfo (plain and the review's own long-padding shape), ports, a bracketed IPv6 host with and without a port, an IPv4 host, a punycode host, query strings and fragments with no path, and a `mailto:` link's empty result, plus a linear-time check on a 65,000-character userinfo run. A new `tests/qml/LinkOpenConfirm` gained a component test: the review's own padded-userinfo URL, confirming the new emphasis line names the real host with no elide at all and the description below it elides in the middle, and that a `mailto:` link hides the emphasis line rather than showing an empty one - confirmed to fail first (no `dialogEmphasis` object existed at all) against the pre-fix `ChoiceDialog`/`LinkOpenConfirm`. `tests/qml/RenameAccount` and `tests/qml/KeyboardOverlays`, which already exercise the shared `ChoiceDialog` frame for other questions, were re-run to confirm the new, default-empty properties left every other dialog unchanged.

## The list search field was the one editable field left out of the windowActive fix, since focus does not follow a click

"A field gated on its own step or dialog-open Loader was still a live sink while the window itself sat open and unfocused" and the composer's own follow-up (both above) gated every other field in this UI on `windowActive` as well as its own edit-intent flag, reasoning that a real blur - which Qt ties to the window itself losing activation - already clears that flag with no extra code, so a window-focus blip while actually typing already destroys the field. `ListColumn`'s own search field was never revisited by either entry. A security review found the reasoning does not hold for it at all: clicking the search placeholder on a window that is *not* the active one does not first require activating that window the way a real desktop interaction normally would here (focus does not follow a click in this UI), so `focusSearch()` runs, `_editingSearch` turns true, and the field is created - but it never gains real `activeFocus` to lose, since the window was never active to begin with, so its `onActiveFocusChanged` guard never fires and the field just sits there, alive, reading every clipboard offer for as long as the window stays open and unfocused.

**The fix: the same `windowActive` gate as every other field, applied here too.** `ListColumn` gained a `windowActive` property (default `true`, wired from `MessengerLayout` the same way `ConversationView`'s already is) and `searchFieldLoader.active` is now `root.visible && root._editingSearch && root.windowActive`. `_editingSearch`'s own `onActiveFocusChanged` guard now checks `root.windowActive` too, the same guard every other self-gated field already has, so a window-focus blip while actually searching leaves it alone rather than clearing it the instant the field is torn down for that reason; `onLoaded` now force-focuses whenever `_editingSearch` is still true, covering the field coming back once `windowActive` catches up, the same way `Composer`'s own `onLoaded` already does - `_searchFocusPending` is gone, since `_editingSearch` alone now decides this, the same simplification the composer's own `_focusPending` already had removed for the identical reason.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/ListColumn` gained `beginSearchWindowInactiveCheck`/`checkSearchFieldSeededThenGoInactive`/`checkSearchFieldRestoredWithTextAndFocus` (seeds `query`, enters editing, toggles `windowActive` false then true, confirms the field is destroyed without losing the query and comes back with the same text and real focus), confirmed to fail first (`Cannot assign to non-existent property "windowActive"`) against the pre-fix component. `tests/unit/textinput-lifetime.test.cjs`'s `WINDOW_ACTIVE_REQUIRED` no longer excludes `components/ListColumn.qml`, confirmed to fail first (`active: root.visible && root._editingSearch`, naming no `windowActive`) against the pre-fix binding, then passed once restored.

## A message's own RemoteID and SenderID had no cap at all, unlike every other sender-controlled field

A security review found `domain.SanitizeIncoming` capped a message's text, sender name, mentions, reactions and media, and even a reply quote's own `RemoteID` (`CapRemoteID`, via `MaxRemoteIDLength`), but never the message's own `RemoteID` or `SenderID`. WhatsApp's stanza id (`types.MessageInfo.ID`, read by whatsmeow with no length check beyond its 16 MiB frame limit) and a JID's user part (`types.ParseJID`, kept exactly as given) both flow onto every message `connector/whatsapp/normalize_message.go`'s `message` and `live.go`'s `handleUndecryptable` build, with nothing downstream ever bounding either - so a multi-MiB id could ride every `message.added` event and every `messages.list` page straight into the shell, which buffers each JSON-RPC line and keeps every loaded message in memory.

**The fix: refuse to store one, rather than cap it.** Unlike every other field, `RemoteID` is the dedup key `store.AddMessage` matches an arriving message against and the handle `MessageByRemote`, `EditMessage`, `SetReactions` and `SetPoll` all key off, so truncating or clearing it (the treatment `CapRemoteID` already gives a reply quote's own copy) could make a later report resolve to the wrong stored message, or become indistinguishable from a message with no remote id at all. `domain.ValidMessageIDs` (new, `limits.go`) reports whether both fields sit within the existing `MaxRemoteIDLength` - generous for any real WhatsApp JID or Telegram id, as that constant's own doc comment already establishes. `app.Ingest`'s `save` (used by both `Incoming` and `History`, so a live message and a backfilled or undecryptable placeholder get the same treatment) and `Edited` both check it before doing anything else, dropping the message and logging only a fixed category (`"ingest: dropped message (reason=oversized remote or sender id)"`, never the id itself) rather than storing or publishing it. On the read side, `store.oversizedID` catches a row an older release stored before this refusal existed: `Message` and `MessageByRemote` report it as `domain.ErrNotFound`, the same as a row that is not there at all, and `Messages` drops it from a page with `slices.DeleteFunc` instead of failing the whole page. Telegram's own message and sender ids are `strconv.Itoa`/`FormatInt` of an `int64`, so they can never actually trigger this, but the check sits in the one path both connectors share, rather than per-connector, so neither can drift from the other.

**Tests.** `TestValidMessageIDs_RejectsAnOversizedRemoteID`/`_SenderID` (`domain`, new file `limits_messageid_test.go`); `TestIncoming_RefusesAnOversizedRemoteID`/`_SenderID`, `TestHistory_RefusesAnOversizedRemoteID` and `TestEdited_RefusesAnOversizedRemoteID` (`app`, new file `ingest_oversizedid_test.go`) confirm the message is never stored, never published, and (for `Incoming`) that the log line names the category, not the id; `TestMessage_TreatsAStoredOversizedRemoteIDAsNotFound`/`_SenderID`, `TestMessageByRemote_TreatsAStoredOversizedRemoteIDAsNotFound` and `TestMessages_DropsAStoredOversizedRowFromThePage` (`store`, new file `messages_oversizedid_test.go`) cover a row written directly through `AddMessage`, bypassing `Ingest`, the same way the existing legacy-row tests in `messages_sanitize_test.go` do. Every new test was confirmed failing - the oversized id stored and returned - before the corresponding production change.

## A thumbnail's own 64 KiB cap was checked on input, never on what imagesafe actually re-encoded it into

A security review found `domain.CapMedia` drops a message's embedded preview (`Media.Thumb`) over `MaxInlineThumbLen` on the raw bytes a connector first produced, but `store.sanitizedThumb`'s own re-encode through `imagesafe.Sanitize` - always a fresh JPEG, quality 90, for an opaque image - runs after that check, never before it, and nothing re-checked its result. A highly compressible image (plain colours and smooth gradients compress to a fraction of the cap either way) is not what exposes this: a fine, high-frequency pattern - a checkerboard, confirmed directly at 1024x1024: a 20 KB PNG input, a 549 KB JPEG output, about 27 times larger - does, since JPEG's own block DCT handles sharp repeating detail far worse than PNG's filters and deflate do. `store.scanMessage`'s read path happened to still catch this by chance: it runs `domain.SanitizeIncoming`, whose `CapMedia` re-checks `Media.Thumb`, after `decodeMessageColumns` has already re-encoded it. `AddMessage`, `fillMedia` and `EditMessage` (`store/messages.go`, via `verifyThumb`) have no such second pass at all: each returns the message it just wrote directly, and that returned value - never a subsequent read - is what `app.Ingest` publishes live as `message.added` or `message.updated`.

**The fix: the cap enforced once, where the re-encode itself happens.** `store.sanitizedThumb` (`thumbsafe.go`) now base64-encodes `imagesafe.Sanitize`'s own output and drops the thumbnail - `""`, width and height both zero, the same result every other failure in this function already gives - the moment that encoded length runs past `domain.MaxInlineThumbLen`, rather than trusting the caller to recheck it. Both `verifyThumb` (every write path) and `decodeMessageColumns` (every read) call this one function, so neither can drift from the other, and `CapMedia`'s own check becomes pure defence in depth for a row some future path writes without going through either. Alongside this, `imagesafe.Thumbnail` gained `DownscaleTo: 512` - the size WhatsApp and Telegram both actually use for a static sticker, confirmed as every real use of this profile (a message's own embedded preview, and `app.imageSafeProfile`'s and both connectors' own sticker-document display-copy profile): a real sticker is never shrunk, since it is never larger, but an oversized claim up to `Thumbnail`'s own 1024-pixel accept cap is now shrunk before being encoded at all, rather than paying the full cost of a size nothing legitimate ever needs. This does not replace the hard cap above - the same checkerboard pattern at 512x512 still re-encodes to about 140 KB, several times over the limit - it only means a real preview or sticker almost never needs the hard cap to step in to begin with.

**Tests.** `TestAddMessage_DropsAThumbThatReencodesOverTheCap`, `TestAddMessage_FillMediaDropsAThumbThatReencodesOverTheCap` and `TestEditMessage_DropsAThumbThatReencodesOverTheCap` (`store`, new file `thumbsafe_cap_test.go`) call each write path directly and check its own returned message, not a later read, using a new `fakeOversizedThumb` helper (`helpers_test.go`) that builds the checkerboard pattern measured above. `TestReencode_ThumbnailKeepsARealStickerAtFullResolution` and `TestReencode_ThumbnailDownscalesAnOversizedClaim` (`imagesafe`, new file `reencode_thumbnail_test.go`) confirm a real 512x512 input is never touched while a 1024x1024 one is shrunk to it. All four were confirmed failing - the oversized result kept, or the claim left at its full declared size - before the corresponding production change; the first "keeps a real sticker" case passed even before the fix, since `DownscaleTo` unset also never shrinks, and stayed green after, confirming the fix does not cost a real sticker any sharpness.

## `syscall.Dup`'s own result carried no close-on-exec flag, unlike every descriptor the os package opens

A security review found `claimProtocolStdout` (`backend/stdout.go`) duplicates the protocol file descriptor with `syscall.Dup`, a thin wrapper over the bare `dup()` syscall, which - unlike `os.OpenFile`, `os.Pipe` or anything else the standard library opens, all marked close-on-exec by Go's own runtime since Go 1.9 - carries no such flag by default. The duplicated descriptor is this helper's one handle for writing JSON-RPC traffic back to the UI; left inheritable, every child process started afterwards - `wl-copy` and `wl-paste` (`internal/clipboard`) and `bwrap` for the sandbox (`internal/sandbox`), which goes on to run `ffmpeg`/`ffprobe` inside it - would inherit its own writable copy of that same pipe, for as long as the child keeps running, whether or not it ever means to touch it. A grep across the whole backend for any other descriptor opened by a raw `syscall.*` call, rather than through the `os` package, found none: this is the only one.

**The fix: `syscall.CloseOnExec` right after the duplicate is made.** One call, `syscall.CloseOnExec(dupFD)`, added immediately after `syscall.Dup(fd)` succeeds and before the descriptor is wrapped in an `*os.File` or used for anything else. `claimProtocolStdout` already runs as the very first thing in `main`, before any connector, the sandbox or anything else that could fork a child exists yet, so there is no window in which a concurrent exec could still inherit the descriptor before this call marks it - unlike a general-purpose library function, which would need the atomic `F_DUPFD_CLOEXEC` form to make the same guarantee under concurrent use.

**Tests.** `TestClaimProtocolStdout_DuplicatedDescriptorIsCloseOnExec` (`backend`, `stdout_test.go`) reads the real descriptor's own `FD_CLOEXEC` flag back with a direct `fcntl(F_GETFD)` syscall - the standard library's `syscall` package exposes no higher-level accessor for reading it, only `CloseOnExec` for setting it - and confirms it is set. Confirmed failing (the flag absent) against the pre-fix code.

## imagesafe's own decode-budget estimate trusted two header reads that a crafted file can displace

A security review found `estimatedDecodeBytes`'s two cheap, header-only checks - whether a JPEG is progressive, whether a PNG is 16-bit - could each be fooled into reporting the cheaper shape for a file that actually costs more to decode, the one check `reencodeStill` relies on before ever running the real, expensive decode. `jpegIsProgressive`'s marker walk treated an "FF 00" byte-stuffed pair - which only ever escapes a literal 0xFF inside entropy-coded scan data, and so never legitimately appears before any SOF - as an ordinary marker carrying its own two-byte length field, reading that length from whatever bytes happened to follow and skipping that far ahead: placed just before a real SOF2, it skips clean over it, so the walk reaches the end of the file having found no frame marker at all and reports "not progressive". `pngBitDepth` read the bit depth byte at a fixed offset 24 bytes past the signature, assuming IHDR is the very first chunk there; image/png's own decoder only orders IHDR, PLTE, tRNS, IDAT and IEND against each other and silently ignores any other chunk type wherever it appears, including before IHDR, so splicing one in there shifts the real IHDR - and the fixed-offset read - into that chunk's own bytes instead. Both were confirmed directly: a real, ten-scan progressive JPEG fixture with the pair spliced in immediately before its SOF2 still decodes exactly as the unmodified fixture does (image/jpeg itself ignores the pair, exactly as its own decode loop's comment says extraneous data is), and a 16-bit PNG with one ordinary `tEXt` chunk spliced in before IHDR does too - in both cases, only this package's own cost estimate for them changed, not what a real decode of either one actually costs.

**The fix: once either read cannot confirm the cheap shape, it answers with the expensive one.** `jpegIsProgressive` now returns `true` the instant it sees an "FF 00" pair, a segment whose declared length is under two, or its own walk running out of data without ever finding a frame marker - the last two already existed as edge cases, previously answering `false` - rather than ever again guessing past bytes it cannot make sense of as this file's real marker structure. `pngBitDepth` now confirms the chunk type at the fixed offset is literally `"IHDR"` before trusting the bit depth byte that follows it, answering 16 whenever it is not (or the data is too short to check at all), where it previously defaulted to 8. Neither change can ever cost a real photo anything: a legitimate progressive JPEG's own SOF2 always sits before its first SOS, where "FF 00" never occurs, and no real encoder ever writes a chunk before IHDR - `reencodeStill`'s own earlier `image.DecodeConfig` call already refuses anything too malformed to reach either function regardless. Fixing `pngBitDepth` also surfaced a real bug in the new check's own first draft - `pngIHDRChunkTypeOffset` was written as 8, the chunk *length* field's own offset, instead of 12, where the chunk *type* actually sits - caught immediately by the existing `TestReencode_SixteenBitPNGCostsMoreThanEightBitAtTheSameSize` rejecting a perfectly ordinary 8-bit PNG once the fix landed.

**Tests.** `TestReencode_StillChargesAProgressiveJPEGDisguisedWithStrayFF00` and `TestReencode_StillChargesASixteenBitPNGDisguisedWithAChunkBeforeIHDR` (`imagesafe`, new file `reencode_decodebudget_bypass_test.go`) reproduce both crafted shapes exactly, through the public `Reencode`, and confirm each is now rejected under a budget that only the expensive charge exceeds; `TestReencode_DisguisedProgressiveJPEGStillDecodesForReal` and `TestReencode_DisguisedSixteenBitPNGStillDecodesForReal` confirm the same two fixtures still decode correctly under a generous budget, so the first pair is proven to reject the estimate, not decodability. New fixture helpers `insertStrayFF00BeforeMarker` and `insertPNGChunkBeforeIHDR` (`helpers_test.go`) build both disguises. `TestJPEGIsProgressive` (`reencode_internal_test.go`) gained the "FF 00" case directly and had its "segment declaring a length under two" and "no SOF marker anywhere" cases flipped from `false` to `true`, matching the now-conservative answer; `TestPNGBitDepth_TooShortToHoldTheByteDefaultsToEight` was renamed and flipped the same way, and a new `TestPNGBitDepth_RequiresIHDRAsTheFirstChunk` pins the chunk-type check directly. Every new or changed assertion was confirmed failing against the pre-fix code first.

## A hostile "decrypt-fail" stanza attribute could write an arbitrarily long line to stderr

A security review found `logUndecryptable` (`connector/whatsapp/diagnostics.go`) logged its own `mode events.DecryptFailMode` argument verbatim with `%s`. whatsmeow reads that value straight off the message stanza's own `decrypt-fail` attribute (`message.go`) into a plain `string`-based type with only two values it ever means to set itself (`""` and `"hide"`); nothing validates that the attribute actually holds one of those two before it reaches this log line, so whoever controls that attribute could make it arbitrarily long.

**The fix: a fixed category, the same shape `downloadFailureClass` already gives a download error.** New `decryptFailCategory(mode)` maps `events.DecryptFailShow` to `"show"`, `events.DecryptFailHide` to `"hide"`, and anything else to `"other"`; `logUndecryptable` logs its result instead of `mode` itself.

**Tests.** `TestLogUndecryptable_NamesTheKnownModesByCategory` confirms both real values log their own category; `TestLogUndecryptable_NeverLogsAnArbitraryModeVerbatim` feeds a 10,000-byte hostile mode and confirms none of its text reaches the log, only `"mode=other"`. Both confirmed failing (the raw mode logged) against the pre-fix code.

## `imagesafe.Profile.MaxTotalPixels` was set on two profiles but read nowhere

A security review found `MaxTotalPixels` - meant, per its own doc comment, to cap "the canvas's pixel count multiplied by its frame count" - set on `Thumbnail` and `FullImage` but never once read by `Reencode`, `reencodeStill` or anything else in the package. The package's own doc comment already explains why no such check exists any more: a GIF is refused outright by its magic header before any decode, and an animated WebP has no Go decoder at all and is refused the same way, so every image this package actually decodes today is a single frame. The field was left over from before that redesign, with no "frame count" value anywhere in the current code for it to multiply against.

**The fix: removed.** The field, both struct literals' own values for it, and `FullImage`'s own doc comment sentence that referred to it ("Its total allows a single frame of that size and no more") are gone. Nothing else in the repository, including every test, ever referenced it.

**Verification.** The full `imagesafe` suite, and every package that depends on it, still pass unchanged: nothing was reading the field, so nothing could observe its removal.

## The per-account media database stored a vote's and a message's own sender id with no cap

A security review found two places `connector/whatsapp` writes a sender-controlled id into its own per-account `-media.db`, beside the caps `putPoll` and `setVotes` already give an oversized poll option id: `setVotes`' own `voterID` parameter, read from a live vote's `reactorKey` (a JID with no length limit of its own), goes straight into `poll_votes.voter_id`; `putMessageKey`'s own `key.senderID`, read the same way by `senderKeyID`, goes straight into `message_keys.sender_id`. Neither call site capped it first.

**The fix: `domain.CapRemoteID` on both, dropped rather than truncated.** Both `setVotes` and `putMessageKey` now pass their own sender id through `domain.CapRemoteID` before using it, clearing one past `MaxRemoteIDLength` to `""` instead of truncating it - the same treatment the function already gives a reply quote's own `RemoteID`, and for the same reason: a cut id could read as a different, real, shorter sender it was never actually sent by. `""` already means "sender unknown" for `messageKey.senderID`, so this never collides with a real identity.

**Tests.** `TestSetVotes_DropsAnOversizedVoterID` (`pollstore_test.go`) and `TestMessageKey_PutDropsAnOversizedSenderID` (`keys_test.go`) each save a vote or a key under an id past `MaxRemoteIDLength` and confirm it is never read back verbatim. Both confirmed failing against the pre-fix code.

## `domain.CapDisplayName` capped a display name's length but stripped no control or bidi character

A security review found `CapDisplayName` - the one cap every sender-controlled display name goes through, on write and on every read (see `store.scanMessage` and `store.scanConversationWith`) - cut a name to `MaxDisplayNameLength` but removed nothing else: a self-chosen name could still carry a newline or tab to break a single-line layout, or a Unicode bidi override or invisible format character to make what is shown read differently from what is actually stored, the classic trick for disguising a file's own dangerous extension. `domain.CapFileName`, the matching cap for a sender-claimed file name shown in the UI, had the same gap.

**The fix: strip first, then cut.** A new, shared `isControlOrFormatRune` (`limits.go`) flags an ASCII control character (`U+0000`-`U+001F`, `U+007F`) or one of the Unicode bidi/format characters a self-chosen name could use this way (`U+200B`-`U+200F`, `U+202A`-`U+202E`, `U+2060`-`U+2064`, `U+2066`-`U+2069`, `U+FEFF`) - except `U+200D`, the zero-width joiner, which a real emoji sequence (a family, a profession) is several code points joined by, and which stripping would break apart. `stripControlAndFormatRunes` removes every flagged rune; `CapDisplayName` and `CapFileName` both run it before their own existing length cut, so a legacy row already stored with one of these characters is repaired on its next read the same way an oversized name already is.

**Tests.** `TestCapDisplayName_StripsControlCharacters`, `_StripsBidiAndFormatCharacters`, `_KeepsZeroWidthJoiner` and `TestCapFileName_StripsBidiAndFormatCharacters` (`domain`, `limits_test.go`), each confirmed failing against the pre-fix functions first.

## A WhatsApp sender's self-chosen push name, and a Telegram profile name with no contact behind it, were shown exactly like a saved contact's

A security review found a WhatsApp sender's own push name - entirely self-chosen, with no verification of any kind - filled in `Message.SenderName` whenever no saved contact resolved one (`connector/whatsapp/normalize_message.go`'s `senderDisplayName`/`senderName`), and the same push name, through `handlePushNameUpdate` and `contactDisplayName`'s own last-resort fallback, could become a direct chat's `Conversation.Title` (`contacts.go`, `normalize.go`) or a reply quote's `SenderName` (`store.resolveReply`, copying whatever the quoted message stored) - all shown by the UI exactly as it shows a contact the user actually saved. A stranger could set their own push name to a saved contact's name, such as `"Mum"`, or to `"You"`, the exact label `normalize_message.go`'s own outgoing-message branch and `live.go`'s own self-chat handling use for the user's real, own messages. The official WhatsApp app marks a self-chosen name with a leading `"~"` and shows the phone number instead for a direct chat with no contact; this helper did neither. The same gap exists on Telegram: a profile's first/last name or `@username` is just as self-chosen, unless `tg.User.Contact` or Telegram's own `Verified` badge says otherwise - neither of which this connector read at all.

**The fix: a verified flag, carried alongside every name, computed from each service's own existing trust signal.** `domain.Message.SenderVerified`, `domain.Reply.SenderVerified` and `domain.Conversation.TitleVerified` (new, optional protocol fields - see `docs/api.md`) are `true` for a saved contact's name, a verified business name (WhatsApp) or Telegram's own verified badge or `Contact` flag, a group's own name (there is no self-chosen personal identity to mark for a group's own topic), a fixed label such as the self-chat's or an outgoing message's `"You"`, or this connector's own neutral fallback (a phone number, a generic placeholder) - never for a bare push name or profile name with none of those behind it. WhatsApp already ranked a resolved name's trust for a different reason (`nameRank`, so a later, weaker report can never replace a better name already cached); `verifiedRank` exposes that same ranking as the boolean the protocol needed, so no trust judgement changed, only who gets to see it. `contactDisplayName`'s own push-name fallback, previously folded into the same trust level as a saved name once cached by `device.contactName`, is now ranked `nameRankPushName` like any other push name, closing that gap too. Telegram's own per-connector name cache gained the same rank (as `namedEntry{name, verified}`, replacing a bare string). `connector.SenderNamer.SenderName` and `store.RefreshSenderName` both gained a `verified` parameter, so a name correction that arrives later - a contact resolving after a message was already stored under its push name - corrects the stored verified flag along with the name, on every affected message and the reply quotes that name them, not just the text. `store`'s `messages` and `conversations` tables each gained one column (`sender_verified`, `title_verified`, both `DEFAULT 1`: a row stored before this existed keeps the trust it already showed, rather than a retroactive downgrade with no new information behind it); a reply quote's own flag lives inside its existing `reply_to` JSON, needing no column of its own. `app.Commands.Send`'s own outgoing message and reply-quote resolution both set `SenderVerified: true` for the user's own identity. `policy.Input` gained `SenderVerified`, and `policy.Notification` marks an unverified sender's own name with `"~"` in a desktop notification's title the same way the UI does, without ever marking a group's own title. `domain.Conversation` also gained `Phone` - the official app's other half of this: a direct chat's other person's own phone number, shown beside an unverified title instead of trusting it alone. WhatsApp's `phoneNumber` reuses `titleFallback`'s own phone-JID formatting (through `phoneFormOf`, so a LID already mapped to a phone JID still resolves); Telegram's `sharedPhone` reads `tg.User.Phone`, set only when that person has chosen to share it, which most never do. `conversations` gained a `phone` column alongside the two above.

**Tests.** `store`: `TestAddMessage_RoundTripsSenderVerified`, `TestAddMessage_FillsReplyVerifiedFromTheQuotedMessage`, `TestEnsureConversation_RoundTripsTitleVerified`, `TestEnsureConversation_RoundTripsPhone`, `TestRefreshSenderName_UpdatesSenderVerifiedOnEveryMessage`, `TestRefreshSenderName_CorrectsAReplyQuotesVerifiedFlagToo`, `TestMigrate_DefaultsSenderAndTitleVerifiedToTrueForExistingRows`. `connector/whatsapp`: `TestContactDisplayName_PrefersSavedNamesOverAPushName`, `TestContactName_IsUnverifiedForABarePushName`, `TestSenderName_FallsBackSensibly`, `TestHandleContactUpdate_CorrectsTheSendersNameOnAlreadyStoredMessages` (extended for `verified`), `TestPhoneNumber_FormatsAKnownNumberOrReportsNone`. `connector/telegram`: `TestUserName_MarksAVerifiedNameUnlikeASelfChosenOne`, `TestSharedPhone_FormatsAShareOrReportsNone`. `app/policy`: `TestNotification_MarksASelfChosenSenderName`, `TestNotification_NeverMarksTheReservedOwnMessageLabel`. Every new assertion was confirmed failing - the field missing, or defaulting to the wrong trust level - before the corresponding production change.

**Follow-up: the same gap, found again in six more places.** A later audit, looking for any UI surface still showing a self-chosen name unmarked, found the fix above had not reached everywhere a name is shown. `ui/components/ConversationList.qml`'s own delegate hand-picked which fields of a conversation reached `ConversationRow`, and `titleVerified` was not among them - every row in the real list showed its title unmarked regardless of the helper's actual flag, since the field never arrived at all; the first fix's own QML tests missed this because they built `ConversationRow` directly, never through the real list. Five more surfaces carried a name with no `verified` of its own alongside it at all: the @-mention picker's member list and the composer's reply banner (`domain.Member` gained `Verified`; WhatsApp's `memberName` and Telegram's `memberList` now return it the same way `senderDisplayName`/`userName` already do); the command palette's conversation and matched-message rows (`domain.Conversation` gained `MatchSenderVerified`, read back out of `store`'s own FTS5 join); the new-chat contact list (`domain.Contact` gained `Verified`; WhatsApp's `syncPushnames` now prefers `device.contactName`'s own resolved contact over the raw, always-unverified push name it used unconditionally before, and Telegram's `contact` is always verified, since its only caller reads `contacts.getContacts`, which names nothing but a real saved contact); and a group's typing indicator (`connector.Sink.Typing` gained a `TypingState{Verified, Active}` - bundled to stay within this codebase's argument-count limit - carrying the same cached rank `nameFor` already keys every other name from). Read receipts, the notification body and reaction/poll-voter lists were checked and confirmed to show no name at all, so there was nothing to mark there.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/ConversationList`'s `checkUnverifiedTitleIsMarked` drives the real list end to end rather than `ConversationRow` directly, the regression test for the dropped-field bug. `tests/qml/ConversationView`'s `checkImpersonationIsVisiblyDifferentFromTheRealContact` confirms a self-chosen title reading identically to a real, verified contact's own name ("Mum" impersonating "Mum") still renders marked with its own phone number, proving the marking depends only on the flag, never on comparing text against a known name. `connector/whatsapp`: `TestMembers_MarksABarePushNameUnverified`, `TestContactFromPushName_PrefersAKnownContactOverTheBarePushName`, `TestHandleChatPresence_NamesTheTyperOnlyInAGroup` (extended for `LastTypingVerified`). `connector/telegram`: a bare-profile-name case added to `TestMembers_NamesEachParticipant`, `TestContact_IsAlwaysVerified`, `TestTyping_ReportsStartAndStopInKnownChats` (extended likewise). `store`: `TestConversations_SearchReportsWhetherTheMatchedSenderIsVerified`. `app`: `TestTyping_PublishesForKnownConversations` (moved to its own `ingest_typing_test.go`, split out of `ingest_test.go` to keep that file within this codebase's file-length limit). `tests/unit/palette.test.cjs` and `tests/qml/Composer`, `tests/qml/NewChatContactRow`, `tests/qml/ConversationController` each gained a marked/unmarked pair for their own surface. Every one of these was confirmed failing first.

## Every sandboxed job capped only its own decoder at two threads, leaving the encoder and its filters free to use every core

A security review found `commonInputArgs`'s own `-threads 2` (`transcode/args.go`) sits before `-i`, which ffmpeg only ever reads as an input-side option: it bounds the decoder reading a message's own video, never the encoder or filter graph a profile maps its output through. Video's and Animation's own chained `scale` filter and `libx264` encode (`video.go`, `animation.go`) were both left to size their own thread pools by this machine's core count. Confirmed directly against a real encode on a 16-core machine: an otherwise-unmodified Video job peaked at 99 OS threads, almost all of them the filter graph's own thread pool (`vf#0:0`, measured at 74) and the audio filter's own (`af#0:1`, measured at 16) - not `libx264` itself, whose own encode thread showed just once at that sample, since x264's own internal worker pool gets no distinct name under ffmpeg's own thread-naming scheme. A job already keeps running until `sandbox`'s own 900 CPU-second limit or either profile's own five-minute timeout, so an uncapped one competes with the shell this plugin runs inside for every core it can reach the whole time it runs, not just briefly.

**The fix: cap the filter graph and the encoder too, and run every job at the lowest scheduling priority besides.** `args.go` gained `mediaThreads` (2, the same figure `commonInputArgs`'s own decoder cap already used) and three helpers built from it: `filterThreadArgs` (`-filter_threads`, for a profile's own `-vf`/`-af` graph), `outputThreadArgs` (a second `-threads`, on the output side, for whichever encoder a profile maps its output through), and `libx264ThreadArgs` (`-x264-params threads=2`, fixing libx264's own internal thread count directly rather than trusting ffmpeg's generic output-side `-threads` to reach it through `AVCodecContext.thread_count`). `videoArgs`/`animationArgs` now give ffmpeg all three. Confirmed directly against the same real encode used to measure the regression above: peak OS threads for the same job fell from 99 to 18, stable across three repeated runs each way. Separately, `sandbox.buildArgv` now wraps every job in `nice -n 19` and `ionice -c 3` (new `niceLevel`/`ioClass` in `argv.go`) as the outermost layer of the command it execs, ahead of `env`, `prlimit` and `bwrap`: a process's own niceness and I/O scheduling class are both inherited across every `fork` and `exec` in a chain (POSIX), confirmed directly all the way down to the actual sandboxed process bwrap's own internal fork finally execs - reading niceness and I/O class back from the host's own `/proc` for a plain sandboxed `sleep` showed `19` and `idle`, not just on `bwrap` itself partway down the chain. `Runner.resolveTools` now also resolves `nice` and `ionice`, in the same outermost-to-innermost order `buildArgv` chains them, alongside the three tools it already required.

**Tests.** `TestBuildArgv_FullSpec` (`sandbox`, `argv_test.go`) was updated for the new five-argument `buildArgv` signature and the `nice`/`ionice` prefix now expected ahead of `env`; `TestResolveTools_PartialFailure` gained `"nice missing"` and `"ionice missing"` cases alongside the three it already had. `TestVideo_Args` and `TestAnimation_Args` (`transcode`) were updated for the three new flags each profile now gives ffmpeg. The thread-count and priority measurements above were a one-off, direct verification against the real ffmpeg and the real `nice`/`ionice`/`bwrap` chain, not a new automated test: counting `/proc/<pid>/task` while an uncapped encode actually ran, then again after the fix, and reading a sandboxed process's own niceness and I/O class back from `/proc` and `ionice -p`. Neither this package's own existing real-pipeline tests (`sandbox_test.go`) nor `transcode`'s own real-encode tests assert on either directly, though both continue to pass unchanged, confirming the fix does not break the pipeline itself.

## A failed play copy was only ever logged, never remembered, so reopening a conversation transcoded it again every time

A security review found `playCopy` (`app/play.go`) logs a failed transcode through `logPlayFailure` but does nothing else with it: the next play fetch for the very same message - which reopening its conversation triggers for every message in it, the same way `FetchMedia`'s own `play` flag already does on each individual fetch - runs `transcodedPlayCopy` again from scratch, paying up to the full job budget (Video's and Animation's own five-minute timeout, or Audio's) a second time, and every time after that. `media.go`'s own `refusedDisplayCopies` already solves exactly this shape of problem for a photo or sticker whose display copy fails its safety check, for the identical reason: the UI asks again on its own, so a failure that is not remembered is a failure that is paid for again and again.

**The fix: a bounded set of failed play-copy ids, the same shape `refusedDisplayCopies` already uses.** New `refusedPlayCopies` (`playrefused.go`) remembers up to `refusedPlayCopiesLimit` (500, the same figure `refusedDisplayCopies` uses) ids, oldest evicted first, cleared on restart like its sibling. `playCopy` now checks it first, through a new `playCopyID(m)` helper (`cacheIdentity(m) + "-play"`, the exact id `transcodedPlayCopy` already built inline - both now share the one helper) - and returns `""` at once for an id already remembered, before ever reaching the transcoder - and remembers the id itself, alongside the existing log call, the one time `transcodedPlayCopy` actually fails. `Commands` gained a `refusedPlay *refusedPlayCopies` field, constructed in `New` alongside `refused`.

**Tests.** `TestFetchMedia_FailsFastOnASecondPlayFetchAfterAFailedTranscode` (`play_test.go`) fetches the same video message twice with a failing fake transcoder and confirms the transcoder is called exactly once, not twice; confirmed failing (two calls) against the pre-fix code. `TestRefusedPlayCopies_RemembersAndLooksUp`, `_EvictsTheOldestPastItsLimit` and `_RememberingAgainDoesNotDuplicateTheEntry` (`playrefused_test.go`, an internal test for the same reason `mediarefused_test.go` is: proving eviction through 501 real play fetches would be slow and only indirectly about the thing being proven) cover the type directly, mirroring `mediarefused_test.go`'s own three cases for `refusedDisplayCopies`.

## A crafted sample table could make the rebuilt MP4 grow far past its own size cap, and a notification sound that failed to transcode was retried on every message

A security review found `remux.go`'s own `remuxCaps` bounds each individual sample's size (`maxVideoSampleLen`, `maxAudioSampleLen`) and how many samples a track may declare (`maxVideoSamples`, `maxAudioSamples`), but never the rebuilt file's own combined total. A crafted sample table can declare many thousands of samples, each within its own per-sample cap, that all alias the same handful of bytes in the sandboxed file's own, already-capped mdat; `collectSamples` would write that same small payload out again once per declared sample, so a compromised ffmpeg could make the helper write on the order of `maxVideoSamples` x `maxVideoSampleLen` of scratch disk - roughly 70 GiB for Video's own caps - not bytes anywhere near what the sandboxed file it rebuilds from actually held. `remuxCaps`'s own doc comment made this worse by claiming the opposite: that `checkOutput`'s cap on the sandboxed file as a whole already covered this, which it never did, since that check runs before `remuxMP4` ever reads a single sample.

A second, unrelated gap shares this entry for being just as cheap to fix: `notificationsound.go`'s `storeAndTranscodeSound` recorded a source file's identity in `transcodedSoundCache` only on a successful transcode, so a file that fails - corrupt, not actually audio - was forgotten the moment the call returned. `resolveServiceSound` runs on every incoming notification, so it re-ran the sandboxed transcoder for the exact same failing file every single time, rather than only its first attempt - the identical gap the entry above fixes for a failed play copy, for the identical reason: a failure that is not remembered is a failure that is paid for again and again.

**The fix: a running total alongside the per-sample caps, and a failed attempt remembered the same as a successful one.** `remuxCaps` gained `maxOutputBytes`, set from each profile's own existing output cap (`videoMaxOutputBytes`, reused by `Animation` too, the same way its other remux caps already are). `collectSamples` now gives `collectVideoSamples` and `collectAudioSamples` a shared `scratchWriter` (new, `remuxsamples.go`) instead of a bare `*os.File`; its `write` method refuses - before anything reaches disk - once video's and audio's own combined running total would exceed `maxOutputBytes`. `remuxCaps`'s own doc comment was corrected to say what actually bounds what: the per-sample and per-track caps bound validation cost, and `maxOutputBytes` is this package's real defence against the rebuilt file's own size. Separately, `transcodedSoundIdentity` gained a `failed` field, and `storeAndTranscodeSound` now calls `transcodedSoundCache.record` on every attempt, not only a successful one, failing fast with `errSoundNotPlayable` for an unchanged source already known to fail rather than reaching the transcoder again.

**Tests.** `TestRemuxMP4_SampleRefusals`'s new `"rebuilt output would exceed its total size cap"` case (`remuxsamples_test.go`) sets a 10-byte cap against three 8-byte rebuilt samples, confirming the second one is refused even though each sits far under `maxVideoSampleLen` on its own; `TestScratchWriter_RefusesOnceTheRunningTotalWouldExceedMax` covers `scratchWriter.write` directly, including that a refused write never reaches disk at all. `TestResolveNotificationSound_ServiceDoesNotRetryAnUnchangedFileThatAlreadyFailed` (`notificationsound_test.go`) resolves the same failing service sound twice and confirms the transcoder is called once, not twice. All three were confirmed failing against the pre-fix code first.

## `domain.AllowedLinkURL` checked only a URL's scheme, so a backslash or an invisible character could make it open somewhere other than this helper's own idea of its host

A security review found a browser's own WHATWG URL parser ends an http(s) authority at a backslash the same as it does a forward slash, while `AllowedLinkURL` (`backend/internal/domain/formatting.go`) checked only the scheme before a connector or `app/openexternal.go` ever reported or opened a URL, and `ui/lib/Links.js`'s `urlHost` split an authority only at `/`, `?` or `#`. `https://evil.example\@paypal.com/login` passed both unchanged: `LinkOpenConfirm` read `urlHost`'s own result and said "Really opens: paypal.com", while a browser - and the desktop portal `app/openexternal.go` hands the raw string to - would actually open `evil.example`. Three further variants (`https:\\evil.example`, `https:/\evil.example`, `https:evil.example`) carry no real `"//"` authority of their own at all, or only a backslash standing in for part of it, which `urlHost` read as having no host to show (hiding the emphasis line entirely) while a browser still resolved one regardless. A separate class - a URL carrying a zero-width space, a bidi override or isolate, or the byte-order mark, none of them rendering as anything visible - could glue a short, honest-looking link to a trailing domain that was never really part of it.

**The fix: one strict rule, enforced in the helper and mirrored in the UI.** `AllowedLinkURL` now refuses a URL outright - never merely degrading its displayed host - when it carries a backslash, real whitespace, an ASCII control character, or one of the Unicode "format" characters (category Cf: zero-width spaces and marks U+200B-U+200F, bidi embeddings and overrides U+202A-U+202E, word joiner and invisible operators U+2060-U+2064, bidi isolates U+2066-U+2069, or the byte-order mark U+FEFF), or when its scheme is http(s) but it does not spell out a real `"scheme://authority"` (a non-empty run of characters right after `"//"`, not itself `/`, `?` or `#`). `mailto:` and `tg:` need no authority of their own, so neither is held to that last rule. `ui/lib/Format.js` gained the identical rule as `spanURL`'s own two new checks (`HIDDEN_OR_UNUSUAL_CHAR`, `hasHTTPAuthority`), which `Links.isAllowed` already reused with no code change of its own needed, so a link can never be shown as safe on one side of the protocol and refused on the other. `Links.urlHost` keeps its own authority-split logic unchanged: its doc comment now says plainly that it only agrees with the browser that will actually open a URL once `isAllowed` has already accepted it, which refusing every backslash now guarantees. `LinkOpenConfirm.qml` checks `Links.isAllowed(target)` itself before ever computing or showing a host: a refused link shows a dedicated state instead - `Links.REFUSED_LINK_MESSAGE` ("This link has hidden or unusual characters and was not opened") as its description, no emphasis line, and only a Close choice, never Open or Copy - since the real open-time guarantee (this URL is never actually opened) comes from `isAllowed`/`AllowedLinkURL` regardless of what any dialog shows or which key is pressed.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/domain/formatting_test.go` gained table-driven cases for every backslash variant above, the no-real-authority variants, each hidden-character category (built with `string(rune(...))`, never a literal invisible character in the source), and confirmation that ordinary authorities and `mailto:`/`tg:` links are unaffected - all confirmed failing first against the pre-fix scheme-only check. `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` and `tests/unit/links.test.cjs` gained the matching node cases for `spanURL` and `isAllowed` (built with `String.fromCharCode`, for the same reason), also confirmed failing first. `tests/qml/LinkOpenConfirm` gained a case driving the review's own backslash URL through the real dialog, confirmed to fail first against the pre-fix component (`"Really opens: paypal.com"`), then to show the refusal state with no Open or Copy button once fixed.

## `URL_PATTERN` ended an autolink at the one invisible character JS's own `\s` happens to match, gluing the rest onto it with no visible seam

A follow-up to the entry above: a security review found `Format.js`'s `URL_PATTERN` stops a plain autolink's own match at anything JS's `\s` matches, which - unlike every other invisible Unicode "format" character `HIDDEN_OR_UNUSUAL_CHAR` now lists - includes the byte-order mark (U+FEFF). `"https://paypal-login.ru"` + that mark + `".paypal.com/login"` read, on screen, as one unbroken, paypal.com-looking run (the mark renders as nothing at all), but only the short prefix before it was actually linked - and since a plain autolink's own `displayText` is always its own `url` by construction, `needsConfirmation` had nothing to catch: it opened straight to `paypal-login.ru` with no question asked at all, worse than the backslash case above, which at least still showed a (possibly wrong) confirmation.

**The fix: spell out the terminator class instead of writing `\s`, leaving out only the byte-order mark.** `URL_PATTERN` now excludes every character JS's own `\s` matches except U+FEFF, listed explicitly rather than written as `\s`. Every other hidden character `HIDDEN_OR_UNUSUAL_CHAR` lists (zero-width spaces and marks, bidi overrides and isolates, word joiner) was never part of `\s` to begin with, so this one exclusion is enough to sweep all of them into the match too, not just the byte-order mark specifically. The swept-in URL then fails `spanURL`'s own hidden-character rule (the entry above) and is refused outright when `isAllowed` runs, rather than silently autolinking the shorter, innocent-looking prefix the old pattern stopped at. No change to `linkify`, `lineHtml`, or any of `messageHtml`'s other per-segment callers was needed: all of them already run text through this one pattern.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` gained a case for the byte-order mark (the review's own example, confirmed failing first - the old pattern linked only the short `paypal-login.ru` prefix) and one each for a zero-width space, a right-to-left mark, a left-to-right override, a left-to-right isolate and a word joiner, confirmed to already pass even before this fix (none of those is part of `\s`, so the old pattern already swept them into the match) - kept as regression cases so a future change to the terminator class cannot silently narrow it back down. A plain case confirms real, visible whitespace still ends a match as before.

## A link preview's "already shown" check still matched an anchor's own href, not what that anchor actually shows as text

A follow-up review of "A link preview's own 'already shown' check was a plain substring match..." (above) found that entry's own fix - comparing a preview's URL against the message's rendered anchors - still compared it against each anchor's `url` (its href, the real destination a click actually goes to), not its `displayText` (what the anchor actually shows on screen). A formatting span whose visible text reads as something else entirely - `"Click here"`, say, linking to `https://evil.example/login` - still made a link preview card sharing that exact `https://evil.example/login` href count as "shown", even though the user never saw that URL as text anywhere in the message; the card's own title (`"My Bank"`, the sender's to choose freely) then opened with no question asked, on the strength of a match the user could never have actually seen.

**The fix: compare against `displayText`, not `url`.** `previewDisplayText` (`ui/lib/Links.js`) now checks `anchors.some((a) => a.displayText === url)` in place of the old `a.url === url`. "Shown" now means the preview's own URL was itself the exact visible text of some anchor in the bubble - a plain autolink (`displayText` always equals its own `url`, see the raw-URL entry below) or a span whose own covered text happens to spell out the URL literally - never merely that some other link in the message, with its own different visible text, happens to share that same href.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/links.test.cjs`'s existing span-based `previewDisplayText` case was rewritten: it used to assert a span whose own href matched the preview's URL counted as shown even though its visible text ("link") was something else entirely - exactly the gap this fix closes - so it now asserts the opposite, confirmed to fail first against the pre-fix code (asserting the old, wrong expectation). A new case reproduces the review's own `"Click here"` / `"My Bank"` shape directly, confirmed to fail first (the old code returned the href match as "shown"). A further case confirms the check still finds a URL shown as text behind one anchor even when a second, differently-targeted "disguise" anchor sharing no text with it sits earlier in the same message, so the fix checks every anchor's own `displayText`, not just the first one found.

## A plain autolinked URL was recorded HTML-escaped, not as the sender actually sent it

A security review found `Format.js`'s `linkify` recording a matched URL's own escaped text verbatim into `anchors` - `URL_PATTERN` runs over text `escapeHtml` already turned a literal `&` into `&amp;` in, and `linkify` stored that same escaped string as the anchor's `url`, never decoding it back. A link carrying a literal `&` in its query string - `https://example.com?a=1&b=2` - was recorded, and later opened, as `https://example.com?a=1&amp;b=2`: a different, functionally wrong URL the sender never actually sent. It also broke `previewDisplayText`'s own exact-match check (the two entries above): a WhatsApp link preview's own URL, reported by the connector in its real, unescaped form, could never equal an autolink's escaped one even when the two were really the same link, so a legitimate preview needlessly asked to confirm.

**The fix: decode the matched text back to raw before recording it; keep the escaped form only for what actually renders on screen.** `Format.js` gained `unescapeHtml`, `escapeHtml`'s own inverse - decoding `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;` and `&quot;`, plus a decimal or hexadecimal numeric character reference (the same entities `URL_PATTERN`'s own trailing alternation already tolerates inside a match) - back to the character or code point each came from. `linkify` now calls it on the matched, trailing-punctuation-trimmed text before pushing `{url, displayText}` into `anchors`, while the `<a>` tag's own visible text keeps the original escaped form, so `&` still renders correctly on screen as the single character it is. Nothing else changed: a link span's own URL (`styledRanges`) was never escaped in the first place, since it comes from a connector's own structured field rather than from matching against already-escaped message text, so this gap was specific to the plain-autolink path.

**Tests.** `tests/unit/format.test.cjs`'s existing `&amp;`-entity `linkify` case had its expected `url` changed from the escaped form to the raw one, confirmed to fail first against the pre-fix code (which recorded the escaped string verbatim) - the rendered HTML itself is unchanged, still escaped. New `unescapeHtml` cases cover all four named entities, both numeric forms, plain text with none at all, a lone `&` with no matching entity after it, and more than one entity in the same string. A further `linkify` case checks a URL carrying both a decimal and a hexadecimal numeric entity decodes correctly. `tests/unit/links.test.cjs` gained a `previewDisplayText` case with a literal `&` in both the message text and the preview's own URL, confirming the two now compare equal.

## `ClipboardPaste.pasteInto`'s own guard let it insert HTML into a read-only message body

A security review found `pasteInto`'s only guard - `typeof field.insert !== "function"` - true of `MessageBubbleContent`'s own message body too: a `TextEdit` with `readOnly: true`, `selectByMouse: true` (so a click can give it real focus, for text selection) and `textFormat: TextEdit.RichText`. `QQuickTextEdit.insert()` does not check `readOnly` at all, and, given `RichText`, inserts its argument as HTML rather than plain text. Ctrl+V or Shift+Insert, pressed while that field held focus, reached `Panel.qml`'s global `field.paste` binding the same as any other field's would, and `pasteInto` pasted whatever the clipboard held - an `<img src=...>` tag, say - straight into a bubble no sender ever controlled, making the shell fetch and decode whatever it pointed at.

**The fix: refuse a read-only or non-plain-text target outright, before `clipboard.readText` is even asked for.** `pasteInto` now returns early when `field.readOnly === true`, or when `field.textFormat` exists and is not `TextEdit.PlainText`. The `textFormat` check reads `!== undefined` first since `TextField` - a perfectly normal paste target - has no such property at all, and `undefined` must never compare as "not plain text" the way an actual rich-text format value does. An audit of every other place this UI inserts or sets text programmatically (`MessengerLayout`'s `onNewlineRequested`/`onTextInsertRequested`, both always targeting the composer's own fixed, plain-text, non-read-only `TextArea`) found no other gap of the same shape: `ClipboardPaste` is the one shared path every field outside the composer already pastes through, by design (see its own header comment), so one fix here closes it everywhere that matters.

**Tests.** `tests/qml/FieldPaste` gained a step driving real Ctrl+V into a read-only, `RichText` `TextEdit` - the same shape `MessageBubbleContent`'s own message body takes - confirmed to fail first (`fakeService.lastMethod` became `"clipboard.readText"` and the field's own HTML changed) against the pre-fix `pasteInto`. The test's own window gained an ancestor `Keys.onPressed` mirroring `Panel.qml`'s `keyArea`, needed because every other field in this test already intercepts Ctrl+V itself ahead of that level; a read-only field, like the real message body, does not, so only an ancestor handler reaches `pasteInto` for it at all - the exact path a real paste into an open conversation's message body takes. The "text unchanged" assertion compares against the field's own text read back once, right after it loads, rather than the literal string this test sets it to, since Qt re-serialises `RichText` into a full HTML document on assignment regardless of any paste.

## A fourth sandbox review found System V and POSIX IPC could bypass RLIMIT_AS the same way memfd did

A differential security review of the range ending at 872dc2c found one more route past the resource limits `sandbox/limits.go` documents as a job's own total, the same shape of gap the third sandbox review above closed for `memfd_create`/`memfd_secret`.

**System V shared memory, message queues and semaphores, and POSIX message queues are a fourth route to the same `RLIMIT_AS` bypass memfd already closes.** The third review above considered `shmget`/`shmat` and left them allowed, reasoning they were "bounded differently - by system-wide shmmax/shmall sysctls". That reasoning does not hold inside this sandbox: `--unshare-all` gives every job a fresh IPC namespace, which starts at the kernel's own compiled-in defaults, not whatever this helper's own host happens to have tuned those sysctls to - confirmed directly, `/proc/sys/kernel/shmall` on this machine reads 18446744073692774399, effectively unlimited. A hijacked ffmpeg looping `shmget`/`shmat`/fill/`shmdt` holds pages charged to the kernel's own shared-memory accounting, never to the job's own address space, until its IPC namespace is torn down - up to `videoTimeout`'s own 5 minutes - during which the OOM killer, unable to free memory that belongs to no process, starts killing real processes instead, such as omarchy-shell itself. Message queues and semaphore sets share the same per-namespace-default, no-`RLIMIT_AS`-coverage shape, and POSIX message queues are a fourth, independent API to the same kind of kernel object.

**The fix: refuse all of them outright, in their own seccomp group, the same way `memfdSyscalls` already is.** `ipcSyscalls` (new, `seccomp.go`) names `shmget`, `shmat`, `shmdt`, `shmctl`, `msgget`, `msgsnd`, `msgrcv`, `msgctl`, `semget`, `semop`, `semtimedop`, `semctl`, `mq_open`, `mq_unlink`, `mq_timedsend`, `mq_timedreceive`, `mq_notify` and `mq_getsetattr`, added to the policy as their own `EPERM` group. None of `deniedSyscalls`' own "no legitimate reason to call this" framing applies - the reason is the same `RLIMIT_AS` bypass `memfdSyscalls` exists to close - so, like `memfdSyscalls`, this is left out of `maxJobBytes`'s own sum rather than given a made-up cap.

**Tests.** `TestSeccompInstructions_RefusesIPCSyscallsOnTheNativeArch` (`seccomp_test.go`) runs the real assembled filter through the BPF software VM over every name in `ipcSyscalls`, the same way `TestSeccompInstructions_RefusesMemfdSyscallsOnTheNativeArch` already does for `memfdSyscalls`; confirmed failing (undefined: `ipcSyscalls`) before the group existed. `TestRun_SeccompDeniesSystemVSharedMemory` (`sandbox_test.go`) is the real-bwrap test, confirmed failing first against the pre-fix filter (re-checked by temporarily removing the new seccomp group and re-running): it runs a real `python3 -c` script that calls `shmget` through `ctypes` straight into libc, and asserts the call itself failed, rather than depending on the process dying - without the fix, `shmget` succeeds and the script reports failure (its own check is inverted: it signals success only when the allocation itself did not go through).

**Coverage.** Measured the same way as the second and third sandbox reviews: `bwrap`, `ffmpeg` and `ffprobe` hidden from `PATH` entirely (a symlink farm of `/usr/bin` minus those three, built under `build/` and removed immediately after), so every real-pipeline test in both packages skips rather than runs. `sandbox` held at 92.0%, still above the 90% gate on the unit and fake-runner tests alone. With `bwrap`/`ffmpeg`/`ffprobe` present, the full suite measured `sandbox` at 92.0%.

**Verification.** `coredumpctl list` was checked before this review began and again after every test run above, with `-race` and real `bwrap`/`prlimit`/seccomp paths included: the count never moved. `ipcSyscalls` joins `memfdSyscalls`' own `EPERM` group, so this changes nothing about how a denied syscall is enforced - expected, not merely hoped for.

## A fifth sandbox review found an unbounded thread count could exhaust the whole session's own processes

The same differential review found `clone(CLONE_THREAD)` has no process-count limit of its own, so a hijacked ffmpeg can loop starting threads until the whole session's processes and threads are exhausted - not just this one job's.

**`clone(CLONE_THREAD)` has no process-count limit of its own.** The second sandbox review's own `processCreationSyscalls` already refuse every way a job could fork a second process with a fresh `RLIMIT_AS` and `RLIMIT_CPU` of its own, but a thread shares its parent's address space by definition and is charged against the same CPU total, so that fix still has to let `clone(CLONE_THREAD)` through - every profile's own `-threads 2` needs it. A thread's own stack, though, is kernel memory the kernel allocates on demand, not something `maxAddressSpaceBytes` sees until it is actually touched: a loop of threads that only block, never writing to most of their own stack, can hold the user's entire process/thread quota - and the whole session's, since `RLIMIT_NPROC` is a real-UID limit, not a per-job one - well before `maxCPUSeconds` or `maxAddressSpaceBytes` notice anything is wrong. Every later process or thread this helper's own session tries to start then fails too, including omarchy-shell's own Qt threads, its own `Process` launches and Hyprland's exec, until the job's own timeout ends it.

**The fix: `prlimit --nproc`, run a second time, inside the sandbox.** The outer `prlimit` `buildArgv` already runs sets `RLIMIT_AS`, `RLIMIT_CPU`, `RLIMIT_FSIZE` and `RLIMIT_NOFILE` - but it runs before bwrap's own `--unshare-user` creates the job's fresh user namespace, and `RLIMIT_NPROC` is counted per real UID *within whichever user namespace set it* (confirmed directly: setting `--nproc` on the outer `prlimit` call, before the new namespace exists, does nothing to bound a thread bomb once it runs). `innerPrlimitArgs` (new, `argv.go`) is a second, inner `prlimit --nproc=N` that `buildArgv` places as the very last thing before `spec.Binary` in bwrap's own argv - so it runs *inside* the sandbox, after `--unshare-user` has already taken effect, and is what actually bounds `RLIMIT_NPROC` for the job's own namespace rather than the helper's. It reuses `prlimitPath`, the same absolute path the outer call already resolved: `/usr` is bound read-only into the sandbox at that same path, so it resolves identically on either side of the bwrap boundary, with no second tool to look up.

`maxProcessCount` (new, `limits.go`) is 40: measured directly against every real profile this package runs, with `bwrap`, `ffmpeg` and the inner `prlimit` all real, by bisecting the lowest `--nproc` value each profile still succeeds at. Video - the heaviest, libx264 encoding a real portrait clip with audio - needed 18; Animation (silent, otherwise identical video settings) needed 12; Audio needed 7. A fourth profile, StillFrame, first needed 53 - far more than its own simple "decode one frame, encode one PNG" work should ever cost - which traced to a real, separate gap: `stillFrameArgs` was the one profile in this package that never called `filterThreadArgs`/`outputThreadArgs` at all, so its own scale filter and png encoder both defaulted to this machine's own core count rather than `mediaThreads`, exactly the unbounded-thread-pool shape `args.go`'s own `mediaThreads` doc comment says every profile's filter and encoder should avoid. Fixing that oversight (see below) brought StillFrame's own floor down to 11, in line with every other profile. 40 is a little over double the resulting worst case (18), generous for a slower machine or a slightly different libx264 build while still refusing a loop that tries to hold hundreds.

**The StillFrame fix, found while measuring `maxProcessCount`.** `stillFrameArgs` (`transcode/stillframe.go`) now calls `filterThreadArgs()` right after `commonInputArgs()` and `outputThreadArgs()` right after `-c:v png`, the same positions Video and Animation already use for their own filter and encoder thread caps. This is a production fix in its own right, not just a test-measurement convenience: an unbounded filter or encoder thread pool is exactly the shape of resource use `mediaThreads` exists to bound everywhere else in this package, and StillFrame's own gap meant a received animated WebP sticker - processed automatically, with no click - could spin up as many threads as this machine has cores, every time.

**Tests.** `TestInnerPrlimitArgs` (`argv_test.go`) pins `innerPrlimitArgs`'s own exact output; `TestBuildArgv_FullSpec`'s own full literal comparison was updated to include the inner `prlimit --nproc=40` segment. `TestStillFrame_Args` (`transcode/stillframe_test.go`) was updated for the two new thread-cap flags.

`TestRun_PrlimitInsideSandboxStopsAThreadBomb` (`sandbox_test.go`) is the real-bwrap test, confirmed failing first against the pre-fix argv (re-checked by temporarily dropping the inner `prlimit` call and re-running): it runs a real thread bomb - `threading.Thread(target=block.wait, daemon=True)` in a loop, blocking forever rather than doing any work - and asserts the sandboxed job fails on an ordinary error (`threading`'s own `RuntimeError`, wrapping the kernel's `EAGAIN` from a failed `pthread_create`), never on a timeout. The test sets `threading.stack_size(64 KiB)`, far under glibc's 8 MiB default: confirmed directly that without it, `maxAddressSpaceBytes` (4 GiB) alone already stops a default-stack thread bomb around 500 threads, which would pass this test for the wrong reason and prove nothing about the `--nproc` fix specifically - exactly the shape of thread bomb worth guarding against, since a real attacker has no reason to ask for a larger stack than the exploit needs. `TestStillFrame_RealEncode_AnimatedWebP` and every other real-profile integration test in `transcode` were re-run after both this fix and the StillFrame one together, confirming every real profile still succeeds under the new `--nproc` cap.

**Coverage.** Measured the same way as the fourth sandbox review above: with `bwrap`, `ffmpeg` and `ffprobe` hidden from `PATH`, `sandbox` held at 92.0%, `transcode` at 91.1% - both still above the 90% gate on the unit and fake-runner tests alone. With `bwrap`/`ffmpeg`/`ffprobe` present, the full suite (every real-profile integration test included) measured `sandbox` at 92.0% and `transcode` at 91.3%.

**Verification.** `coredumpctl list` was checked before this review began and again after every test run above, with `-race` and real `bwrap`/`prlimit`/seccomp paths included: the count never moved. A thread that cannot start fails with an ordinary `EAGAIN` from `pthread_create`, never a signal, so this changes nothing about how a resource limit is enforced - expected, not merely hoped for.
